McDOUGALL'S Paterit SELF-RAISING FLOUR OANUFNED. The Examiner Saturday, September 12, 1903. FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

CHAPTER XXIII.

We cannot deny that the fashions of late have displayed a leaning towards the grati- fication of the demands of common sense, the short trim walking skirts forming per- haps the most striking example of this the freshest and newest mood of Madame la Mode. Indeed, the girl of to-day puts ap- pearance and comfort on terms of equality when selecting her gowns, a state of affairs vastly different from what existed in fashion- able society a short time ago. This is par- ticularly noticeable in the travelling English girl of the present time, who, instead of donning half worn-out and soiled finery as

THE TREASURES OF TEMAWR.

AT this point Tredegar noticed that the footprints which had hitherto kept close to the wall of the cavern, now branched off at right angles and disappeared into the central darkness. He decided to follow them, and they turned their backs on the long tiers cot tombs and made their way through the vast open grave of the less honoured dead:

- Every twenty yards a gigantic column, cut out apparently from the solid rock, rose up from the sand into the darkness above. These columns, which were nearly twelve feet in diameter, varied considerably in design. Sortie were roughly. hewn, and not even chiselled down to a circular forM ; others were smooth, and richly ornamented with grotesque carving. In all the formal decorations the mystic circle and triangle played a prominent part. • But here and there were rude attempts at pictures. Superior indeed to the conventional design, of the Egyptians, but evidently conceived and executed on traditional lines. Most of them were of a maritime nature—ships and fish floating stiffly on the top of very solid water ; oars, ropes, anchors, such as an intelligent Child of seven might draw on its slate; occasionally, too, the grim repre- sentation of skulls and bones, and here and there a whole skeleton in a fantastic attitude.

At last something else loomed through the darkness, and as they drew near to it they saw that it was a small' temple, supported by eight thick columns of jasper. The canopy above was richly carved, and towered up so high that its summit was lost to sight in the gloom. An inscription ran along the face of it.

They passed between two of the pillars, and saw what looked like a gigantic coffin of white marble. The slab on the top, nearly a foot in thickness, was broken in two pieces, and one half lay on the ground. Tredegar went up to it, and directed the rays of the lantern into the interior. It was half full of a dull grey dust, and out of this protruded a few pieces of black metal, which looked more like scrap iron than anything else.

Tredegar took hold of pne of the pieces and pulled it out from the debris. Then, lo and behold! as it came into the light of the lamp it flashed with all the colours of the rainbow and glittered like a circlet of fire. It was studded with jewels—with diamonds, with rubies, with sapphires, with emeralds, lying on its dull surface like a crest of light on the dark waters of the sea.

Tredegar inspected it closely. It was circular in form, and had probably been a collar for the neck. He took out a penknife and scraped the dull surface, till there was the yellow glint of metal underneath. It was undoubtedly of solid gold, and, indeedi the magnificent jewels which studded it would hardly have been placed in a meaner setting. Then Tredegar set the lantern on the marble slab, and they both rummaged in the dust of the tomb and drew out one thing after another, till the floor was littered with treasure that might have provided the ransom of a dozen kings. Cups, vases, bracelets, chains, rings, ornaments, and vessels of every shape and description, all of gold, and most of them crusted with jewels. The sandy floor beside the tomb glittered like a galaxy of coloured stars. ' But more than once they drew out something of less weight that neither sparkled nor shone, and Mavanwy cried out with horror as the lamp fell on the dull surface. For the bones of a dead man were mingled with what might have been the treasures of an empire. In the excitement of the moment they forgot everything else, and they had not noticed that the candle in the lantern had burnt almost to the socket, and that in a few minutes they would be in darkness. At last the tomb only contained a thick layer of dull grey dust. They passed their hands through this again and again, but found nothing else. Tredegar looked at the glittering heap upon the floor and smiled. "Do you realise what this means, Mavanwy ?" he said. "It means a lot of money," she replied, with a sigh. "It means," he said, triumphantly, "that after the Government have taken their share of this treasure trove I shall Iv a rich man, and-"

He stopped. In the excitement he had forgotten all that had happened. I am glad," Mavanwy said, turning her face away from the light. "I am very glad, Emrys." "And you, Mavanwy ?" he cried, trying to take her hand.

" I shall still be as I am now," she an- swered faintly, moving a little away from him. "But lam glad—'for your sake." Tredegar frowned, and kicked the heap of ' gold and jewels. A gemmed cup flew tink- ling against the marble slab. "I beg your pardon," he said brusquely. "I had forgotten. I will not refer to the future. But with reference to the past, du you see what this means ? " Mavanwy shook her head. Indeed, she was not trying to think of the past. The dull future occupied all her mind. "The Walroyds ! " Tredegar said. " This explains much. The tomb is only hall full. This is the secret of their wealth, but Heaven knows how they discovered it. In the last hour much has been made plain to me. Walroyd's visits to the old lead mine. Mr. Cantrip's relations with his empioyer. Walroyd's eagerness to buy the property. I understand everything. But I expect he has taken the last penny from this bank of his, What, remains is for me—and the Govern- ment. I fancy ,the latter will have a con- siderable bill against Mr. Walroyd, and that PI "Oh, Emrys," she broke in hurriedly, " the lantern ! "

He walked round and noticed that• the light had grown very low and that it had begun to flicker ominously. "Great Heavens ! " he cried; "we must go at once." "I fancy we are too late," slle replied.

"I have two boxes of matches," he said; "we must get along with these. Come along at once, Mavanwy, we must make our way forward. It is hopeless to go back now. There must be an opening the other side of the cavern. At any rate it is cur only chance."

They hurried off from the tomb, but before they had gone half a dozen yards, the light leapt up in a last flicker, and they were in darkness.

Tredegar lit a match,. but the cool current of air that swept through the cavern blew it. out. He lit another, and yet another, shied- ing them with his hand. But the light was so uncertain and fitful that it was almost worse than useless, and served only to in- tensify the darkness. At last Tredegar stopped and laid his hand on Mavanwy's arm. "This is hopeless," he said, "and we are only wasting the matches. I have to cover them with my hand, and they give no light •at all. We will return."

They groped their way back in the dark ness, shuffling silently through the sand, and wandering from pillar to pillar till they reached the tomb, more by good fortune thar by any definite knowledge of the route. Then suddenly a small speck .of ]igh!' gleamed in the 'darkness, and moved slowly towards them. Tredegar whispered to Mav- anwy, and they both crept away from the tomb and hid behind one of- the great columns which supported the roof of the Lavern. The light disappeared every now and then, but it came nearer and nearer, and in a few minutes they heard the sound of soft footsteps in the sand, and the occasional click of a boot against the bones on the ground. The. semi-circle of light still advanced, but all was darkness behind it, and it was not until it was within a few yards of them that tl ey saw the shadowy figure of the man that carried it. He advanced to the tomb, and placing a lanterp op the marble slab, leant over the side and feered into the vault. Then for the first time the light fell on his features. It was John T. Walroyd. They saw a sudden look of rage and feat cross his face. The lantern was snatched

A TWEED GOWN,

."good enough" for a railway journey, gratifies the eyes of all beholders by her quiet yet well-built tailor-made costume. The days are now It tppily included in the past when people considered their oldest and -ugliest clothes the most suitaNe for travel- ling. This improvement has no doubt been brought about not wily by the good taste of the leaders of the world of dress, but by the increased comfort and cleanliness offered to the travelling public, by the railway and steamship companies. A girl who wishes to inspire a feeling of admiration in her own circle and in the eyes of all she may meet should aim at a next trim appearance, and select for her travelling toilette colours which do not show dust or stains. Black should, therefore, never be chosen, for it shows every speck of dust and is hot looking; grey, brown, or a mlxed tweed are the best shades. The gown sketched is of a so-called heather mixture, very m'ell cut and fitted, the smart little coat worn over a simple but modish blouse. A bright red leather belt and a red quill in the hat give a pleasing touch of -colour to the tout ensemble.

A gown to be really comfortable wear when travelling by land or by sea must be -made with a plainness and severity of style above everything. as any elaborate trim- mings only serve to catch dust and dirt, and

A REEFER COAT:

-therefore the whole toilette soon exhibits a general effect of shabbiness while yet com- paratively new. This is a particularly undesirable state of affairs to those to whom expense is a great consideration and the -purchase of a new frock a matter for much meditation. A serge frock, however, is one of the most- satisfactory investments one can make, provided always that it is well cut and fitted. A gown of this description can always hold its own amongst much more gorgeous toilettes; and this year a bright shade of blue serge is much in vogue, the serge being of a very fine light make. Just now when the evenings are apt to be chilly outdoor wraps demand their meed of attention. Very comfortable and easy to slip on is the little reefer coat sketched, which is in fact an old friend restored to our affections by the revolution of the wheel of fashion. There is, to. be true, a change in the sleeve which partakes of the nature of a modified bell shape widening out at the wrist. It is made of blue serge, and is daintily lined, with oyster satin. A PRETTY HAT:

always in favour, while for driving a sort of modified Inverness cape or coat with a semi- fitting back, loose fronts and cape-like sleeves, has a certain amount of vogue amongst those who are in search of novel- ties. Rather difficult to describe are the long loose capes with sleeves fashioned after the style of the extinct dolman, but very com- fortable they, prove in wear. Indeed, as I have said before, fashion and comfort have united their forces that one can look forward undisturbed to the autumn modes. The military-looking coat of serge or cloth with its fulness caught in at the back by a wide strap or band buttoned at either side is a cosy wrap to which we willingly return as the season advances. Just now these coats are' to be seen expressed in lighter materials, such as alpacas, and form smart dust cloaks. In this case, hcwever, they are generally made with a well-fitting shoulder cape. The pretty hat depicted is of burnt straw trimmed with clusters of red and black currants and their leaves. it is caught at the upturned side by a strapping and bow of scarlet velvet, and is an exceedingly stylish and smart-looking piece of headgear, which could be worn on into the autumn without being termed de mode. It is wonderful how early felt hats have this season made their appearance; and, indeed, a friend of mine who unexpectedly found herself in immediate need of . a straw hat had much difficulty in obtaining any- thing at all smart, as all the best milliners seemed to have only the remnants of their sales on view, all of which bore traces of their past experiences, or else a number of sporting-looking felt hatsJ

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Emperor of Austria.—The reception accorded to the King at Vienna was as enthusiastic as his Majesty could desire, though there was an ancient feud between the Guelphs and Hapsburghs, in which the former ulti- mately lost all their possessions. The King,it is known, entertains the highest regard for t4e Emperor Francis Joseph, who is regarded in Austria Hungary with much the same affection as was bestowed upon Queen Victoria in this country. The Emperor's private life has been full of sorrow and he seldom attends th eatres, or other places of amusement. Shooting, and hunting are his principal recreations, and in spite of the rigid Court etiquette at Vienna, the Emperor manages to live simply,and is both an early riser, and a hard worker. Royal Movements.—The King has been the guest of Lord and Lady Savile at Rufford Abbey for the Don- caster races, and His Majesty will reside at Balmoral until the end of the first week in October. Amongst other visits the King is expected to be the guest of Sir Reginald and Lady Gordon Cathcart at Cluny Castle before returning South. The Queen who has gone to Copenhagen to join the numerous family guests whom King Christian always entertains at this time of the year, will remain abroad until the end of next month. The Court will then be at Sandringham, where their Majesties will entertain some shooting parties. The Rufford Ghost.—Rufford Abbey, the seat of Lord and Lady Savile, in Notts, is a modern mansion, but it is built on the site of an ancient monaitery, and is declared to le haunted by a well-authenticated ghost. ' The King has on several occasions, stayed at Rufford Abbey, when Prince of Wales but his Majesty has never seen the ghostly vision, thoiigh several persons of un- doubted veracity have declared they have done so. A well-known lady was once greatly alarmed by seeing its reflection in her dressing glass, and she afterwards gave a detailed description of its appearance. Ghosts are somewhat discredited in these days, but however imaginary they may be, they have often had a very real effeec in killing people with fright, and the Ruffcird ghost is said to have terrorised more than one man into an untimely death. The Ducal Wedding.—The wedding of the Duke of Roxburghe and Miss May Goelet is to take place in November, and if the wishes of Mr. Goelet are to be followed, it will be carried out upon a scale of magnifi- cence seldom ifoever equalled in American society. The Duke is said to be opposed to anything like ostentatious display, but in such matters, men seldom manage to get their own way. lathe choice of an American bride, the Duke of Roxburghe has but followed the example of the Duke of Marlborough, andthe Duke of Manchester, and precedents established on these occasions will no doubt have their influence upon the arrangements of the Ducal marriage. The Goelets derive their wealth from Real estate in New York, and rank among the "knicker- bocker class of society in America, but the statement that Miss Goelet has a fortune of £5,000,000 is scarcely correct. No doubt she has a large independent fortune, but she has only a life interest in the bulk of the property under her father's will. The Ducal Mansions.—The two seats of the Duke of Roxburghe, Floors Castle and Broxmouth Park, are intimately associated with stirring incidents in Scottish history, so that if the future Duchess is anything. of an antiquary, she will find much to interest her In the surroundings of the Ducal mansions. Of Floors Castle, which is situated close to the border about a mile from Kelso, Sir Walter Scott said it combined "the ideas of ancient grandeur with those of modern taste." Since then additions and improvements have been made to the Castle and the gardens and grounds have been beaulii- fully laid out. It was here—the scene of so many border conflicts—that James 11. of Scotland was killed in 1460. Broxmouth also stands on classical ground, for close by is the scene of the Battle of Dunbar, where Cromwell triumphed over tho Scottish army on September 3rd, 1650.

Summer Tragedies.—The summerhas been remarkable not only for its bad weather but also for the large number of "mysterious disappearances" in which young women have generally been the cause'of anxiety. It is notorious that the number of suicides always greatly increases in the summer time, but if climatic conditions are the governing factor, in such cases, this year ought to have been an exception, seeing that we have had so little real summer weather. Yet there has been more than the usual epidemic of tragedies and "disappear- ances" and to these must be added a new class of victims, those people who utterly forget who they are, and where they live. Several young women have been found in this condition lately, their minds being an utter blank as to their own identity. Of all the forma of mental relapse, this seems the most strange, as the victims are, as a rule, perfectly sane and in excellent health. _ . .

Pockets.—Though the tailors are no more laslish in the matter of pockets in skirts than are dressmakers, they are certainly doing their best to make up the de- ficiency by giving us a great many pockets in our coats. The drawback to this arrangement is that we do not ilways need a coat, still one must be thankful for small mercies where the whims and fancies of Dame Fashion are concerned. What we shall do when the pouched blouse is no more, it is difficult to say. The amount of odds and ends it is possible to store into this receptacle is little short of marvellous, and such a state of affair compensates in no inconsiderable degree for our still quite pocketless condition. A Barbarous Custom.—Birds of every possible size and plumage are in evidence in all the second and third rate millinery establishments, the sea pigeon and king- fisher being apparently preferred above all others by callous purchasers. Now so many leaders of fashion decline to wear these dead birde!that they are happily no longer really modish, and it is to be trusted that in the fulness r of time lesser lights will come to regard the matter from the same standpoint. Fashionable Headgear.—Cloth toques with the fur brims promise to enjoy a wide vogue this autumn and coming winter, a quill, a cluster of flowers, or aknot of ribbon, being the only trimming necessary. Felt, and silk and beaver hats too, will be very popular, and with a tailor-made gown, nothing is more suitable or smart looking. Many a costly tailor-made costume is spoilt by impossible headgear. This to be correct, should be plain, neat, and business-like, with nothing about it that even our climate can spoil. Dearer Preserves.—The failure of the fruit crop and the increased prioe of sugar, are responsible for making jam much dearer, and housewives who have to cater for large families will feel the difference. Those who are fortunate enough to have had any fruit of their own grow- ing to spare, will have done well to preserve it for winter use, as, contrary to the old fashioned ideas, it is now held that sweets and jams when pure are excellent food for growing children. .But many people have given up making jams. It can always be had of excellent quality from the grocery stores though now we are told it is going to be considerably dearer. MADGE.

OVERWORKED GIRLS. Interview with one of the Victims

Shop and Factory girls have the advantage of regular hours, and there are inspectors whose duty it is to see that the'conditions under which they work are sanitary, and that the hours do not exceed the legal limit. There is, however, a class—Domestic Servants—which enjoys no such benefits. _ .

The strongest constitution may be broken down by overwork. The story of a young servant in Lincoln , shire, showing how she regained her health by Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people is very interesting reading. The girl's name is Charlotte Wood, and she resides at 310, Willingham-street, Great Grimsby. When Miss Wood talked to a reporter of the "Grimsby Daily Telegraph," she at the very outset declared her firm belief that her recovery was due to Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people and to nothing. else. "It was indigestion and anmnza that caused the trouble," said Miss Wood, "and it was brought on through overwork in a house. I was really not strong enough for the work I did. l'he first thing I noticed the matter with me was loss of sleep. That was about ten or eleven months ago. I could not sleep at all, and very soon had pains in the chest and back, by which at last I was laid up. I had no appetite, and in fact felt better when I did not eat, for whenever I did take any- thing it caused dreadful pain. I could not even drink a glass of water without vomiting. I suffered like this for over five months."

"And what induced you to give Dr. Williams' pink pills a trial?" "It was owing to the advice of a friend. She had had ulcer of the stomach, and received considerable benefit from Dr. Williams' pink pills. She strongly re- commended me to get some. I did get a box, and after taking the contents felt great relief. I then got a second box and it was really wonderful how much better it made me. A third box entirely cured me." "And were these all you took ?" "I had only four boxes, which I took during about two months, I am now in good health, which is all owing to Dr. Williams' pink pills, for before I took them I am certain I was going down the hill." Here is one of the many cases in which Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people—the genuine pills, not a sham or substitute, but Dr. Williams'—have made a new being of a suffering, anmmic invalid. Thousands of strong men and women who were once miserable weaklings, 'owe their health to the same remedy. Ana3mia, bile, consumption, fits, gout, headache, indiges- tion, kidney disease, paralysis, rheumatism, St. Vitus' dance, have all been cured after all other treatment proved futile. Williams Medicine Co„ Holbom-viaduct, London, send post free for two and ninepence, but the genuine pills can be obtained everywhere,provided care is taken to avoid substitutes lacking Dr. Williams' name. The injury inflicted on the Constitution of Girls by Overwork, in Houses as well as Factories and Shops, is incalculable. We have indicated above the way in which the trouble can be met. But it is time that Employers realise that they inflict a real hardship on Girls whom they overwork.

Machinery, yarns, and textile fabrics and leather manufacturers all show large increases in experts.

BY HARRIS BURLAND, AUTMOR OF 'DACOBRiCETC.

CHAPTER XXll.—Continued

"1 cannot speak of what I saw that even- ing," she said. "It is too horrible! After it—after my father—l was seized, and only prayed that I might die without pain. Then I fainted, and remember no more till I woke up in the darkness. Fortunately, I had a box of matches in my pocket, and lighting them one after the other I tried to make my way out of these endless passages and gal- leries and caverns. Every moment I ex- pected to see the monster that had killed my father. Every drop of water that fell from the roof, and every flake of rock that tinkled to the ground concentrated a year of agony into the space of a second. In the last few days I have died a dozen deaths. Oh, Emrys, thank God that you have come! —though when you touched my hair I thought that it was—" "But food ?" he said, hurriedly opening his knapsack. "I have some here. How have you existed ?" "I will tell you," she continued; "it almost seems like a miracle. In the course of my wanderings I came to a high ledge on the wall of one of the caverns. I thought it would be a good place to conceal myself, and, after many attempts, succeeded in get- ting up to it. There, to my surprise, I found a lantern, half a dozen candles, a store of matches, and a large stock ot provisions— tinned and potted meats, biscuits,L etc. I could have lived here fOrta month."

"I don't think it is an .interposition of Providence," Emrys answered, slowly. He was thinking of Walroyd's visits to the shaft, and when he had put two and two to- gether the 4hole thing seemed very far short of a miracle.

Where is this food ?" he asked, after a pause, "I will show you," she replied. "It is \not far from here. In spite of all I have gone through, I have kept my wits about me. I have marked the rock every twenty yards, and can find my way about. I cannot under- stand, though, why I have not been able to find any way of escape from this horrible dungeon." She led the way to the ledge, stopping every minute to examine the wall of the pas- sage and find the large crpsses she had scratched on the stone. When they reached the spot, Tredegar examined the store of provisions. He found a tin-opener and two knives, but nothing that would give any clue to the person who had placed them there. Then he opened one of the. biscuit-tins. • It was labelled " Osborne," but contained "Bath Olivers," and he suddenly recol- lected that the only night he had ever dined with the Walroyds they had had these bis- cuits with their cheese. He also remem- bered John Walroyd saying that it was the only English biscuit he really cared for. He decided to tell Ma.vanwy all he knew about Walroyd, and he did so in as few words as possible. When he had finished his story she shook her head, wild looked at him as though asking for some ' explanation. He was silent, and appeared to be thinking of some possible solution to the mystery. " I don't understand," Mavanwy said. "It does not seem to me that Walroyd could •have any possible business in this place. And lam sure that no one would come here for pleasure. The darkness, the silence, and the awful terror which lies hidden somewhere in these depths- Oh, Emrys, if we should meet it! I cannot describe to you the horrible fear I have of it !" .

"1 ask nothing better than to meet it," Tredegar replied, grimly. " I came here, first on the chance of finding you, and then to get to close quarters with this monster. My innocence cannot be established till it is captured or killed. I intend to kill it ! I thank God that he has given me the strength to do so. You are not afraid of it, Mavanwy, when yo 4 are with me ?" She looked for a moment on his gigantic form, and smiled.

"No, Emrys," she replied, "I am not afraid—now—with you. But what is it? A man or a beast? How did it come here ?" Emrys was silent. He did not wish her ' to know the truth. The secret of this creature's identity could never be confided to any living soul. He owed that much to Cynthia.

Have you no views on the subject ?" she asked, after a pause. "Yes," he answered: "I think that it is human—a homicidal maniac. But it has sunk to the level of a beast, and must be regarded as such. However, we are not concerned with it till it shows itself. Our first business is to get out of here—for you to get out of here. I shall return when you are safe. I have my work yet to do." They set off on their search. The lantern burned but feebly in the close atmosphere, and they could only see half a dozen yards in front of them. Every twenty paces they stopped and made a cross on the rock with a piece of sharp stone. They hardly spoke to each other. Mavanwy's face was wan and pinched after all the terrors she had experienced, and she was almost too tired to drag her feet along. Tredegar's thoUghts were none too pleasant, though he tried to conceal the bitterness of them from Mavanwy. He did not know what had come between them, but he felt that the occasion called for some display 'of affection, and that whatever laws Mavanwy had made unto herself should have been relaxed under the circumstances. She had stood face to face with a horrible death, and the sudden re- action would have excused a departure from the course she had marked out for herself. He thought pitterly that if she had any real affection for him she would have flung her- self into his arms, and given way at least for a moment to the dictates of her heart. From time to time Ae looked at him ner- vously, and made as though she would speak but she dared not trust herself; and they wandered through the long galleries and pas- sages in silence. After an hour's fruitless search, they came to a part of the mine which neither of them remembered to have passed through before. Mavanwy was certain on this point, for there were no crosses on the walls. Tredegar could not be quite so sure, as he had made most of his exploration in darkness. It was a higlt cavern, so high that the feeble light of the lantern could not penetrate to the root. They crossed it, and came to a narrow tunne! on the far side.

Then suddenly a cool draught came up from the dark entrance, and the candle ir the lantern flickered. "There is some communication with the open air here," said Tredegar. "My worlll what a relief to be able to breathe !"

They stopped for a moment, and, peering down the passage, drank in the fresh breeze. Then they proceeded through the archway.. The tunnel was very narrow, and sloped rapidly downwards. The roof glistened with moisture, and there appeared to be a crystal line deposit on the walls. Tredegar scraped off a little of it with his nail, and put his finger in. his mouth. It tasted of salt, and he guessed that this part of the workings ran under the sea. There was, however, no sigr, of any lead ore, and he feared that it might prove to be a cul-de-sac which had been aban- doned by the miners in disgust. However, as they proceeded down in,to bowels of the earth, the current of air seemed to increase in volume, and Tredegar fancied that he could scent the salt breezes of the sea. It appeared as though they must be nearing an opening of some sort. - At last they came to a small heap of broket+ rock, and beyond it the tunnel narrowed so considerably that Tredegar could hardly squeeze his gigantic frame between the two

sides of it. The floor was two feet deep in debris, and the roof was so low that he had to bend almost double to pass under it. He s.topped and inspected the walls witn the lantern. He gave a low whistle of surprise. "This has been excavated 'quite recently," he said, turning to Maya nwy. " .^-ot more than a year ago, at any rate. I shOuld like to discover who has done it, and why it has been done."

He moved on a few 'paces, and then sud- denly he stopped. The feeble light of the lantern showed nothing but a dark abyss before them. He shrank back from the edge,. and told Mavanwy to stand still. Then he crept cautiously forward on his hands and knees, and saw that a long flight of rudely excavated stone steps ran sideways down the face of the precipice. The ground ap, peared to be some forty feet below them, and there was a .glimmer of white at the bottom when the lantern ivas directed downwards into the darkness.

"Take hold of my hand, Mavanwy," Tredegar said, "and keep close to the wall. You will be quite safe. I will go first. Or I will carry you if you like." "1 would rather hold your hand," Alavanwy replied. "I am not afraid." The two descended cautiously step by step till they reached the bottom, and their feet sank deep into a layer of fine white sand. Tredegar stooped and picked. up a handful. It was full of broken pieces of shell and dried seaweed, and it was so dry that it ran out between his fingers before he had time to thoroughly examine it. Then he looked carefully round; and saw what he quite ex- pected to see—innumerable footprints 'crossing the sand in every direction. They walked along by the wall, and noticed that the bottom part of it was hollowed out as though by the action of the sea. Every now and then they came across small round pebbles and boulders. It was quite evident that at some distant period the ocean had flowed into this wide cavt.rn.

When they had gone about fifty yards. they both stopped and' looked at each other in amazement. For havn• out in the wall of rock there was a low - archway, and set deep in this was a door of rough dark wood, heavily clamped with iron. Tredegar seized a rusty iron handle and turned it. But the door did not open. Then he threw all his gigantic weight against it, but it did not give an inch, though they heard the hinges creak and the lock rattle in its socket.. "I must break it open," he said, looking' rather doubtfully at the solid mass of oak and iron. "You had better stand away, Mavanwy." She retreated a few paces and Tredegar, picicing up a' boulder that must have weighed nearly two hundredweight, hurled it with all his force at the lock The cavern rever- brated with the crash, and the missile split into two pieces. A huge dent in the wood, and the plate of the lock, crumpled and torn like a piece of paper, showed the fearful strength of the blow. But the lock itself still held, though one of the timbers of the door had craeked in the middle, and the top half had been driven an inch inwards.

Tredegar took up another boulder from the soft sand and sent it spinning against the other side of the door with such force that the stone crumbled, into a hundred fragments, and, the hinges gave way, tearing out the staples from the solid rock. Then he went up to the door, and catching hold of it with both hands literally wrenched the timber off the lock and flung it out into the darkness beyond. .

then they both stepped through the opening. In front of diem loomed another huge cavern, and the light of the lamp showed 'a massive pillar rising trom the floor and dis- appearing in the darkness above. Tredegar's foot struck something hard, which was half- buried in the sand. He stooped and un- covered it, and then' tried to bury it again before Mavanwy could catch a glimpse of its smooth yellow surface. It was a human skull, but he need not have taken the trouble to conceal it, for as far as the yellow light of the lantern shone, the whole floor was dotted with them, and human bones stuck out from the 'sand like sticks in every direction. Mavanwy cried out aloud in horror, and turned her eyes away. from the ghastly scene to the walls of the cavern.

Tredegar threw the light of the lantern upon them, and they saw niche after niche cut in the rock, and stretching away tier after tier into the darkness. And in each niche there was a perfect skeleton lying on its back, with its arms folded across its breast, and its hollow eyes staring at the rocky canopy above it. For a few moments Mavanwy and Tredegar neither moved nor spoke, but gazed in silence on the awful spectacle before them. The dim light of the lantern intensified the horror of the scene. It would have been sufficiently gruesome in broad daylight, but the feeble yellow flame that was almost swallowed up in the darkness, lent an air of mystery to the whole picture, and suggested sights even more terrible in the glooln beyond. Then Tredegar laughed, and the sound of his laughter echoed and re-echoed through the cavern, till it seemed as though he 'were being mimicked by a hundred voices of the dead.

" I don't think they'll hurt us," he said. "It's not worse than the catacombs at Rome must have been. I should think it would be most interesting to an antiquarian. I wonder who they are, and how they got here? Ancient Britons, I expect. Perhaps some of our ancestors, Mavanwy. " Don't jest, Emrys," she replied, gravely. " I think it is terrible !" and she shuddered. "„Well, we are not concerned with them, Mavanwy. Our business is to get out of here: We must be near an opening.. Do you notice how cool the air is? One can almost smell the sea." "Let

" Let us go on," she said, hurriedly. us get out as quickly as possible." They proceeded slowly along by the wall of the cavern, following the line of numerous footprints in the sand. It was evident that someone had passed to, and fro several times in this direction. Mavanwy kept her eyes on the ground, picking her way carefully among the bones and skulls. But Tredegar scrutinised the niches in the hope of finding some clue to their history and origin. When they' had gone about a hundred yards, he stopped and examined one of them more closely, It was almost twice the size of the others, and its edges were decorated with a rude border of scroll work and fantastic ,ymbols4 He held the lantern close up to it, and then gave an exclamation of surprise. In the centre of the top border, and surrounded by a wreath of what might have been oak- leaves, there was carved the same strange symbol which he had seen on the half disc of gold—the same, too, as that which had been engraved on Mr. Morgan's ring—a right- angled triangle, with the right angle at the centre of a circle.

The circumstance was so extraordinary that for the moment all other thoughts passed out of his mind. He called Mavanwy's attention to it, and she, too, forgot the horrors of the place in her amazement. Neither of them could trace any connection between these catacombs and a curio which had been picked up on a lonely Pacific island. Mavanwy regarded it as a curious coincidence; but Tredegar—who knew, at any rate, to whom the disc had once belonged—saw only another link in the mystetious chain of circumstances -4hich was being ?...sawly forged 'about his life.,

from the slab, and its light thrown on the heap of gold and jewels, and then round in a circle through the darkness,. as though in the hope of discovering who had been meddling with the treasure. Tredegar and Mavanwy drew back their heads sharply before the light fell on the pillar behind which they were con- cealed,

Then Walroyd looked for a moment at the footprints in the sand, and drawing out his revolver, walked straight 'up to them, and flashed the lantern in their faces.

Tredegar would have sprung upon him there and then and risked the chance of being shot, but Mavanwy laid one hand on his arm and implored him to restrain himself.

For -a few seconds neither of the three spoke. Then Walroyd drew back a couple of paces, and smiled sardonically. "We have been looking everywhere for you, Miss Morgan," he said in a tone of re- pressed fury. I am glad to find you have been in such safe hands—with the murderer Of your father."

" You infernal scoundrel," cried Tredegar hoarsely. "Do you mean to suggest that Miss Morgan has been with me f?ir the last few days ? "

" Appearances are rather against you," Walroyd replied. "But in any case, you may consider yourself my prisoner. I will take care that you do not escape again." Tredegar crouched low behind the pillar, and his great muscles quivered like the sinews of a wild beast about to spring. But again Mavanwy, whispered in his ear.. She knew that Walroyd could hardly miss him at that distance, and that the American's eye was as keen as his hand was sure.

"You blackguard ! " Tredegar said; "you had better not let me get within arm's length of you, or I will choke the life out of you." I will take your advice," Walroyd replied. "You are not likely to get much closer to me than you are at present." "What do you intend to do ? " asked Tredegar, wishing to gain time, while he thought out some scheme for depriving Wal- rovd of his weapon. "I have told you. You are my prisoner." • _ .

"I have a proposition to mike," Tredegar replied. "Both 1 and Miss Morgan know your secret. If you will conduct us out of this place, and let me go free, and•say nothing of what has happened, we will keep silence about the treasure and undertake not to interfere with your disposal of it." Walroyd did not answer, but he kept his keen eyes on Tredegar's face, and raised his revolver a little so as to cover his heart. Walroyd's own face was in darkness, and neither Tredegar nor Mavanwy could read his thoughts. Then Walroyd laughed. "it is for me to make terms," he said, "and my terms are these. Unless you both swear a solemn oath to say nothing of the treasure, I shall ensure your silence in my own way." "And that way is ? " Tredegar *queried. Walroyd shrugged his shoulders. "When is a man most silent ? " he asked.

"You mean that you will kill us both," Tredegar said. "I have no doubt you would shoot me like you would a dog. But Miss Morgan ? " "I would never offer violence to a lady," he replied courteously. "But lam afraid I should be compelled to decline to show Miss Morgan the way out of the cavern. And she doubtless would not wish to leave you." Tredegar's blood ran cold with horror. In a moment of time he saw the terrible fate of the woman he loved, condemned to linger out her life in darkness with no companionship but .his own dead body. A more fiendish scheme had never been designed by man. It would be a thousand times wirse than death. Tredegar prayed that he might restrain himself, and keep a cool head. For he knew now that whatever he swore Walroyd would kill them 'both. The oath would be a farce. Walroyd knew well 'enough that Tredegar;s innocence was already on the way to be proved, that he himself had described the murderer of Dennis Riley, and that those who had pre- viously been hunting for Erirys Tredegar were now in search of another man. But Walroyd supposed that Tredegar had been hiding in the lead mine, and that he was ignorant of the circumstances attending Riley's death. So he had proposed an alter- native bargain, in the hope that Tredegar would refuse, and that the murder would not have to be done iil cold blood. Tredegar was not prepared to enlighten him on this point. "I think," he said, after a pause, "that you would ran rather a risk. Miss Morgan might find her way out, and then—well ?" Miss Morgan might reach the bottom of the shaft," Walroy-d replied. " But I think I could put two or three obstacles in her way. And, to tell you the truth, Tredegar, I have made up my mind to have the shaft covered over. It is dangerous. I should like to know your decision as soon as possible." Tredegar had made up his mind. Twelve feet of rock stood between him and Walroyd. He saw the folly of leaving his position, and marching tamely in _front of another man's revolver till such time as the latter should think fit to put a bullet in his back. The darkness was all in his favour. Walroyd's lantern would only betray his own where- abouts. The advantage would be with the pursued, who could slip from pillar to pillar, and chance a stray shot till an opportunity offered itself. His only fear was for Mavanwy. He drew her close to him and whispered in her ear.

"He means to kill us both in any case," he said. ".It is better to have it out with him now. I will lead him a pretty dance through the darkness. I run no risk. Directly he follows me away from here, slip into the tomb and lie down. You will be safe there from a chance bullet."

"If he kills you !" she whispered, in terror.

"It's not likely," Tredegar answered. "Be brave, my darling." He drew her closer still to him, so that a strand of her hair touched his cheek. But he could feel her shrink away from him, and he did not kiss her.

"Well," said ' Walroyd, sharply. "Have you decided ?"

BACK TO THE LAND.

It has often been asserted that to mike a nation healthy it must get back to the land. A great deal of truth lies :in such a 'statement, but it is nevertheless a fallacy to think that country life is always conducive to health. Our representative was recently at Beeston in Nottinghamshire, and called in at Padge Farm to see Mrs. S. A. Chamberlain, who had some interesting things to say upon this subject. Now, although she has been a country woman all her life, yet at times she has battled with might and main against sickness. "My troubles commenced," she said, "nine years ago, when I was attacked with diabetes. For a long time I was under medical treatment, and when atllast I was declared to be well I was in reality in a low and weakcondition. From the illness of nine years ago I never recovered, and to such a state did I sink, that it was impossible for me to take any food with pleasure. The breaking-up of my health brought me to a state of general debility. This, to a woman who must be ever at her work, is a terrible thing. Moreover, I became constipated, and this, com- bined with my weak condition, was more than I could bear., For days at a time I would be in a hot, feverish and excited condition. I tried medical men in Nottingham in order to see whether it were possible to obtain some relief for my long pent- up sufferings. But all was of no use, for the dozens of different remedies I tried still left me far from experiencing the joys of good health. Relief came at last, however, for looking through a newspaper I came across an account of the cure of a case as bad, if not even worse, than my own, by Dr. Morse's Indian Root Pills. I made up my mind that I would give them a fair trial. A few bottles have made a most wonderful change in my health. My constipation has left me, and once more I am enjoying the blessings of perfect health." Dr. Morse's Indian Root Pills are a. positive cure for liver and kidney complaints, bilious- ness, indigestion, constipation, piles, dyspepsia, foul breath, sallow complexion, 1 and female ailments. They are a perfect blood purifier, curing eczdn:a, pimples and blotches. Sold by leading chemists and stores, price is. lid. per bottle. If unable to obtain where you reside, Dr. Morse's Indian Root Pills may be had post free on receipt of price from the W. H. Comstock Company, Ltd., 21, Farringdon Avenue, .London,E.C. There are many points in favour of the back to the land movement. It would hardly seem, however, from the investigations made at Padge Farm, that the health of the community will be made better by its realisation, unless preventive measures are taken to safeguard the public.

EVENTS OF THE WEEK

TWO NEW COLONIAL GOVERNORS.

The promotion- of Lord Northcote to the fbigbly important post of Governor-General of .Australia in the natural course of events left a vacancy at Bombay, about which there has been considerable speculation in political 'circles. Nov it is announced that Lord Lamington has accepted the post and. will proceed there immediately to take up the duties. Lord Lamington, who is forty-three years of age, has been prominent in politics .ever since he came down from Christ Church, 'Oxford, at the beginning of the eighties. He sat for some years as M.P. for St. Pancras, and was private secretary to the late Marquis of 'Salisbury during tho Home Rule crisis. In 1890 he succeeded to the title and five years 'later he was appointed Governor of Queens- land. In that same year he married the daughter of Lord Newlands, and a son and heir was born to them in the colony, who bears amongst his other names that of " Bris- bane " to mark the fact. Another new Governor is Sir Henry Arthur Blake, who is exactly twenty years older than Lord Lamington. He is at the present time at Hong - Kong, to which he Was :appointed in 1898, but he has been chosen

;LORD LAMINGTON. SIR H. A. BLAKE, G.C.M.G. 10 succeed Sir West Ridgeway at Ceylon. His career has ben most distinguished. Sir Henry Blake started life in the Irish Constab- ulary, in which he rose to special magisterial 'appointments. In 1884 he was made Gover- nor of the Bahamas, and in 1887 he was -transferred to Newfoundland. A year later he .appoint ed to Queensland, but resigned with- out taking up the appriintment at all. A year later he became Captain. General and Gover- -nor-in-Chief of Jamaica, and he stayed in .command of the island for eight years, his -term of office being twice extended at the special request of the Legislature and public btrodies of the place. In 1897 he went on to •ul°ng Kong, which he now vacates for Ceylon.

THE LATEST IN TRAMCARS.

There is nothing in which such immense -improvement has been effected of late years -as in the matter of tramcars. The tramcar of the latest 'type is hardly recognisable as be- longing to the same genus a§ the tram of the last decade. To begin with—except in Lon- don, which is municipally very backward, but IS gradually following the example of some of the more progressive provincial towns—the Iwo-horsed tramcar with its fearful wear and tear on the horses has given way to electric cars, 'the poor man's motor," as Mr. Benn happily christened then? at Tooting. These cars are, of course, much faster, considerably exceeding the legal limit, it is whispered, and, -therefore, they only stop at stages, and are not pulled up at any point for the convenience ,of any stray passenger. This is a great sav- ing of time and labour in itself, and every effort is being made to render these vehicles as economical and convenient as possible. At Southport, for instance, on the new tramcars the flesh-and-blood conductor, who has always seemed such an essential part of the vehicle, has been abolished entirely. In his place is

NY-IN-THE-SLOT C WORKING IN SOUTHPORT.

an automatic machine by the driver, into -which the passengers drop their fares, receiv- ing tickets in exchange. The dropping of the penny in the slot automatically affords en- trance; and it is under the immediate super- vision of the driver, who sees that all is fair and above board. Thus at one blow the race .of conductors is summarily abolished, and the expenses of the company in wages practically halved. It is an admirable idea, and • should be universally adopted. However, Conservative London, where very many thousands a week 'could be saved by this simple method, will probably be amongst the last to take advan- tage of it.

THE ENGAGEMENT OF THE MOMENT.

The announcement of the engagement of the Duke of Roxburghe to Miss Mary Goelet has created the liveliest interest on both sides of the Atlantic. Apart from the boy Duke of Leinster, the Duke of lioxburghe is the last bachelor in the highest gikade of the peerage, and thus rumour has often made free with his matrimonial intentions. As for his fiancée, Miss May Goelet, probably no young lady's -engagement has been more frequently an- -flounced by the papers of her own County, -which have coupled her with all the lesser European royalties, the leading young un- married peers of this country, and every rich and prominent man in the States. Her name has so frequently been linked with that of the Duke of Roxburghe during the last five years, that people had quite begun to think that it Was not after all going to come off I A few -weeks ago, however, he travelled over to America in company with Miss Goelet and her

THE DUKE OF ROXDURGHE-MISS MAY GOELET. mother, Mrs. Ogden Goelet, incognito. Since then he has been their guest, and his presence and his engagement were revealed simul- taneously last week. He is twenty-seven Years of age, and did very well out in South Africa with his regiment, the Royal Horse Guards Blue. In fact, it was thought that at °ne time he was going to be recommended for the V.C. for saving a trooper's life under fire. He is very popular—not least of all with the Royal family, who have always had the greatest regard for his mother, who will now SO soon be the Dowager Duchess. He went on the famous " Ophir" trip with the Prince and Princess of Wales. Miss Goelet herself, in

LEADER OF THE MACEDONIAN REVOLT.

The generalissimo and chief organiser of the formidable insurrection in Macedonia, Boris Sarafoff, is still quite a young man. He is not a Bulgarian, like many sof the other rebel leaders, but a real Macedonian, having been born at Libiakhovo, near Nevrokop, in 1876. When still a boy he was sent to Bulgaria for his elementary edu- cation. After- wards he pro- ceeded to St. Petersburg, and completed his studies at the Military Acade- my in that city. He then entered the Bulgarian army, and proved a very capable officer. Hedistinguished himself in 1895 at the siege of Melnik, which he captured by a coup de main. In 1899 he re- signed his com- mission. The first years of his public life he devoted to promoting the interests of Bulgaria in Macedonia, encouraging the idea that Macedonia should belong to Bulgaria. But he afterwards changed his mind, and he is now giving all his energies to the formation of a new Balkan State, for which he has adopted the formula, "Macedonia for the Macedonians." The result has been that he has ceased to be a "persona gratissima" in Sofia, and not very long ago he was arrested and expelled from the Principality. His change of views resulted in a serious split in the Macedonian Coanittee, which for a time paralysed the revolutionary movement, but this has .lately been healed, and Boris Sara- foff is now at the head of the whole move- ment.

A £l,ooo,otto HOTEL.

The New Midland Hotel of the Midlana Railway Company at Manchester, one of the finest buildings of its kind in the country, has taken over three years to build, covers an area of two acres, contains 480 rooms, of which 400 are bedrooms, and cost over a million stirling. It .occupies a splendid site in front of the

THE MIDLAND HOTEL, MANCHESTER,

The huge building, con- structed mainly of steel, is fireproof through- out, and contains many features illustrative of the remarkable progress made in this class of accomodation. When the railway company acquired the site they entered into an under- taking to provide a new concert hall in the hotel in the place of the Old Gentlemen's Concert Hall, a building which for nearly iso years had played a prominent part in the musical education of the city. They have kept their word by providiug a large room, decorated in white and gold, with dark oak columns, and capable of accomodating, with its gallery, 800 people. It is approached by a handsome marble staircase from the entrance hall. In addition to rooms one usually finds in first-class hotels, there is a fine lounge, which has a balcony for a band, a French restaurant, a winter garden, a private ball- room, a Turkish bath, and private dining rooms and reception rooms. The dining saloons are furnished and upholstered in a most lavish manner, and white and delicately tinted marble has been freely used.

AUSTRALIA'S COMING CAPITAL.

Speculation is running high as to how long it will take Australia to make its new capital, Tumut. Australia is used to sudden city- building. No town within its borders is yet 120 years old. Melbourne—still slightly the biggest of them—is not quite seventy; sixty years ago people were advertising for stilts to help them get across the quagmires in its main street, and all Toorak—now its Belgravia and Mayfair and Kensington combined—was sold for

1 - "'-'- =---1 --M -) -.1.

...............-.

..-......-.... .........

BIRD'S-EYE VIEW OF THE TUMUT OF TO-DAY. £l2O. The worst of the sudden system was that houses were put up anyhow. To this day Sydney streets are disgraced by the " shant y"- like erections that rub shoulders with some of their finest buildings. But Tumut, we may hope, will be more carefully treated. The spreading, scattered little houses will still bask placidly among their trees on the long, slow slope to Tumut River; but on the other slope, that is now just sheep-paddocks covered with the wiry native grasses and dead timber stand- ing ghostly white, there will be great palaces of granite and marble, Government House and Parliament House and public offices and private mansions, such as become the dignity of the Commonwealth's chief city. The sur- roundings of Tumut are prosaic enough, but they manage to epitomise Australia fairly well. There are good roads and a passable r4ilway to Tumut. The new railway runs up the Adelong Creek through one of the oldest goldfields in the Colony. Here is the first mine that struck payable gold a thousand feet down, and secured the Government reward of Li,ooo. Most of the work is now done by sluicing, and more than a thousand loads a day are washed in the district. Tumut dis- trict is very fertile. Tobacco, maize, and fruits of all kinds can be easily cultivated, for between the river-flat and the great ridges to southwards there are climates to suit all tastes. "Failure of crops in the district has never; been known," says the official reports Altogether the future looks happyd

ATTRACTIVE GIRLS.

GOLD IN ENGLAND.

News is to hand that gold has been found in a plot of land in Durham. This establishes another connection between the coal county and the far off-land, Australia. A few years ago, a famous Australian chemist found— not gold, but a far more valuable substance. He dis- covered the vegetable substance which forms the chief ingredient in the famous household remedy, Charles Forde's bile beans. From that discovery, not only Durham people, but people all over the globe have benefitted. Now Mrs. Mary Greener of North Terrace, Waterhouse's Colliery, Durham, says, that in finding these beans she has found something even better than a gold mine. To a" Durham Adver- tiser" reporter, she said recently:—" I became so bad from liver disease that it was impossible for me to lie down, and so I had often to sit up all night. I went to Sherbum Hospital for three months, but they could not cure me. I then consulted a doctor, but neither his advice nor his medicine did me the slightest good. I had constant pain in the back and side, could not eat, had terrible headaches, was sometimes so fagged and done up, and had such awful pains in the stomach, that I scarcely cared what happened to me. My son begged me to try Chas. Forde's bile beans. At first I refused, thinking they were same as other medicines. Then he read me a number of instances of the cures they have worked, and I determined to give them a trial. After a few doses I experienced distinct relief. I continued to take them regularly, taking two every night for eight weeks, and the result is lam now in perfect health. I am now so hearty that I can eat anything. They have made me feel as I used to feel in my younger days, and I can recommend them to all who suffer from liver complaint in any form." Bile beans, which did so much for Mrs. Greener, are obtainable from all chemists at one and three-half-pence or two and nine per box, but you are warned against worthless, if not actually harmful substitutes.

===== 0002601_19030912_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
PROPOSED ROMAN CATHOLIC REFORMATORY AT WIDNES.

The monthly meeting of the Widnes Town Council was held on Tuesday, the Mayor (Alderman W. W Gossage) presiding. The minutes of the Highway Committee contained a letter from Mr. Richard Yates, solicitor, secretary to the Liverpool Catholic Reformatory Association, enclosing a plan showing the position of certain land arranged to be bought for a new school to be erected in Norlands-lane, Widnes, and asking the Town Council to extend the main sewer to meet the requirements of the new building.—The Committee recommended that the sewer be extended as requested at an estimated cost of £650.

Councillor Lewis asked if the land purchased was within the borough boundary. He would also like to know something of the character of the boys, and whether this was a new institution or a transfer from some. other neighbourhood. . _

Alderman Quinn replied that the land was within the borough boundary. As regards the kind of boys that were sent to these institutions, they were sent there not for doing good but for something else. It could hardly be said that it was a new institution, because these boys were formerly on the ship Clarence, which was burned down in 1899. Afterwards they were taken to a college at Mold, belonging to an order of Jesuits. After about twelve or eighteen months the Jesuits required their building, and for some time the boys had been stationed at Kirkedge, seven miles outside of Sheffield. The Achool belonged to the diocese of Liverpool, and was managed by Liverpool gentlemen, and it was highly desirable that it should be nearer to Liverpool. The Managers had also received an intima- tion from the Home Office that they must have their school nearer to the diocese to which it belonged. Alderman Shuttleworth said it was highlyundesirable for such an institntion to be brought into Widnes.- The boys on the ship Clarence were a bad lot of boys, and, as they all knew, had burned their ship. There were enough bad boys in Widnes, without offering an induce- ment to bring more. He proposed that the recommend- tion be struck out. Councillor Norris seconded.,

Councillor Lewis said the matt9r was one which needed careful consideration, and he advised the Coun- cil to proceed cautiously. He suggested that the ques- tion be deferred so that the members might get a little more information on the subject.

Councillor Allen thought it was a very desimble site for such an institution.

Councillor Davies said if this institution belonged to Liverpool why did not they erect it inLiverpool? It would spoil that portion of the Borough for residential purposes. Alderman Timmis asked if this institution were built would not the Council be compelled to put in a sewer. Alderman Quinn said if there was no sewer in a street the residents had the power to apply to the Local Government Board, and if the latter body considered that a sewer was necessary the Council would be com- pelled to do the work. Dealing with the whole subject, he said it was not fair to blame the whole of the boys for the acts of two or three. From information he had received he found that there were only two or three boys implicated in the burning of the Clarence; and they had only been on board a very short time. In res- pect of the period when they were at Mold, he read a letter from Surgeon-Lieutenant-Colonel Williams, medical officer of Mold Urban District Council,in which that gentleman stated that the behaviour of the boys was very good,and he had never heard from anyone that their presence in the town was anything but an acquisi- tion. Mr. Quinn also read a number of letters from gentlemen at Ainsdale showing that the boys in the Ainsdale reformatory were very well behaved, and the school was no detriment to the district.

On a vote being taken the amendment was lost, only the proposer and seconder voting for it. The proposal was therefore approved.

WEDDED MISERY AT RUNCORN.

At Runcorn, on Monday, John Alfred Done, of Percival-lane, was charged with deserting his wife Annie, who asked for a separation under the Married Women's Act. Mrs. Done told the magistrates a sad tale of wedded misery. The parties had been married over five years, and had one little girl,four years of age. They had been separated tivo years ago, and the defen- dant came out of prison in December last, after serving six months for an assault. During that time Mrs. Done had obtained employment at Helsby Wire Works, and in March, in consequence of his continued impor- tunities, she consented to live with him again. They took rooms at his father's house, but after a few weeks the father turned her and the child out, and they took a home in Percival-lane, Mrs. Done's mother providing the furniture. A few weeks ago he ill-used her, and turned her into the street.—The defendant denied on his oath that he turned his wife out.—The bench made an order upon the defendant to pay 10s. per week to the wife and child.

GAOL IN PREFERENCE TO THE WORKHOUSE.

Amy Cunliffe, a young girl, Tth a diminutive baby in her arms,wasicharged at St. Helens on Saturday with ex- posing the child. Sergeant Jackson said the child was aged three months, and was the illegitimate son of prisoner, who lived with her mother at Sutton Heath. At twenty minutes to ten the previous night he found the girl sitting on the doorstep of an empty house in Sutton Heath-road. Her mother and she had been destitute for several weeks. The mother formerly lived in a house at Sutton Heath, and kept a lot of pigs. She was prosecuted for keel:in three without licences, and as she could not, pay the fines she went to gaol. This broke up the home, and during the three weeds since the mother returned from Walton she and this irl had been living on the charity of the neighbours, and when they could not get anyone to take them in they slept in the fields. The child was neither baptised nor vaccinated, and its birth not registered.—The magistrates on the Bench, Mr. J. B. Leach and Dr. DonnellanAinquired why the girl did not go to the workhouse.—Prisoner: I don't want to go to the workhouse.—Sergeant Jackson: She told me last night she would rather die in the road than go to the workhouse. She also told me she wished the child was drowned.—Mr. Leach: You won't go to the workhouse so the court have decided to send you to prison for two months.

A little boy and girl, aged eight and eleven respec- tively, children of Dr. Macneilage, of Queen's Park, Manchester, were on their way to school on Friday when a man jumped off a gipsies' van, and threw them both into the van. About four o'clock on Saturday morning, whilst the gipsies were asleep, the children , crept out of the van somewhere in the neighbourhood 'of Rochdale. They wandered about for the greater part of the day, and at 9.30 in the evening were found near Hebden 'Bridge, over forty miles from Manchester. IThe police were communicated with, and the children were sent back to their parents. .

The Examiner, Saturday, September 12, 1903.

By selecting a year here, and a year there, it is possible to find checks to the expansion of our trade. But 1903 does not seem likely to prove helpful to the pessimists. The August tables, for example, do not indicate that exports are station- ary. They have been steadily growing since the beginning of the year. So far as the general pros- pects of national commerce are concerned, Mr. CHAMBERLAIN has not chosen a propitious season for his agitation. He has two strings to his bow—the Protectionist string and the Prefer- ential string—and neither of, them makes persuasive music. The working man who cannot see why the immense volume of our trada demands the revival of a Protective Tariff is equally uncon- vinced by the assertion that without Preferential Trade with the Colonies we cannot keep the Empire together. There is no reason to believe that this view is widely • entertained in the Colonies. It receives no support from Sir WILFRID LAURIER. No Australian statesman has confirm° it. Mr. SEDDON alone has given it any counten ance. Nobody can pretend that Australia has hailed Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S policy with enthusiasm. The only rational basis on which Colonial Prefer- ence could be established is the reduction by the Colonies of their duties on British goods. Is there even a semblance of Australian opinion in favour of this course? Sir EDMUND BARTON has expressed in general terms his sympathy with Mr. CHAMBER- LAIN'S idea, but he has carefully refrained from approving the only condition on which it can be put into practice. Reciprocity is one of the discoveries MR. CHAMBERLAIN has made since the days when he was a young Radical ; but there seems to be a painful lack of it in his plan for binding the hearts of Australia and the Motherland. The Common- wealth is not exactly eager to welcome our manu- factures. In his Budget statement the Federal Treasurer anticipated for the ensuing year a de- crease in the consumption of imported goods. That may be very satisfactory from the Australian point of view; but our Trades Unionists may be excused for not regarding it as an argument for a Preferential Tariff, constructed on the principle of pure altruism on our part, and exclusive ad- vantage for the Colonies.

WE fear Mr. BAL- . NATIONAL BRAIN FOUR will be pained POWER. by Sir NORMAN

Iburdens. A terribly costly war has proved to us that we have the most expensive and least efficient Army in the world. The expenditure which has made the Navy so formidable we do not grudge; but every reformer perceives the need of subor- dinating the Army to the Navy, instead of main- taining it upon a scale which is out of all propor- tion to necessity and to the national resources. This economy is imperative; without it we cannot undertake fresh expenditure, even of a reproduc.. tive character. Of all the tasks which await a Liberal Administration the most important is the task of curbing waste in the public services. When that is done we may see with satisfaction a good deal of public Toney flowing into the channel to which Sir NORMAN LOCKYER justly calls the national enterprise.

The Commissioners who are to conduct the investigation into the trading transactions of the Corporation have lost no time in making the preliminary arrangements. They met for the first time. on Thursday and an advertisement in another column announces that they will com- mence to sit on Wednesday next. It may there- fore be said that the inquiry is now well within sight. It is impossible to predict how long the Commissioners will be sitting. This must depend largely upon the plan adopted in conducting the investigation. Whatever course is pursued we hope it will be thorough and final. The Com- missioners have been given a free hand,with ample power to get to the bottom of every transaction. Upon them has been cast a great responsibility and we believe they will prove equal to the occasion. This inquiry question has too long vexed the public mind and it is some relief to know that the beginning of the end is now in sight.

An unsuccessful attempt was made at the meet- ing of the Warrington Boari of Guardians to re- open the question of the appointment of a" handy man." At the interim meeting held a fortnight ago, the Guardians sanctioned the appointment of a man, who for the princely salary of £25 per annum and the usual emoluments, is to undertake the duties of a male servant in the hospital, and in his spare time, do" sundry painting, plumbing and corresponding work so often required to be done." The Chairman, on Wednesday, resolutely refused to reconsider the :matter. In doing so, he was acting strictly within his legal rights and it is quite conceivable thaV if members were allowed to re-open matters which had been settled, nothing definite would be arrived at and the work of the Board would come to a standstill. But in the case in point there were exceptional circum- stances which merited further consideration. In the first place such an appointment ought never to have been rushed through on the plea of urgency at an interim meeting,when few Guardians are present. We have heard of the " urgency " plea before, and from quite a different source. Then, again, it would have been much more satis— factory if the position had been advertised ill the usual way. To accept the nominee of any person for a public position savours of exclusiveness and partiality. Not that we have any objection to the person appointed. We believe he will prove a useful servant to the Guardians, and if he fulfils all that is required, we shall consider him badly paid. The Guardians may think they have done a " drefful " smart thing in engaging a man to do tradesmen's work at such a low figure, but we believe they will find it to be false economy in the eod. Instead of being model employers, the Guardians have set a very bad example to other employers of labour, and it is this continual screw- ing down of wages to the lowest possible limit which is responsible, more than anything else, for the numerous calls upon the Guardians for assist- ance from the poor rates.
