THE EXAMINER.

SBPTEMBER 12th 1903.

THE attitude of the MR. CHAMBERLAIN'S Trade s-Unionist LATEST. leaders towards Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S pro- posals does not pleabe him. He has likened them to the old Tories in the days when he was a young Radical. They have learnt nothing; they cling to old shibboleths; they are unwilling to plunge into experiments of what he supposes to be pro- gressive ideas. The obvious answer to this is that Mr. CHAMBERLAIN has taken up an old Tory shibboleth, and is trying to make out that it is an entirely new cry. His preferential trade,he would have us believe, would not be Protection; it would be a new bond between the Mother Country and the Colonies. He may call it by what name he pleases, but the working classes of this country will still perceive that it is a revival of the fiscal system which even the old Tories have discarded. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN is really no further ahead with his progressive ideas: than HIJSKESSON, who re- modelled the old Protective Tariff about the year 1823 on the principle that moderate im- port duties could not harm anybody. Eighty years later the Colonial Secretary, with the air of having made a grand discovery, proposes to be a little more moderate than HusKissoN, by setting up a Tariff which is to show that Free Trade is a worn-out shibboleth. Having prospered exceed- ingly for half a century, the country is to revert to Protection, beginning with the kind of taxation which will be immediately felt in the poorest homes. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN undertakes to enter any working man's household, and prove to the assembled family that they will be just as well off with taxes on bread and meat as they are now. A wholesome scepticism as to this exercise in arithmetic he dismisses as old Tory obstinacy. His Birmingham leaflets, which cover the land like a cloud of locusts, extract from the tables of exports and imports harrowing proofs of our com- mercial decay. The excess of imports over ex- ports is the theme of all the old Protectionist jeremiads. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN says that new condi- tions have arisen which demand new fiscal measures but all this novelty fails to impart much freshness to the arguments of his supporters. We are told that the Cobden Club is hopelessly out of date by writers who do not know that imports are payments for 'exports, and for the services of British shipping, which continues to grow enor- mously. The idea that the excess of imports signifies exhausting drafts upon the capital of the country is one of the delusions which used to make Mr. CHAMBERLAIN smile in the days when he was a young Radical.

LOCKYER'S Address to the British Association at Southport on Wed- nesday. It is usual for the President of that body to open its annual proceedings with a discourse upon the particular branch of science with which he is identified. Sir NORMAN LOCKYER is a dis- tinguished astronomer, but there is not a word in his address about astronomy. He surveys the whole educational outlook for the country, and finds it very bad. There is a lack of scientific training everywhere, especially in Downing-street. Without the scientific spirit we cannot have a proper cultivation of brain-power. Love rules the Court, the camp the grove, says the :poet; but the astronomer is struck by "the frequent absence of the scientific spirit of the nation generally, from the Court to the parish council." He quotes Mr. BALFOUR, who said on one occasion: "I do not believe that any man who looks around the equipment of our Universities, or medical schools, or othßr places of educa- tion can honestly say in his heart that we have done enough to equip research with all the costly armoury which research must have in these modern days." But if the equipment of the Government itself be equally scanty, what can you expect from Universities? If the Government is not the centre of the national brain-power, stimu- lating by its initiative the organised activities of the whole people, how can Prime Ministers say in their hearts that they have done enough to awaken us to our needs? Sir NORMAN LOCKYER recalls the Royal Commission on Science, which reported more than a quarter of a century ago: "The Commission unanimously recommended that the State should be aided by a scientific council in facing the new problems constantly arising." This idea was promptly buried in the archives of fruitless inquiry. The only administrator who has acted upon it is Lord CURZON, and the Indian Government is now assisted by an advisory committee of scientific men. But the Government at home has shown no inclination to recruit its brain-power by the same means. The scientific spirit has not visited the department which provided wrongly-sighted rifles and three boxes of serviceable ammunition when the diplomacy of another department was heading for war.

I If we had a Scientific Committee of the Privy Council, as Sir NORMAN LOCKYER suggests, it is certain that the Government would be urged to make a liberal application of the principle of State- aid to University education. Private effort in this direction has not achieved any signal success. It cannot be compared with the munificence which is employed in the United States for the same object. ,The disparity, indeed, is so great that Sir NORMAN LOCKYER proposes an heroic endeavour on the part of the nation. In 1838 an immense expenditure was undertaken to strengthen the Navy. Let us "do at once for brain-power what we so successfully did then for sea-power." To compete with Germany and the United States we need "eight additional Universities at the very lowest estimate." After careful inquiry, Sir NORMAN LOCKYER reckons the cost at £24,000,000 "for gradually organising, building, and endowing new Universities, and making the existing ones more efficient." How low the present standard of efficiency as fallen may be judged from the con- dition of Oxford, notoriously crippled for lack of funds. Oxford will "continue to be a mere bundle of colleges unless three millions are provided to enable the University, properly so called, to take her place among her sisters of the modern world." According to Sir OLIVER LODGE, £5,000,000 are needed by Birmingham University, for which private liberality has so far done comparatively little. The material advantage of such an outlay Sir NORMAN LOCKYER illustrates by the example of Germany. "I am told that the surn!of £24,000,000 is less than half the amount by which Germany is , yearly enriched by having improved upon our chemical industries, owing to our lack of scientific thit training. :Man other industries have, been attacked in ame way since; but, taking this one instance' e, if we had spent this money ' fifty years ago, when the Prince Consort first called attention to our bacxwardness, the nation would now be much richer than it is, and would have much less to fear from competition." In all this there is undeniable force, but Sir NORMAN LOCKYER would probably be the first to submit that before it can embark upon such a project the nation must be relieved from some of its present

The conduct of gipsies and other persons who laad a nomadic life has been the subject of discus sion and caused muchconoern in various:parts of the country. At Liverpool a child has died from being mauled by a bear belonging to a gipsy gang and from Manchester comes a report of the kidnapping of two children by gipsies. War- rigton with its main roads running North and South and East and West is perhaps as much visited by these wandering tribes as any place in the kingdom. Their presence not only endangers property but is a menace to the health of the community, and it is a relief to see them moving on. The Superintendent of Winwick Asylum has found it necessary to complain of the encampment of gipsies on a piece of ground in Hollins- lane, which was given by the Asylums' Board to !make a road improvement. Mr. Knowles showed the gravity of the complaint when he informed the Council that the gipsies not only encamped there but stopped the use of the cin- dered footpath near the Asylum by putting ropes across in order to pen their horses and other animals in. We have heard of persons finding it necessary to jealously guard their property against gipsies, but it seems thst the gipsies them- selves are equally on their guard against depreda- tors. As their presence is not desired the local police have had instructions to give them marching orders.

Lifeboat Saturday is now an event of the past and Warrington people have once more given striking proof of their willingness to assist deserv- ing causes. It was only on seeing the attractive pageant on Saturday that one realized how en- thusiastically the officials and committees had thrown themselves into the work of preparation. They gave their services freely and ungrudgingly and no doubt felt amply rewarded in knowing that they had been the means of lending a helping hand to the brave lifeboatinen of whom all England is worthily proud. The public showed their appre- ciation by contributing handsomely,and the result is all the more gratifying when it is considered that trade at present is by pc) means brisk. At least £250 will be handed over to the institution and the donation will, we are sure, be accepted as an evidence of Warrington's sympathy with the great life saving work so nobly carried on round the coasts of our sea-girt isle.

Although the street collection in the aggregate was highly satisfactory, it must not be supposed that every spectator was in a generous mood. The inevitable practical jokers found a grand oppor- tunity for playing their little pranks, and at the same time" bluffing" the collectors. When some of the boxes were opened the variety of the con- tents was found to be really astonishing. Bad coins, farthings, medals, buttons, and workmen's checks had been placed in the slots by many who evidently had not the courage to resist the appeal for some contribution. Then there were pieces of tubing, nuts and bolts, -tram tickets marbles, slate pencils, hairpins, and clog_ tips, whilst one bystander had actually been pre vailed upon to present the institution with a portion of a pair of braces. The articles might well be placed in the museum as examples of the generosity of certain Warringtonians in 1903! They form an interesting collection.

Runcorn possesses a unique organization in the shape of a Grappling Corps. It is composed of workingmen who voluntarily undertake the task of recovering the bodies of drowned persons with the aid of special appliances. Last week-end four members of the corps were instrumental in recovering the body of a boy who had been drowned in Sankey Brook at Warrington Their action naturally attracted the attention of Coroner Brighouse, who conducted the subsequent inquest, and his appreciation took the tangible form of a donation towards a fund which has opened with the object of purchasing a new grappling boat. It was intimated that the Corps would be willing at any time to come to Warring- ton when their services were required and the proposal that Warrington should raise 25 to enable them to obtain the necessary boat will, we are sure meet with willing support.

gcA saving of £1 a ton to the Iron industry" write a London correspondent, "sounds almost too geed to be true. Yet this result is promised by 3101 Cohen and Rouse, who have invented a process o c inverting ironsand into iron at a small cost. T1?1 ironsand, of which there are millions of tons 10 different parts of our colonies, particularly on the shores of the St Lawrence in Canada and 14 Taranaki in New Zealand, is composed of particles of iron broken off from huge submarine iron mountains and of sandy quartz. For over half century attempts have been made to turn this iron' sand into iron, but hitherto the cost of separating the iron from the quartz has been too great for cour mercial purposes. The most common scheme vnig ' add to the ironsand sufficient quantities matter to consolidate the whole,but the beiquettee thus produced failed to stand the heat of furnac.es* Imported iron ore is often of such poor qualitt that 271-, tons have to be melted down to make 007 ton of iron. In other words, freight dues have ti° be paid on an extra 150 per cent. This ironsanu, on the other hand, contains 70 per cent. pure Iron, so that dues would have to be paid only ou 90 extra 30 per cent. In the new process the iron: sand is rid of its quartz by being passed throuP a machine containing sEveral powerful magoets• Then the pure iron is mixed with a secret. win' pound and is pressed into briquettes, whicl. ars then put into the furnace and reduced to pig-iron. The whole operation occupies only one hour and.a quarter, and costs roughly is. a ton. If there no flaw in the discovery its importance is obvious.

Secondary School Scholarships. At the Technical Institute yesterday an examination Was held for scholarships, tenable at the Secondary school. The examination was open to children of residents or ratepayers who intend to become pupil teachers in the Borough. There were 60 candidates for the 35 scholarships. Each scholarship is of the value of ;£5 per annum, together with free tuition and books. The examiners were Mr. A. Percival and Mr. J. Simkins, his Majesty's inspectors of schools. The object of the scholarships is to encourage young people to adopt teaching as a profession so that the needs of the Education Committee may be met by local candi- dates. We understand that an effort will be made at an early date to induce local gentlemeri to provide scholarships at the Secondary schools, which may be Won by candidates, independent of any restriction as 'to their becoming pupil teachers. Death of Mr. John Gartland. Another familiar figure in Warrington has been re- Moved by the death of Mr. John Gartland, the care- taker of the Manchester and Liverpool District Bank in Sankey-street, who passed away at his residence O n Saturday. Mr. Gartland, who was 62 years of age, had been unwell for three months, cancer on the stomach being the cause of death. He was an ex- soldier, having served in the Royal Horse Artillery for nearly 24 years. So well did he perform his duties that he rose to the rank of sergeant. He was With the forces in India for 16 years and "ling that period acted as Sergeant-Major. For the Past eleven years the deceased ad been the caretaker of the District Bank and occupied the post with credit to himself and satisfaction to his employers. He leaves a Widow, six sons and five daughters, with whom /Pell sympathy is expressed in their bereavement.— he funeral took place, with military honours, at the kjetnetery on Thursday afternoon. A firing party from Clrford Barracks was in attendance and fired three v.,cd.leYs over the grave. The Rev. Dr. Mercer conducted p,se,service. LOCAL WEDDINGS.

DUNNICO-RATHBONE.

A wedding which aroused considerable interest in Warrington was solemnized at Atherton Baptist Church on Monday afternoon the contracting parties being the Rev. Herbert Dunnico, pastor of the Baptist Church Golborne street Warrington, and Miss Harriet Rathbone second daughter of Mr. Robert Rathbone, contractor, of Atherton. There was a crowded congregation,including some twenty or thirty friends and members of the Warrington congregation,in addition to the following:— Mr. B. Few, Mr. and Mrs. Balmford, Mr. and Mrs. Heywood, Mr. W. Buckby, Mr. H. Buckbv and Miss Birch, Mr. and Mrs. Clatworthy and Mrs. `Wood. The streets adjoining the church were lined with spectators The bride who was given away by her father, was charmingly attired in ivory silk crepe de chine,embroid- ered with Parisian insertion and ribbon. She also wore a beautifully embroidered veil fastened with a spray of orange blossoms, and carried a shower bouquet of white roses and lilies of the valley. Her orna- ments were a gold chain and pendant, the gifts of the bridegroom. The bridesmaids were Miss Lucy. Rath- bone (sister of the bride) and the Misses Cisme and Nellie Dunnico (sisters of the bridegroom). Miss Rathbone was attired in a dress of white muslin, em- broidered with insertion and lace, while the Misses Dunnico had dresses of cream cashmere trimmed with silk, and lace. The bridesmaids whose hats were trimmed with silk, lace and roses, carried bouquets of pink and white roses and wore gold brooches the gifts of the bridegroom. The bride's mother wore a dress of black figured silk, with lace collar and bonnet of pearl sequin, and Mrs. Dunnico (mother of the bridegroom) was dressed in dark blue habit cloth, trimmed with velvet and Parisian insertion, with bonnet to match. They carried streamers of red roses. Mr. J. Welsby of Ratcliffe, was the best man. Principal Bowser, M. A.,of the Midland Baptist Training College, Nottingham, per- formed the ceremony, assisted by the Rev. T. Featon,of Atherton. The hymn 0 love divine and golden" was sung during the service and Mr. Frank Unsworth, at the close, played Mendelssohn's "Wedding March." The reception, which was of a semi-.private character, was held at the Parish Church House, Atherton. The Rev. H. Dunnico during the afternoon received con- gratulatory telegrams from Professor Bumby (Notting- ham), and Dr. Witten Davies (Bangor). Principal Bowser expressed the great pleasure hehad had in being present and conducting, with Mr. Featon, the ceremony. Mr. Dunnico, whom he first knew about four years ago. went to the Midland Baptist College as one of his students, and during his college life he (the speaker) had had no reason to have any other than the highest respect and affectionate regard for him. He proved a diligent student, and made himself very acceptable to the churches in which he conducted a more or less pastoral work, especially the Church at Stanton Hill where he very much endeared himself to a mining population. He wished Mr. and Mrs. Dunnico the highest possible joy in their married life, and the largest measure of prosperity and success in their united service for Christ and the Church._

Mr. Robert Rathbone, in proposing the "Health of the bride and bridegroom," hoped their lives would be one of continued happiness, prosperity and joy. He was extremely pleased to see the representatives from Warrington.

The Rev. T. Featonalso spoke. Mr. Benjamin Few, of VVarrington, expressed on behalf of the Golborne-street Baptist Church, Warring- ton, their %welcome to Mrs.)" Dunnico as wife of the pastor of that church. Mr. Dunnico had lived with them for twelve months now, and they had had a happy time together. They were looking forward to still happier times when he brought his wife amongst them: He was sure Mr. and Mrs. Rathbone would feel proud to receive a son like Mr. Dunnico in their family, and Mr. and Mrs. Minnie() would equally beproudito receive a daughter !like Miss Rathoone in their family. All the congregation at Warrington welcomed them and wished them all prosperity. The Rev. H. Dunnico, responding, said he very much appreciated the kind ramarks which had been made about his wife and himself. He would just content himself with thanking them all for their kind wishes and would propose the " Health of the bridesmaids." Mr. J. Welsby responded. Later in the afternoon the happy couple were given a hearty send-off on the honeymoon tour to Ireland, returning via the Isle of Man. The bride's travelling dress was of blue frieze.

Considerable local interest was taken in the marriage on Tuesday morning at St. Alban's Roman Catholic Church, Bewsey-street, Warrington, of Mr. Thos. P. Rigby, builder, Wilderspool-road, and Miss Mary Fagan, second daughter of Mr. James Fagan, St. Benedict's- avenue, Orford-lane. The Rev. Father Whittle, rector, officiated. The bride, who was given away by her father, wore a white silk dress trimmed with lace, and a white picture hat bedecked with an ostrich feather. She carried a shower bouquet of lilies of the valley. There were two bridesmaids—Miss Katie Fagan (sister) and Miss Mary P. Rigby. (sister of the bridegroom). i The former was attired n pink silk, and Miss Rigby had a blue costume trimmed with cream silk. Each wore a black hat, and had bouquets of marguerites. After a reception, Mr. and Mrs. Rigby left for the lake district, the bride travelling in a green tailor-made costume and black velvet hat. They were the recipients of many choice presents. _ ROYLANCE—LOUGHLIN.

On Tuesday morning at St. Anne's Church, Edgehill, Liverpool, with the full ritual of the Catholic Church. the wedding took place of Mr. Harry Roylance, second son of Mr. Abraham Roylance of Rolleston-street, Warrington, and Miss May Longhlin, the only daughter of Mrs. Loughlin, of Edgehill, Liverpool. The bride who was handsomely attired was given away by her brother, Mr. Tom Loughlin. She was attended by Miss Hill and Miss Mitchelson as bridesmaids. Mr. W. Bate discharged the duties of best man. The bride's mother afterwards gave a reception at her residence. The happy couple afterwards departed for Colwyn Ray. The pre- sents were very numerous. OWEN—POTTER.

The Examiner, Saturday September 12, 1903.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

BURGLARY AT HA YDOCK.

On Thursday there appeared in the dock at Newton- le-Willows Police Court four St. Helens corner boys named John Connolly, Henry Hunter, Richard Kerwick, and John Wayman, on charges of frequenting with intent to commit a felony and with attempting to break into the pawnshop owned by George Gleave, Clipsley- lane, Haydock, on the Ist inst. On the application of the police Wayman was discharged. The evidence showed that the four young men met near St. Helens Station and decided upon " bursting " Gleave's pawnshop. Wayman was watching,whilst the other three attempted to effect an entrance with a key. Sergeant Walker came along the road, whereupon Wayman walked sharply away and the other three hid in a hedge. The officer's suspicions being aroused, he apprehended Wayman, took him back to the pawnshop, and discovered that an en- trance had been attempted. Hunter and Kerwick sur- rendered themselves, and Connolly was apprehended. The three prisoners were sent to gaol for two months with hard labour. Kerwick was then charged with breaking and entering the pawnshop of Peter Grundy, also of Haydock, and stealing silk handkerchiefs and clothing, value £2, early in July. The magistrates con- sidered that the evidence was not sufficiently strong against the defendant, and gave him the benefit of the doubt.

BETTING RAIDS AT EARLESTOWN.

On Thursday, at the Newton-le-Willows Police Court, Mary Unsworth, 40, Fairclough-street, Earlestown, was brought up on remand charged with keeping a betting house. Defendant pleaded guilty.—Mr. A. Browne,who appeared on behalf of the police, stated that defendant had carried on the betting business in the absence of her husband, who was a collier. Evidence having been given, defendant was fined £5 and costs.—Thomas Sulli- van and Garrett Murray, who were found in Mrs. Unsworth's house were bound over for six months in £5 for aiding and abetting. Jane Traverse, of Collin's Green, was also charged with keeping a betting house. She admitted the offence, and was fined -R5 and costs, in all £7 13s. 6d.

ST. HELENS.

DEATH OF MR. E. TURLEY,_ OF ST. HELENS.

Mr. Ernest Turley, of Loanda Villa, Denton's Green, St. Helens, died on Sunday afternoon after a somewhat lengthy illness. The deceased had been in bpsiness, in succession to his father, for many years, and was highly respected. He was only thirty-four years of age, and leaves a widow and four children to mourn his loss. POLICE APPOINTMENT AT ST. HELENS.

Detective Joseph Holding, of .the St. Helens force, has been promoted to the rank of sergeant. The new sergeant has been a member of the force since its insti- tution, and is a very able and popular officer. He will take charge of the Thatto Heath and Sherdley districts. Constable James Curran has been appointed to succeed him as detective.

ALLEGED THEFT AT WIDNES.

On Monday, at Widnes, a middle-aged man named Michael Jennings, 14, Barn-street, was charged with having stolen a silver lever watch, value £6,the property of Ralph Boyle, 14, Frank-street, Widnes. Superintend- ent Keighley stated that on Saturday afternoon the prosecutor went to visit his former landlady at 14, Barn-street, and while there the prisoner sho wed him his watch. The prosecutor also showed the prisoner his watch, taking it off the chain and handing it to him. Just then a missionary called with some tracts, and the prosecutor and the landlady left the room to talk to him. When they came back the prisoner and the watch were missing. He was afterwards arrested by Police- constable Lord at a house in Bridge-street:—The prose- cutor bore out the above statement. In answer to the magistrates, he said the prisoner seemed a decent m and he did not think he intended to steal the watch. He would be much obliged if the bench allowed him tr, withdraw the charge.—The magistrates adopted this course, and discharged the:prisoner with a caution.

NEWTON URBAN COUNCIL.

The Charges for Hospital Patients.

The monthly meeting of the Newton-in-Makerfield Urban District Council was held at the Earlestown Town Hall on Monday, Mr. W. Collingwood presiding. There were also present :—Messrs. Borron' Emmett, Gilmour, Ormand, Thompson, Clarke, Evans, Drs. Patterson and Latham, Mr. C. Cole (clerk), and Mr. A Bowes (sur- veyor). FINANCE COMMITTEE

At a meeting of this committee the Clerk submitted to the committee notice received from Mr. C. F. Jordi- son district auditor, of his intention to commence the audit of the accounts of this Council for the year ended the3lst March, 1903, on Monday, the 21st September

The minutes were adopted. Dr. Latham drewattention to the charges for patients maintained in the infectious hospital. He moved that the minutes be referred _back to committee for farther con- sideration. He submitted that when the fact was put before the ratepayers that there would be a charge in future for the maintenance of patients in hospital it would be detrimental to the health of the township. Mr. Evans seconded, because he thought this was a matter that ought to be thoroughly inquired into. Mr. Clarke hardly thought this was a questidn to be raised in Council. The Council was not a philanthropic institution and could not afford to pay for everybody. It would be a good policy for the Council to pay for those who could not afford, and the Council would make a mistake if they departed from the principle laid down in the minute. Mr. o.rmand thought it had been the practice of the Council to seek for and insist upon payment in all cases where the Council was assured that the payment could be afforded; and he knew of only one case in which payment had been refused. Dr. Latham asked if Mr. Ormand could give the names of every individual in cases that had been pre- viously paid for. The Chairmani ruled the question out of order. Mr. Ormand added that it was absolutely out of order as Dr. Latham 'had no right to make another speech. As a matter of courtesy he could give the names of the people and the amount recovered by the Council in committee, but not in Council. Mr. Gilmour said he was pleased with the commit- tee's recommendation that those who could ought to pay. On the motion being put, Dr. Latham and Mr. Evans were the only members who were in favour of the resolution. At a meeting of the Highways and Sanitary Com- mittee the Medical Officer of Health attended, and reported the receipt of a notification of small-pox in Crown-street, Earlestown, that immediately the case was notified he had taken steps for preventing the spread of the disease, and that he was communicating with small-pox hospitals in the neighbourhood with the view of the woman's immediate removal to such an institution. He also stated that the tents provided by this Council for the isolation of patients suffering from this disease were being erected in case of a further out- break. It was resolved that Drs. Latham and Patter- son and the Medical Officer of Health be empowered to take such steps for the isolation of cases of small-pox as they may deem necessary, and further to engage such medical attendance and nurses, and to incur suchexpen- diture in relation thereto as may appear to them requisite. It was resolved to consider various schemes before the committee in the following order : —Mr. Carnegie's offer, Isolation Hospital, baths anci wash-houses, site for destructor, extension of Town Hall. The minutes were carried.

THE CRICKET CLUB.—The concluding fixture of the season was played at Lymm and ended in defeat for the visitors. Lymm battered first and made a poor display with 43,but their opponents answered with a feebler one their score only totalling Si runs. For the winners, Lowe and Thorpe, bowled very successfully. Thorpe 15 and Marsden 14, contributed most to the score, War- burton, for Grappenhall, played a good innings for his 5, but, unfortunately, no one backed him alp.

The gale and heavy rain of Tuesday night caused a good deal of damage in Lancashire and Cheshire. The Irwell, the Ribble. and other streams overflowed their banks, and, in additionto some flooding of houses, mills were stopped at Clitheroe and Nelson by the water invading the engine rooms. The crops also suffered. In North Wales considerable. damage is reported. Rail. way traffic between Bala and the coast has been interrupted. Bala itself and other places have been flooded, and losses of sheep are reported from several districts.

===== 0002601_19030912_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FIGHT FOR CHEAP FOOD FOR THE PEOPLE.

BY ALFRED EMMOTT, M.P.

"If you are to give a preference to the Colonies . . . you must put a tax on food." • So said Mr. Chamberlain in the House of Commons on May 28th; and on June 26th, speaking at the Constitutional Club, he added: "A system of Preferential Tariffs is the only system by which this Empire can be kept together." I am asked to write on "Cheap Food for the People." Let me establish :

(1) The very elementary point that import duties on food in a country like this must raise the price of food by at least the amount of the duty. (2) That the taxing of corn and meat with a prefer- ence to our own Colonies will cost the country far more than it will bring into the Exchequer. (3) That wages do not rise and fall with the price of bread; indeed, that they are more likely to vary inversely. (4) That the condition of the mass of the people in other protected European nations is much worse than in this country. Myfirst position is that import duties on articles of food which we import largely must, in the long run, raise the price of those articles by qkt least the amount of the duty. Let us deal with the matter theoretically and practically. Theoretically the price of wheat in the homemarket is the price of that portion of it which is most costly to produce, and for which there is a demand. We import more than three-fourths of the wheat we use, and the price is set from abroad for all practical pur- poses. If it falls, our farmers produce less ;if it rises, they produce more. The governing factor for many years has been the price in the United States, plus the cost of bringing it over here. The effect of an import duty in such a market as ours would vary according to its amount. If it were comparatively a small duty it would not lead to any great increase of wheat-growing in this country. The price, therefore, would remain the cost price, allowing for usual profits. of the portion mostdifficult to produce abroad which comes to this market, plus the cost of carriage and the duty. If it were a large duty the effect would ' be somewhat different, but it is needless to pursue that subject, for no one suggests a large duty. It is said that part, if not the whole, of the duty would be borne by exporting countries. That may be possible, in a falling or stagnant market, in the case of a small duty suddenly imposed, and during a very few weeks or months after its imposition. Otherwise the position is unthinkable, for what does it mean if true? It means that by putting a 2s. duty on corn here we immediately lower the price of all the wheat in the United States by a similar amount. There cannot,be two prices for the same article in the same market: so if the foreigner pays any small duty we put on here, he must take a lower price than he otherwise would sell at in his own market, not only for what he sells for export, but also for what he sells at home. If that were the case, and the price were lowered below the cost of pro- duction of the portion of -wheat most difficult to produce, then a less acreage would be put on to wheat until the price rose again to a Jiving figure.. So much for theory. Practically, the matter is simpler to deal with. Francois almost self-supporting as regards wheat. There ia no reason, therefore, why the price of wheat should be materially higher than it is in this country, even though there is a heavy import duty. What are the facts, however? The price in France is regularly and persistently higher to a great degree, and recently an answer given in the House of [ Commons showed that the price in France was I 13s. 20. per quarter higher than our price, although I the )duty was only 12s. 20. Mutatis Tautanclis, I the result in Germany is the same. Not many years ago Germany exported wheat, and whether there was an import duty or not the price was often lower there than in this country. Of late years Germany has imported wheat,and the price is practically the same price as in this country, plus the import duty of 7s. 7d. per quarter. I might go on with Italy and i other importing countries, but it s unnecessary. I may point out, however, that we import from abroad SO enormously greater a proportion of the wheat we con- sume than do France or Germany, that, all the more as compared with them, we, and not the exporting , country, must pay any duty we impose. I Having, now shown theoretically and practically, why import duties on di-tides of food which we import largely must, in the long run, raise the prices of those articles by the amount of the duty, let me deal with the second point—that the taxing of corn and meat, with a preference to our own Colonies, will cost the country I far more than it will bring into the Exchequer. Roughly speaking, the amount of wheat and flour produced in this country and imported from our own possessions is three-eighths of the whole. If we put a tax on wheat and flour from foreigncountrie.s, we shall raise the price per quarter at least as much as the tax; but the Ex- chequer receipts from the tax will only be live-eighths of the extra cost due to the price being raised. Thus, supposing we retain for home consump- tion 62,500,000 cwt. of foreign wheat and flour, the amount received by the Exchequer, if the tax were Gd. per cwt., would be £1,562,500 but the amount that the country would pay owing to the increase of price would be £2,500,000. In reference to meat, a greater proportion is raised at home or imported from our own possessions. The effect of a tax would be to raise the price in accordance with the tax, but the Exchequer would not receive more than one- half of the extra amount the country would have to pay. This is why it is impossible for Mr. Chamberlain to compensate for the increased cost of bread, "by a proportionate decrease in some other articles I either of consumption or that are necessary for the comfort of life." All our tea, sugar and tobacco come from abroad. Any tax put upon them is paid upon every pound which is consumed in the country. The basis of taxation cannot be adjusted by transferring taxation from tea, sugar, and tobacco Ito corn and meat without its costing the country a great deal more than the Exchequer will receive. 1 Turning to the third point, that wages do not rise and fall with the price of bread—indeed, that they are much more likely to vary inversely with the price of bread—l may again treat the question theoretically and practically. Theoretically, the argument is that man must eat to live, and that when his income is limited, and food is dear, a greater proportiOn of his income must go in payment. for, food and a less amount for rent, clothes, furniture, travelling, and luxuries. When the price of food rises, therefore, the working man has a less margin to spend on these other items, and that must prejudicially affect the general trade of the country. _

So muclifor theory; now for practice. lam content, to shelter myself behind the authority of "An Econom- ist," who has recently been writing to the Times in support of Mr. Chamberlain's views. In his third article he says "I should maintain that, ecteris paribus, the chances are that in modern England a tax on the neces- saries of life, considered as an isolated expedient, would in no case be followed by an increase of wages propor- tionate to the tax. The economic history of England does not give much support to the view that a rise of wages necessarily follows an increase in the cost of living." So much for our modern economist. My next quotation is over sixty years old. On February 24th 1842, Cobden, speaking in the House of Commons, said: "Do you think that the fallacy. of 1815, which I heard put forthso boldly last week, that wages 'rose and fell with the price of bread, can now prevail in the minds of working men after the experience of the last three years? Has not the price of bread been higher during that time than for any three consecutive years for the last twenty years? And yet trade has suffered Ogreater decline in every branch of industry during this period than in any preceding three years." There is no doubt, I think, of the facts in this case, and undoubtedly they had an im- portant effect onthe mind of Peel, and greatly increased the doubts he was beginning to feel of the efficacy of Protection.

Finally, on this branch of the subject, let me appeal to the history of the last thirty years. Prices of food- stuffs, and of commodities in general, have fallen during tbat period=-more particularly down to 1895—yet wages on the whole have materially increased. Whoever has heard during that period of a demand for an increase of wages being based on an increase of the cost of food. On the whole, demands have been made, and have been enforced, on account of good trade and general pros- perity. Ido not know of a single instance where a plea has been entered on account of the cost of food.

The last consideration I mentioned was anall-import-1 ant one from the standpoint of the working classes the relative condition of the mass of the people in Other European countries I say " European Countries" advisedly, for obviously there can be no , comparison between an old country like ours,l whose exports must be preponderating manufactured goods, and a new country like the United States, whose 'exports are principally foodstuffs and raw material. A ' much fairer comparison can be drawn between this country and France or Germany than between this country and the United States. leave France out of account, because no one suggests that France is forging rapidly ahead at our expense. All our fears and jealousies, so far as Europe is concerned, are centred on Germany, and if I can shew that the condition of the mass of the people in Germany is far inferior to that of the mass of the people in this country, it will not be necessary to make a comparison with any other European nation. Germany's great advance in the export of I manufactured goods is not disputed, but it is a very I much more doubtful question whether it can continue. A desperate effort has been made in that country to regulate the prices for the home market by syndicates and cartels, a.nd to sell any surplus product abroad, even at a loss, in order to keep their manufactories at work. One extreme case is quoted by Consul-General Oppen- heimer, in his report of the trade of the Consular dis- trict of Frankfort-on-Main for the year 1902, of the syndicate of German wire tack manufacturers who are said to have made a profit of £60,000 in six months in their home market and suffered a loss of £43,000 on ' their export trade. What is the result of all this ? It has not yet had a sufficiently extended trial to enable us to judge accurately. This much we know. Up to 1900 there was a period of inflated credit. Then came an inevit- able collapse with the failure of the Leipziger Bank. No such collapse occurred here. Since that time Ger- many has suffered severely from depression at home,

althengh her export trade has been maintained. She has, in fact, been selling her goods abroad at any price obtainable, whether it shewed a profit or loss. Consul- General Schwabach, in his report on the trade of Ger- many for 1902, says: "It must not lbe forgotten that the flatness of the whole market led to an export trade, which was in many cases unremunerative, to use no stronger expression." Now, Germany is not strong enough, financially, to cultivate a permanently unre- munerative export trade for the sake of her home manu- facture. The more her foreign trade is pushed at a loss, the more her people at home sufferfrom the high prices charged by the syndicates in Germany. Last year she constructed a new tariff at the behest of the agrarians. It is not yet put into force. A Government Commission has been appointed to investigate the effect of trusts, syndicates, and cartels on the welfare of Germany. A " grand inquest" of a much more scientific character than ours is going on there. This very fact shows that all is not well with them.

The Gorman factory inspectors give a depressing account of the effect on wages and employment of the slump in trade which has followed the crisis of 1900. In the Berlin factory district an increase in 1902 of 10,150 unemployed—four-fifths adult men—and a large number on short time. At Essen 12,000 applicants for 4,000 posts. In Westphalia "playshifts "and decrease of wages. In the Reichenbach district "the standard of life of the working classes has been worsened." In the machine industry of the Chemnitz district the male workpeople decreased from 23,719 in 1900 to 20,799 in 1902. In the Grand Puchy of Baden, restriction of out- put and reduction of wages, and so on, and so on. What is the position of the German workman at present as compared with that of English operatives? Probably the worst-paid English labourer is the farm servant. His wages vary in different parts of the country, but they probably average fully £43 15s. a year. In a typical German province the wages are £25 7s. for a man, £5.105.. for his wife, and £5 for two . .

children, or £35 17s. in all, compared N.vith the £43 15s. earned by the man alone in England. It is the same with other classes of labour. Sir John Brunner tells us the average daily wage of a worker in alkali manufac- ture is 22 per cent, less in Germany than in England. Turners, fitters, smiths, joiners, patternmakers, railway porters, shunters, pointsmen, locomotive firemen all earn from 20 per cent. to 40 per cent. less in Germany than in England. As they earn less, so their standard of living is lower. Wheaten bread is a luxury to them, for the price of wheat is higher through the duty. They eat black bread instead of white, and less meat, and much of what they do eat the English working man would turn from in disgust. My point is proved. We are asked to imitate Ger- many. The great expansion of German trade has led to widespread depression there, while our condition has remained eminently satisfactory. We still eat as much wheat-flour and meat and tea. and sugar,, smoke as much tobacco, and drink as much beer as ever. We shall want a good deal of persuasion to make us change to a diet of black bread and horseflesh, and a home market kept at an artificially high figure by import duties. ALFRED EMMOTT.

[This article forms one of a series which we have arranged with leading members of the Eighty Club to contribute to our columns. Book copy-right strictly resesved. The next article will be by Mr. Corrie Grant, M.P., on "EDUCATION PROBLEMS."]

AMATEUR OPERA IN WARRINGTON.

Although no public announcement of the fact has hitherto been made it has been pretty generally known that one of the Gilbert-Sullivan operas has for some time past been in rehearsal by a body of local lady and gentlemen amateurs. The project is the outcome of effort made by a subcommittee of The Warrington Musical Society with the object of aiding the society's funds and we believe the idea has been taken up most enthusiastically. The opera chosen is " The Yeomen of the Guard," the finest perhaps of the many happy joint productions of a celebrated librettist and a renowned composer, and, as will be seen from a preliminary announcement in our advertising columns, four grand stage performances are to be given in the Parr Hall in the third week in October. The staging, the costumes, the chorus and the orchestra will, we understand, be on a most complete and elaborate scale, full advantage being taken of the recent thorough adaptation of the Hall for theatrical purposes. In this connection, it may be interesting to state that the en- larged stage at Parr Hall will give a 24 feet opening in the proscenium front with 25 feet depth backwards. Messrs Le Maistre St Co. the ',ell-Imown theatrical ex- perts, who are reconstructing the proscenium and stage arrangements for the Warrington Corporation, will supply a fine background scene depicting the Tower of London for the purposes of "The Yeomen of the Guard" rehearsals for which,—commenced in the Spring and carried on far into July—were resumed after the holi- days on Tuesday last. The whole of the principal parts and the chorus will be undertaken by members of the Musical Society. The orchestra will also be entirely amateur under Nir. R. H. Pearson's leadership. The musical training of principals,chorus and orchestra is,we need hardly say, in the competent hands of Mr. F. H. Crossley,vrhilst the necessary dramatic instruction is being given by an expert of some standing—Mr. E. Thornley- Dodge of Manchester. By the courtesy of Mr. J. New- combe,the hon. secretary and treasurer to the'opera com- mittee, we are Mlle to append the cast which is as follows THE YEOMEN OF THE GUARD.

• DRA.M ATIS PERSONX. Sir Richard Cholmondeley Mr. 3. W. Bibby Colonel Fairfax Sergeant Meryll Mr. F'. Fieldhouse Leonard Meryll Jack Point Wilfred Shadbolt Elsie Maynard Mrs. Arthur Booth Pho3be Meryll Miss Mabel Henshall Dame Carruthers Kate We understand that the chorus numbers about forty and that the orchestra will include some twenty instrumentalists. The rehearsals in the Cairo-street Schoolroom are being gone through with an enthusiasm which augurs well for a successful and artistic present- ment of the opera and we do not doubt that the public support of so novel yet welcome an enterprise will prove alike hearty and encouraging.

ANOTHER WARRINGTON BOY DROWNED.

The Jury's Recommendation.

Another sad drowning case has to be reported and again the victim is a boy. On Thursday afternoon Frederick Curtis Bennie, the seven year old son of Mr. Frederick T. Bennie, contractor, 80, Liverpool-road,was drowned in the Mersey near Messrs. Monks, Hall and Company's:works, and his body was not recovered until a quarter to eight in the evening. The inquest was con- ducted at the Police Station yesterday afternoon by Mr. F. A. Jones, deputy coroner. Mr:Sennie told the jury that he last saw his son alive at eight o'clock on Thursday morning when he went out to play. Later in the day he heard that the boy had been drowned, and in the evening the body was recovered from the river.

Thomas Robert Chambers, nine years of age, 10, Dale- street, stated that he was playing with the deceased by the riverside on Thursday afternoon. They were play- ing at "stonies" when deceased's cap blew off and fell into the water. Deceased went down some steps lead- ing to the water's edge and tried to reach the cap but he slipped and fell into' the river and disappeared in a short time.

Frederick William Dutton, 162, Liverpool-road, stated that he recovered the body at 7.45 in the evening. The jury returned a verdict of "Accidentally drowned" and expressed sincere sympathy with the parents in their sad bereavement. The foreman added that they were of the opinion that somethiag should be done to prevent such fatalities in the future. At present there was no barrier between the roadway and the river audit was an easy matter for children to fall into the water. The river wall was so high and of such a length at that point that a good swimmer would find great difficulty in getting out of the water and the jury thought that lifebelts or similar appliances should be provided. The Deputy Coroner said that if the jury's recom mendation were conveyed to the Corporation something would no doubt be done.

===== 0002601_19030912_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
Fire at Winwick.

At six o'clock on Wednesday evening an outbreak of fire occurred at Aubery Farm, Winwick, which is occupied by Mr. Birchall. The Warrington Fire Brigade was informed and a number of men turned out under the direction of Deputy Superintendent Turner, with a steam engine. On arrival it was found that a stack consisting of about 40 tons of hay was ablaze. The firemen succeeded in preventing the flames from .spreading to the farm buildings but the stack was practically destroyed.

MODERATE IN PRICE.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

IRugby Football.

Warrington have started well. To obtain a double brace of points in the first match is a good beginning According to a Yorkshire contemporary Warrington have already qualified for " top place "by beating Leigh. There seems no reason why Warrington should not "top " last year's position in the League. The team which donned the" primrose and blue , ' jersey on Saturday hardly played up to expectations but unless something unfor-seen happens they should 'ere long, fully justify the good im- pression which has evidently been made in Yorkshire. They will have a splendid opportunity of showing their mettle to-day, when they visit Hull. Two ad- ditional points at the expense of the "third-porters " would indeed be a promising start for the 1903-4 season.

The meeting of Warrington and Leigh is a time- honoured fixture and although Leigh is not one of the best teams to'draw spectators to the Wilderspool en- closure, no complaint could be made as to the dimen- sions of the "gate" on Saturday. A good number journeyed from Leigh and helped to swell the pro- ceeds which amounted to £llO. This totai is exclusive offmembers' subscriptions of which a large number were received. The steady stream of spectators wending their way along Wilderspool Causeway about three ;o'clock on Saturday was, in itself, evidence of the commencement of the football season and a striking proof that interest in the game has been but lying dormant during the past four months. The enaosure looked at its best. The heavy rain which was experienced in the morning had made the turf a little heavy-going, but the grass has grown wonderfully well since last April and many clubs would consider themselves fortunate if they were able to possess such an excellent piece of turf as that at Wilderspool.

Neither side was able to place the best possible fifteen in the field. The unfortunate accident which befel Harris will prevent him from occupying his accustomed place on the right wing for some weeks and the three-quarter line hardly looked the same without him. In Brooks the committee selected a capable substitute, especially when it comes to tackl- ing, but he has not the initiative or scoring ability of Harris. It is almost safe to say that at least one more try would have been registered on Saturday if Harris had been given the pass which Brooks fumbled in the I second half of the game. Then on the Leigh side there was also a notable absentee. Burgess instead of figuring in the Leigh half-back division- was watching the game from the pavilion end, la,m9nt ing his inability to play owing to an injured knee which he sustained at St. Helens in the first minute of the game on the previous Tuesday. This was his only opportunity of playing on the Wilderspool enclosure this season and one cannot but sympathise with him in his ill-luck.

Isherwood lost the toss, and Bennett, the Leigh captain, wisely chose to play with the wind and sun behind his men in the first half. Warrington made a good start,and it looked as though something tangible had been scored when one of the forwards grounded the ball under the post, from a cross-kick by Fish. The referee, however, had a different opinion and a scrimmage was ordered. Then Leigh got down to the Warrington end and a beautiful movement between Bennett and Johnson ended in the last-named player evading Brooks and • registering the first try. At this early success, the Leighites were greatly pleased and visions of points in the Northern and West Lancashire Leagues crossed their minds. As if to add a little more sting to the visi- tors attack Molyneux made an attempt to drop a goal but it went wide and the home spectators breathed freely. At half time the visitors were leading by a try to nil. On turning round it was confidently antici- pated That the Warrington men would soon wipe off the arrears. But some time elapsed before this happy condition of affairs came about. In misfielding the ball Hallam almeist let the visitors in, but Fish came to the rescue and saved in characteristic fashion by a run and kick into touch.

Delays were caused by the new rule abolishing a penalty for " feet-up " and it was with difficulty that the ball could he placed in the scrimmage. There were loud complaints from the spectators and unless some steps are taken to modify the privileges allowed to the scrummagers I am afraid many a good match will be completely sn3ilt. lt was evident from the rough horse play which was taking place in the scrim- mage that the players themselves were being punished. I hear there wera loud complaints in the dressing-room afterwards and it is a pity the authors of the new regulations were not present to hear them.

But to return to the match. Davies showed great perseverance in getting the ball into the scrummage, and did not despair if he was daunted once, twice, thrice, or even four times in getting it to the feet of the players. Several passing movements by the War- rington backs failed to pierce the Leigh defence, and matters were beginning to lolk ominous for the home team until the last quarter of an hour was entered. upon. Then a wonderful change came o'er the scene. A movement initiated by Davies, and taken up by Isherwood and Fish, resulted in the last-named open- ing his scoring ace-flint and registering the first try for Warrington. From this point to the close War- rington played in irresistible fashion. Another score came unexpectedly from the same trio; only on this occasion Fish was unable to complete the movement, and finding his way barred, he passed back to Isher- wood, who forced his way over the line amid a crowd of opponents. Hallatn added the major points by a well-judged place kick. This completed the scoring and redeemed the match from the common place.

Judged as a whole,the Warrington team is a stronger combination than last year's. The men were not Seen at their best on Saturday and I hope to see an improvement in to-day's play. The introduction of Cook (Birkenhead) and Thomas (Newport) has made a wonderful improvement in the forward ranks. When the forwards have become a little more accustomed to each other and combine better in their play they should compare favourably with the best in the league. At half-back it can hardly be said that either Davies or Edmunds t howed to advantage. Davies was much the better man on the dav's play and served the three-quarters with the ball most unselfishly. Greater things may be expected of him after a little more practice with his new colleagues. Edmunds had an " off" day and did little. Many are longing to see Bob Bate given a trial at half-back with Davies and it is a desire which the committee might certainly gratify for once in a way at any rate. In the three-quarter line Fish gives promise of playing up to his great reputation, With Isherwood as his partner from the commence. ment of the season there is no reason why previous records may not be broken. Both players combined together splendidly and the two tries scored were red.]. beauties. Dickenson put in seine good individual work and gave Brooks a chance on one occasion to get through, but he failed to hold the ball. Hallam made one mistake at the opening of the second half but he may be said to have redeemed himself by kicking a 'goal from a difficult angle.

The Leigh team was beaten by a superior three- quarter line. The forwards were evenly matched, and if anything, the visitors had the advantage at half-back liolyneux being a tower of strength to his side. He had a capable assistant in Hiltdn who filled the vacancy caused by the injury to Burgess. Bennett and Johnson figured the most prominently in the three- quarter line.

For to-day's match at Hull, ilie committee have appointed practically the same team as won the Leigh match. The only alteration is the substitution of Harmer for Walter Swift in the forward panks. The teams are as follows :

Warrington.—Hallam, back; Fish, Isherwood, Dickenson and Brooks, threectuarter backs; Edmunds and Davies, half-backs; Boardman, Morrison, F. Swift, Thomas, Naylor, Cook, Harmer and Edmond- son, forwards.

Hull.—Rogers, back; Parry, Morgan, Cook and. Lewis, threequarter backs Hall and Goddard,"half- ;backs ; Lewis, Harrison, Longborn, Fulton, Ellering- ton, Sowerby, Carlisle and Rhodes, forwards.

It will be noted that there are several new names in the Hull team. In the first'place Taylor, the county full back, is an absentee and it can scarcely be ex- pected that Rogers is his equal at full back. In the three-quarter line, Lewis takes Moxon's accustomed place on the win tt. Franks, who played an excellent game at Warrington last year,.is also left off for Hall, a new player hailing from Gloucester. Goddard is also a Gloucester man, so that there will be a Glou- cester pair behind the Hull pack. The long journey of course, tell on the Warrington men but if they

5

put their backs into their work they should be more. than a match for the home team. Warrington have brought back points from Hull on previous occasions and a determined effort is all that is needed to-day to repeat the performance.

The "A" team will play their first match of the season at Wilderspool when St. Helens " will be the visitors. All who are interested in the encourhgement of good junior (rugby) clubs will be pleased to hear that a meeting has been held of the Secretaries of the local Works Clubs with the object cif forming a works' league in Warrington this season, The Vrarrington Club has promised to provide medals for the winning team. A committee of the following representatives of clubs has been formed: Mr. Phillips (Rylands), Mr. Clegg (drosfield's), Mr. Harper Mersey White Lead), Mr. Darlington (Cable), Mr. Powney (Walker's), Mr. Buck (Naylor's) and Mr. Leigh (Locos.) The clubs mentioned have practically consented to join the league and applications are invited from others. Another meeting is to be held on the 22ad inst. and I hope the committee will then be able to form a league and get to work without delay.

It will be seen from theproceedings of the Lancashire County Union Committee that the Wilderpool enclosure is to be the venue of the County trial match on October 12th. After the match a team will be picked from the possibles and probables, to represent the Red rose in the match with Cheshire on October 28th at Broughton.

I have received a copy of Hr. Frank Sugg's football annual which is full of interesting information more particularly for the lovers of the Association code. There are several admirable photographs of football teams and well-known players, and the booklet, which contains the principal fixtures for the season is a' capital-pennyworth.

The Athletic News Football Annual is also to hand and is quite equal to its predecessors. The Guide contains the results of last season, and all the official fixtures of various Leagues for the campaign about to commence, lists of international players, the new laws of the Association game, and much more valuable information. The secretaries' directory will be found more extensive than ever.

Last week we announced that Messrs T. Birchall,J.P A. J. Jolley and L. G-reening—three gentlemen appoin- ted:by therouncil to act as Commissioners at the forth coming inquiry—had intimated to the Mayor their willingness to act in that capacity. Early this Week, the non-trading • committee notified the appoint- ment of their three commissioners to the Mayor. The three gentlemen selected are: .Mr. Norbury Williams, Mr. J: Pemberton and Mr. A. Horrocks. A preliminary meeting of the Commisioners was held at the Town Hall on Thursday.% All the Coin- missioners were present. It was decided that the first sitting should be held on Wednesday next at the Town Hall, commencing at eleven o'clock. An advertise- ment to this effect ,appears in another column. Any person wishing to give evidence or supply information in resp:,‘ct of any trading transactions of the Corpora- tion between April Ist, 1890, and March 31st; 1903, should send a request in a sealed envelope addressed to the Commissioners at the Town Hall. We under- stand that the Commissioners intend to sit three days in each subsequent week until they have completed their investigations. The inquiry will, of course, be conducted in private. A shorthand writer will be present and take a verhitim note.

THE WRITER OF OUR NEXT "SPECIAL."

Mr. Corrie Grant. M.P., who will write our special article next week, is the son of a Suffolk gentleman who suffered imprisonment rather than pay the obnoxious Church rate. He was educated at the City of London School, and was called to the Bar in 1877.

In July, 1895, Mr. Grant undertook on behalf of the Liberal party one of the last fights under the old corrupt system of Parliamentary voting. This was a by-election opposing the return of Lord Rindolph Churchill for Woodstock after his appointmcmt as Secretary of State for India. It took place a month or two before the Reform Act came into force at the General Eleation of that year, but WoOdstock maintained the old tradition and returned Lord Randolph again. Less than 1,000 polled! So good a candidate did Mr. Grant prove that in 1892 the Liberals of West Birmingham adopted him as their candidate to oppose Mr. Chamberlain. Mr. Chamberlain returned the compliment by going out of his way to visit Rugby and speak against Mr. Grant during the Khaki Election of 1900. This, hOwever, did not prevent Air. Grant ousting the Tory and transferring the seat to the Liberal side. _ _

In London our contributor is-exceedingly well-known for his 'excellent work in fighting the reactionary clerical party on the London School Bo3rd. In this he has received splendid support from his wife, who was a Newnham girl, and is an able speaker ; she herself has held a seat on the Chiswick School Board. Mr. Grant has always been a staunch support& of the School Board system, and is known to sympathie very strongly with the Nonconformists in their hostility to the present Education Act.

Fire in the Market-Place. At about eight o'clock on Wednesday night the Fire Brigade was summoned to the Market Place. It appears that Mr. F. W. MerryweatheK's stall of general drapery was illuminated by means of a paraffin lam.p suspended on a rope. The rope broke and the lamp fell:on to the stall igniting the articles. Superinten- dent Turner and a number of firemen turned out with the reel. Water was obtained from a hydramt, an& the flames wgre soon extinguished.

THE BENEFITS OF SCIENCE.

- It is seldom that any discovery of science is such as will benefit all classes of people. Some of the greatest discoveries of the age, while they have proved directly oeneficial to thousands, have also, at least for a time, I injured other thousands. Even some of the best medi- cinal preparations however much they may do to remove one class of disease, or suit some constitutions, at the ',same time either deepen the hold of another disease, or leave it entirely untouched, or if they benefit one class of patients, they may injure another. i Now the great need of the age s some scientifically arranged preparation which will cope effectually with the prevalent diseases of this country, which will be certain to do good when fairly tried, which will be equally adapted to the needs of the mer- chant and the workman, the professional man, and he who wins his bread by the sweat of his brow, the student, the clerk, the factory hand, the miner, and the roadside labourer. It should be, too, such a preparation a contains no injurious ingredients, and which may be taken With impunity by the weakly child, or the delicate lady, as well as by the stronger constituted man. Such a discovery would deserve to be called The Perfection of Medicinal Preparations, and would indeed be an Invaluable Boon to Suffering Humanity. Now this much needed boon has been found, has been tried, has been proved, and wherever proved, it has been found satisfactory. We refer to Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters, the Vegetable Tonic, which when once tried, has been always recommended, and has proved successful when all other medicine has failed to give rerief, and we may say further, that it has proved permanently beneficial, when other pre- parations at best only gave temporary- relief. It is strongly recommended as The Best Remedy of the Age for Indigestion in its different forms, such as Sick Headache, Pains in the Side Giddiness, Loss of Appe- tite, also for Nervousness and Nervous Disorders, Sleep- lessness, Neuralgia, Low Spirits and all kinds of Weakness. It has often proved very beneficial to persons suffering from great Weakness either after an illness, long oonfinement to ill-ventilated rooms or any other cause. It strikes at the source of the Disease, removing the cause of the illness, and strengthens those parts as the system which have been weakened by it, and therefore most liable to colds and other ailments. Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters being purely vegetable, it is a Natural Tonic, and suited to all ages at all seasons of the year. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

TRAM PASSENGER'S DISGRACEFUL CONDUCT,

At the Warrington Police Court on Monday. Isaac Smith, 48, Plumpton-street, was summoned for having made use of obscene language on a tramcar on Saturday August 29th.—Mr. Stanley Wilson prosecuted on behalf of the Corporation and said the defendant boarded the 9.10 p.m. car forSlnkey:Briciges at the Post Office. All the seats were occupied and defendant stood up. When the car reached Braznhall-street he fell on to the knees of a lady passenger. The checker thereupon told him to take hold of the straps in the car. Defendant then turned round and made use of most obscene and offensive language, and followed the checker to the back of the car. The conductor stopped the car and defend- ant then got hold of him by the arm and pushed him off Ihe car and afterwards tried to butt him in the face. In Consequence of his disgraceful conduct the car was delayed for three minutes.—Mr. Monks: Is it the practice of the conductors to allow men to stand inside the car ?—Mr. Wilson: It is done.—Checker Doran and conductor Baxendale bore out Mr. Wilson's statement, and Smith, who made his 23rd appearance, was fined 20s.

A TERROR TO PUBLICANS.

James Hopkins, who is well known, to the police appeared before the magistrates at the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday charged with having been drunk and disorderly in Winwick-street, on Monday evening, also with assaulting John Berry, landlord of the Central Hotel, and with damaging, to the amount of 17s. 6d., a copper kettle, a shirt, and a watch and chain belonging to .Mr. Berry.—Prosecutor stated that at ten minutes to eight on Monday night prisoner went into the Central Hotel, drunk. Cie was requested to leave several times, but refused, and eventually witness ejected him. Ten minutes later prisoner returned and threw a piece of slate at witness, which just missed his face. It however struck a copper kettle and damaged it to the extent of 7s. 6d. He (prosecutor jumped over the bar, and there was a struggle between him and the prisoner, during which witness had his watch chain broken and his shirt torn. Prisoner said he would "swing" for him. A hostile crowd tried to rescue the prisoner.—P.C. Chrimes stated that on the way to the Police Station Hopkins said: "I will do for him yet, if I have to go to the gallows for it."—The Chief Constable said this was prisoner's 30th appear- ance, his last conviction being in March, when he had three months' imprisonment for assaulting the police. He was an habitual station loafer, and he and his brother were terrors to publicans. If a publican would not serve them it was almost certain that he would be assaulted sooner or later. They were desperate characters.—The Bench sentenced prisoner to three months' hard labour.

Warrington Borough Police Court.

WARNING TO FISH DEALEns.—James McNally, 99, Bewsey read was summoned under Section 13 of the Markets and Fairs Clauses Act. 1847. for having exposed fish for sale in Crosfleld street without having paid the toll.—Mr. Stanley Wilson, deputy town clerk,prosecuted and said that McNally had that morning expressed his regret for his conduct, and promised to take; out the necessary license at once. He, therefore asked for the case to be dismissed on payment of costs. At the same time it was thought proper to bring the case, forward as a warning to others,who seemed to be of opinion that the tolls levied under the Act of 1854 could not be en- forced. He wished to make it known that if there were any other cases of the same kind he would press for the full penalty of 40s.—The case was accordingly dismissed on payment of the costs (3s 6d). Daresbury Petty Sessions.

TUESDAY.—Before Messrs. W. Long (presiding), Henry Nield, and Roger Parr. ASSAULT AT GRAPPENHALL.—John Burns and Elizabeth Burns,Canal-side,Grappenhall,were summoned for assaulting a farm labourer named JohnCragban.—Mr. Crompton represented the complainant and stated that Craghan was going on a message for his master when he was met by three men and a woman. One of the men said that Craghan had set his brother against him, and he would have to fight. Craghan said he would not, whereupon two of the men held his arms while the first man and the woman struck him in the face. He was pulled on to the ground and kicked on the shoulder, and in consequence he could not follow his employment properly for some days afterwards.—Burns denied that he challenged complainant and said the latter had been "saying things about him."—A witness named McDonald stated that complainant asked Burns to have a fight and Mrs. Burns and witness only tried to separate them.— The defendants were bound over to keep the peace for six months.

FORMER GAMEKEEPER FlNED.—Samuel Maddocks, Greenhill's-common, Lower Whitley, was charged with trespassing in search of game on land at Lower Whitley. He pleaded guilty.—Mr. Draper (Messrs. R. Davies and Co.), solicitor, prosecuted, and said that an under- keeper of Sir Gilbert Greenall's,named Edward Beckett, was on duty at Crow's Nest Wood, Lower Whitley, of which Sir Gilbert was the owner, and had the sporting rights of, on August 20th, and saw six snares set. He concealed himself behind a bush and shortly afterwards saw the defendant come out of his house, walk along the footpath to the wood, and then go to the snares. As there was nothing in them he put them in his pocket. The keeper then told him he would "have him up." The snares were set 200 yards from the footpatt, and 200 yards from the roadway on the other side.— Defendant said he was an old gamekeeper and was 73 years of age. He had never been summoned before. He had served as gamekeeper under Lord Delamere.—A fine of ss. and costs was imposed, the advocate's fee being allowed. GAMBLERS WATCHED THROUGH FIELD GLASSES.— Thomas Rustage, Ernest Harper, Frank Webster,Thomas Birchall, Colin Bennett,George Knight, Stanley Starkey (all of Stockton Heath), Ernest Miller and Ernest Stannard (of Warrington), all youths, were summoned for gaming with cards at Appleton. on August 9th.— Defendants all denied that they played for money.— P.C. Williams said that at seven o'clock on the Sunday in question he and Acting-Sergeant Bowyer saw the whole of the defendants playing cards on Broomfields footpath. They watched them through a pair of field glasses and saw what they took to be money pass between them. When they made a rush for the defend- ants they heard the sound of money.—Defendants were fined 2s. 6d. each.

A CURE FOR RHEUMATISM.

Mr. William Ginger. of Quenon-street, near New- port, Essex, had suffered very severely and, as he is now quite well, his friends are inclined to believe that the remedy that he used is an effective one. Mr. Ginger's trouble started with Influenza about two years ago. He had it so bad that he was in bed under the doctor's care for A fortnight. He got up and took a Chill and had to go back to bed, this time with Rheu- matic Fever. He tells his further experience in these words

"I was then taken to the hospital and for twenty- one weeks I was unable to do a stroke of work. I did get back and for some months kept going, but when the winter came I was knocked over once more. All the old pains came back worse than ever and I was afraid I would have to go into hospital again. "Just at this time my wife read of some had cases of Rheumatism that had been cured by Dodd's Kidney Pills and I began to take them Improvement came slowly at first but I kept on till now I am as free from pain as I ever was. "Dodd's Kidney Pills are a genuine remedy and I know they will cure Rheumatism."

Singular Railway Mishap.

About six o'clock on Tuesday evening a coal train going from Earlestown to Holyhead with coal for shipping purposes, struck the points at Earlestown Junction, the engine and several waggons getting off the road. A light engine from Manchester running in the same direction ran foul of the derailed train, with the result that they became blocked. The consequence was that the main line between Manchester and War- rington was entirely blocked for about an hour, when one of the lines was opened up for traffic. A great delay ensued, trains for Manchester and Chester having to be worked thmugh Earlestown Station, and back again on the single line.. The traffic between. Liverpool and Manchester was also considerably inter- fered with. The permanent way was damaged to some extent, and it was not until about midnight that the traffic could be dealt with in anything like a normal way. A large number of men were busily engaged. No one was injured.

===== 0002601_19030912_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
The ExamineT, Saturday, September 12 1903.

A WARRINGTON BOY'S SAD DEATH.

Drowned While Catching a Frog.

On Sunday afternoon the body of the boy Joseph Hatton, nine years of age, who was drowned in Sankey Brook last Thursday, was recovered from the brook by William Byfield of Runcorn. The sad circumstances attending his death were related at the inquest which was held at the Police Station on Monday by Mr. S. Brighouse, County Coroner. Alice Hatton, mother of the deceased, who resides at 101, Plumpton-street, said she last saw deceased alive at one o'clock on Thursday- afternoon when he left home to go to play. Later in the afternoon she heard that he had fallen into Sankey Brook and been drowned. Grappling orations were at once commenced but the body was not recovered until Sunday. James Scott, nine years of age, who lives at 212, Liverpool-road, stated that on Thursday he had a half- holiday from school and he and deoeased went to play by Sankey Brook. Whilst in the act of catching a frog deceased fell into the water. Witness went into the brook and tried to reach him but he coed not and deceased went out of sight. The Coroner said he understood there was a great deal of water in the brook and it was running very swiftly. Detective Barlow said the boy Scott went up to his neck in thejwater when he tried to rescue the deceased. William Byfield, general labourer, 13, Thomas-street, Runcorn, said that on Sunday morning he received information that a boy had been drowned in Sankey Brook. He obtained special grappling appliances and proceeded there and recovered the body of the deceased I at 5.10.

The Coroner inquired how it was that witness came to recover the body. . .

Witness said he ;vas the captain of the Runcorn and District Grappling Corps—anumber of workingmen who had purchased a special complete set of grappling appli- ances and recovered the bodies of persons who had been drowned. The only remuneration they received was the allowances sometimes given by., Coroners for the recovery of bodies. He handed the Coroner a circular asking for contributions to enable the Corps to pur- chase a grappling boat which would be moored in the river at Runcorn Bridge so that the corps could at once render assistance when it was required. Four men came to Warrington to grapple for deceased's body. They paid their own expenses. The Coroner promised a sulpoription of 10s. 6d. to- wards the new boat and said he would consult with Detective Barlow with a view to raising a contribution of, /25 in Warrington. A verdict of "Accidentally drowned "was returned. THE CORONER AND THE GRAPPLING CORPS. SUBSCRIPTION LIST OPENED.

The Chief Constable has received a letter from Mr. Brighouse calling his attention to the work of the Runcorn Grappling Corps. "From all I could glean at the inquest" says Mr. Brighouse "this corps ts well deserving of support. It seems the corps is com- posed of volunteers who, witihout any practical hope of reward go into the neighbourhood of Runcorn—and it includes Warrington—with the object of recovering bodies that have been drowned. I need scarcely tell you that when a person is drowned the relatives are always most anxious to have the body recovered and to give it a decent inter- ment, and in the case of the inquest I held four members of the corps came over from Runcorn at their own expense, and dragged the brook in which the body was supposed to be, and although grappling operations had been carried on by others, these members of the corps succeeded after some hours in recovering the,body, to, I should think, the satisfaction of the parents. I promised to ' subscribe 10s. 6d., and as the wants of the corps are not • very great, I thought perhaps with assistance,the good people of Warrington might subscribe. I gathered from Mr. Byfield that if £5 was subscribed in Warrington the corps would be very much obliged, and it would go a long way towards the _purchase of the police grappling boat which, I under- stand,will cost about £l2. Should you,with the assistance of your officers, open a list, I think this amount could easily he collected in Warrington and Warrington would receive benefit from the money subscribed as the corps would always be ready to go from Runcorn to Warring- ton on the river to attempt to recover bodies which have been lost." • We understand that the Chief Constable has decided to open a subscription listand will be pleased to receive donations.
