ST. PAUL'S WESLEYAN CHURCH, RUNCORN. . Re-Opening Services After Renovation.

The Cost of the Scheme.

Re-opening services were held on Monday at St. Paul's Wesleyan Church, Runcorn. For some months the church has been closed; extensive alterations have been effected, the whole building has been re-decorated, and the organ has been thoroughly overhauled and decorated in harmony with the new colour scheme. The first service was held in the afternoon, and was well attended. The service opened with the singing of the hymn "All people that on earth do dwell." The Rev. W. T.Coppin offered prayer and read a portion of scrip- ture. He afterwards explained the present position of the extension scheme fund. According to the com- pleted scheme they had expended £5,000. Into the circuit scheme had been put the cost of the reconstruc- tion of the roof of the church. That amounted to £2,2so.Towards the £5,000 they had raised and had in hand £2,335. Roughly speaking, they wanted about £2,600 to wipe off the balance standing against the fund. They saw their way towards £5OO by means of the forth- coming Halton bazaar and previsional grants. What they therefore wanted that day was a splendid contri- , button towards the £2,100.

The Rev. W. L. Watkinson ex-president of the con- ference, preaclied an excellent sermon. Some sceptical writers, he 'said, declared that man was altogether a creature of circumstance, a waif and stray on a current he did not understand, and which it was impossible for him to resist. He would tell them that everything in life turned upon the question as to whether they were to be mastered by the blind forces outside, or whether they were to be governed by reason within. Was man a frail canoe urged on by cnrrents he could not resist. and must he to-morrow be toppled over Niagara? Was he captain of himself? Could he be what he ought to be in defiance of outside circumstances? That was the deepest question they had to settle. The position of the Christian Church was that the sovereignty was the sovereignty of the soul, that they were responsible for what they did, and that each of them must give an account of himself unto God. If there was one thing clear in the consciousness of mankind it was that they were masters of their own fate They were responsible for what they did, the kingdom was not outside them, but the ruling force of life—the intelligence, the conscience and the will—was the sovereignty of the soul. Many of them felt they would be really line men if they had not inherited certain impulses, passions and weaknesses horn those who had gone before them. He was not going to deny the doctrine of inheritance. The Christian Church taught it from the beginning, only the old preazhers used to call it original sin. They never heard preachers tow talk about original sin. What did they talk about? Inherited evil. It was exactly the same thing. It was the modern way of expressing an ancient doctrine. They got a great deal from their ancestors which gave them great trouble. They often wished their ancestors had taken with them a good deal that they left. But when they talked about their ancestors they must go far enough back. If they had a clear perception of the doctrine of inheritance they must go all the way back. They must remember that they came from a royal root. The greatest and deepest thing in man was the divinity he saw fashioned in the likeness of an intellectual and righteous God. That was a grand thing to think about. The kingdom of God was within them. Tea was afterwards provided in the Brunswick school- room. The newly-appointed ministers (the Revs. R. Green and W. Jackson) were present, and made the acquaintance of the members of the congregation. Subsequently the enlarged and renovated organ was formally re-opened by An organ recital by Mr. William Cooke, F.R.C.O. The Rev. W. L. Watkinson preached at the evening service.

GREAT SANKEY.

TRIP.—The members of St. Mary's Church Choir and Sunday school teachers had their annual trip on Satur- day, the destination being Southport. The party numbering 621 included the Vicar, the Rev. J. R. Jones, Mi. H. P. C. White (vicar's warden), and Mr. T. E. Shoobridge (people's warden). They travelled by the Cheshire lines route, leaving Sankey at 9.35 a.m. Upon arrival at Southport the party broke up into various sections, and spent the day as inclination dictated. During the afternoon a substantial dinner was partaken of at Ye old Castle Cafe, Neville-street. The return journey was commenced at 9.5. The weather was all that could have been desired, and a most enjoyable day was spent.

Warrington Motorists in Trouble at Rhyl: At Rhyl Petty Sessions, on Tuesday, James Buxton of Warrington, and a visitor to Rhyl, was charged with furiously driving a motor bicycle along High- street, Rhyl, on the eVening of the 3rd just, and William Bayly Isaac, also of Warrington, was sum- moned for a similar offence. P.S. M Walter stated that each defendant was riding a motor bicycle with 8. ,side car attached, in which a lady was seated. High- 'street was full of people at the time, and defendants, speed was from 15 to 20 miles an hour. Several wit- nesses having been called in support of the sergeant's evidence, it was submitted for the defence that de- fendants did not travel at an excessive pace. Buxton said he was certain he did not exceed eight miles an hour. Defendants were each fined £1 and £1 176.5 d. costs.

BASSINETTES AND MAIL CARTS in latest art shades.—J. S. GARLAND, Furnishing Ironmonger, 51, 53, and 55, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. Resignation of General Hallam Parr. It is officially stated in Chester that Major-General Hallam Parr will shortly resign the command of the North-Eastern District in consequence of ill-health, due to the effects of an old wound. Though he has been at Chester only a little over a year, General Hallam Parr has done much to increase the efficiency of both the Regular and volunteer forces in the exten- sive district under his command. He has, among other things, secured military camping grounds in both the Isle of Man and Delamere Forest. It will be remem- bered that General Hallam Parr visited Warrington to open the Soldiers' Club in Bewsey-road. FIRE GRATES of every description.—J. S. GAR- LAND, Furnishing Ironmonger, 51, 53, and 55, Bridge-street, Warringion.—Advt. A Ferryman Drowned. Mr. Coroner Brighouse held an inquest at the Boat- house Inn, Rixton, on Monday on the body of Samuel Chesworth, thirty-four, a farm labourer,who was found drowned in the Manchester Ship Canal on Sunday last The deceased resided at the Boathouse Inn, and assis- ted in working the ferryboat from the Inn across the river to the Cheshire side. On Sunday night, the 30th ult., about ten o'clock he took two passengers across and when returning he was sculling the boat, and was seen by a boy named Frederick Hankinson to over- balance and fall overboard. The night was dark and very stormy, and it was very probable that the oar either slipped out of the rowlocks or he missed his footing owing to the strong wind. Dragging operations were carried on for days, but the body was ,not re- covered until Sunday. The jury returned a Verdict of "Accidental death. H. M. The 20th annual show in connection with the Croft Horticultural Society was held last week-end. The weather being favourable there was a very good attendance. Dobson, 'coroner, touching the death of Mary Forrington, wife of a foreman maltster named Colin Forrington, of Kendal, but who, up to last week resided in New-road, Latchford.—The evidence showed that on Saturday night, the 29th nit, deceased and her husband quarrelled and shortly afterwards deceased disappeared. town. Rechabites' Picnic. The annual picnic in connection with John Dimond Juvenile branch took place on Saturday, the place selected being Frodsham Hills. About 140 juveniles with their superintendents and friends left Bank Quay station by the 12-30 p.m. train on which corridor coaches had been attached for the party. The morn- ing was anything but promising after the heavy downfall of rain but when the train reached Frodsham the hearts of the children were gladdened by seeing the sun shining in full splendour. Games such as cricket, bowling, rounders, swings and donkey-riding, were heartily indulged in by the youngsters. Tea was provided at 4-30 when full justice was done to the good things provided. After tea, walks were arranged to the caves and Dunsdale Hollow which were much enjoyed. The time for the return journey arrived only too soon and the beautiful evening tempted a number of the older members to stay somewhat longer than had been arranged. Warrington was 'safely reached about 7.45 p.m. which brought to a close one of the most successful picnics in connection with this branch. The whole of the cost of these annual picnics is defrayed from the surplus funds of the branch. I

PASSIVE RESISTANCE.

Sale of Distrained Goods at Altrincham.

Rev. Stephen Gladstone's Threat.

The sale took place at Altrinchain on Wednesday afternoqn of the goods belonging to the Hale passive resisters against whom the magistrates at Sale granted orders of distraint on the 31st ult. There was a good deal of local interest manilested, owing to the position of the parties, who are well-known residents of the Bowdon district. A local auctioneer, Mr. Walkden, of Market-street, Altrincham, had undertaken to put the goods under the hammer, and for some half-hour before the advertised time his doors were besieged by a crowd of people eager for admission. The little auction-room would hold, at the utmost, some 40 or 50 persons, and when it was found that hardly a tithe of those pre- sent could find standing room, loud protests wereraised. The auctioneer was preparing to submit the first " when the hymn, "0 God, our help in ages past," was started, and the first verse was completed before he could get a hearing. He would allow no singing, he said, and by way of warning, as was supposed, he hinted at the (presence of some eight or nine "professional gentlemen "—meaning policemen—in the building, who would be called on if necessary. The threat, however, was quite needless, as between the police and the passive resisters in this district there is nothing but good feel- ing. The considerateness of the local officials, including the police, in the execution of their delicate duties has been acknowledged repeatedly. The auctioneer's authority to hold the sale was chal- lenged, and, "What about your fees ? ' was asked by several onlookers; but these Interruptions were dis- regarded. A demand was made for his name, but his only response was, "How much for a silver Waltham lever watch?" " Whose is it ?" cried a person in the rear, amid confusion, and shouts of "This sale is illegal; it is not a public auction." "If you want to know," said the auctioneer "the watch is Mr. Manley's."—(Cries of "Hurrah !") Thirty shillings was bid for the watch, and the auctioneer admitted that mall cases as soon as the bidding reached the amonnt required the article would be knocked down. "That's all," said the auctioneer, when he had finished his duties. OPEN-AIR DEMONSTRATION. THE ACT CONDEMNED.

Some hundreds of people proceeded immediately after the sale to the Market Square, where a meeting was heldto protest against the Education Act and express sympathy with those whose goods had been distrained. The gathering was an earnest and orderly one. Public interest in the proceedings seemed to have grown con- siderably during the fortnight which has elapsed since a previous meeting was held in the same place. The Rev. F. C. Lloyd presided, and the Rev. Dr. Townsend, ex-president_of the National Free Church Council, was present, besides several local ministers.

- The Bev. Dr. Clifford sent the .following message : "I congratulate you upon your bold stand. It is a great service; it is a national service ; and it is right. You and your friends must go forward in this warfare. It is a holy war. God Himself is commander. Joyfully we follow, and whether we win or lose, we know we are fighting in a divine cause."—(Cheers). The Rev. P. B. Meyer wrote :—" May the Lord over- rule all this storm for the coming of His kingdom. and the salvation of our country from having the springs of its young life poisoned by priestly influence." Dr.frownsend, who was introduced by Mr. Lloyd as one who by pen and speech had done much to foster the resistance movement, said they had lived to see strange things. -They could not have believed only a short while' ago that Christian men and women would be found selling up their fellow-Christians, and that Protestants would seize and sell the goods of fellow-Protestants,for agate that wasito support a Romanised National Church and infectourchildren with dogmas and practices they abhorred —(Hear hear).Nonconformists had not sought;this battle. (Hear, hear.) They were in no wise responsible for what was taking place. They had never sought a battle in all their religious history.—(Hear, hear.) At the same time they had never shirked one, and, what was more, had never lost one.—(Cheers.) The right would again prevail if they were true to their principles, their history, and their ancestry.—(Hear, hear.) This move- ment had spread through the country like wildfire. In three months 450 leagues had been formed, and a year hence, under more perfect organisation, there would be 800 or more.—(Cheers.) Dr. Townsend stated at length the grounds of passive resistance, dwelling strongly on the aggressiveness of the Anglican priesthood, and de- clared that it was too late in the day for the liberties and rights of Protestant Englishmen to be paltered with. (Cheers.) Mr. Manley proposed the following resolution, which was seconded by the Rev. W. D. Thomas and carried unanimously : 'This meeting records its solemn and emphaVc condemnation of the Education Acts of 1902-3, on the grounds that they grossly violate the sacred principles of justice, freedom of conscience, and religi- ous equality. It congratulates those ladies and gentlemen whose goods have just been sold upon the stand which they have taken; and it rejoices in the marvellous growth of the passive resistance movement and in the great and ever-growing sympathy of the British people both at home and abroad."

Responding to a vote of thanks proposed by Mr. Thomas and seconded by Mr. Frank Spence, Dr. Townsend said that in Wales ten oounty ommcils out of twelve had declared that they would not levy a rate for sectarian schools unless the schools were placed under popular management and control.— (Cheers.) Matters would soon reach a state of dead- lock. The rector of Hawarden' in order to frighten the County Council of Flintshire, had given notice that unless he got the money for his schools by the end of this month he would close them. But there was a counter-movement. Mr. Storey, the Free Church minister in Hawarden had, after consultation with brother ministers, informed the County Council that so soon as the parish schools were closed,their ownschools would be opened to the children, and every possible means would be taken to carry on the work of educa- tion under popular control.—(Cheers.) The Rector might go for a mandamus if he liked. It would not be worth the parchment it was written on. The Council would go to prison rather than submit.—(Hear, hear.) They would then have the edifying spectacle of not Nonconformists only but Churchmen and Tories, marched off to gaol for refusing to carry out theEduca- don Act.—(Laughter and cheers). The Doxology was sung and the meeting broke up. Earlestown Passive Resisters Summoned.

Great interest was centred in the police court pro- ceedings at Newton-le-Willows on Thursday, when six passive resisters were summoned for non-payment of •the education rate. The defendants were the Rev. James Allatt, a venerable figure in Congregational circles, whose pastorate is at Newton-le-VVillows Con- gregational Church; the Rev. George Cooke, pastor at the Earlestown Primitive Methodist Chapel, by whose endeavours the Citizens' Passive Resisters League at Earlestown was founded; Mr. Edward J. George, secretary to Messrs. Richard Evans and Company, Limited, Haydock Collieries; Mr. Rowland King, art printer, Bridge-street, Earlestown ; Mr. George S. King, confectioner, Bridge-street, Earlestown ; and Mr. James Wood, clogger, Queen-street, Earlestown. The unpaid rates were :—Rev. James Allatt, 70., together with costs of application, 3s. 7d,; Rev. G. Gooke,4s.6d. including costs; Mr. George, 3s. 2d. including costs; Mr. Rowland King, Bs. 110. ; Mr. George King, 145.; Mr. James Wood, 14s. lid. All the defendants ad- mitted the rate. Mr. Allatt objected to it On deeply religious grounds; Mr. Cooke because he objected to pay for teaching which he utterly abhorred; Mr. George added no defence; the others had nothing to add. All, however, testified to the courtesy which they had received from the assistant overseer. Mr. Henry -Houghton begged to acknowledge it. Distress warrants were issued in each case. There were originally twelve passive resisters, but six had paid up.

providing a system of religious instruction can only result in presenting what Gladstone called "a moral monster" to those who must perforce regard such attempts as trespassing on the province of the sanctuary. To teach the Bible as literature is, of course, within the province of the State, but for the State to teach it as a religion is at once to conjure the moral monster to many minds. . .

Religion,therefore,as such,whether denominational,un- denominational, or predenominational, the State should leave severely alone. For directly it touches it in- justice is felt by those who profoundly dislike such handling of it. The State, however, should give oppor- tunity—frank, full and free—to every religious body, Christian or otherwise, to teach its tenets to those who desire it, at the expense of the body in question. To me the right seems as elementary as it is obvious. Unhappily, however, Nonconformists as a body appear only to concede this right to Jews,and such as are con- tent with undenominationalism. Other "consciences" who desiderate equal and impartial treatment are looked upon as unreasonable disturbers of the general peace. If to purchase true conditions of peace, which should involve the right of the Church to enter all State schools during school hours to teach Church children the church's faith, the Church of England were to hand over her schools absolutely to the State, there are many Churchmen, like myself, who would gladly make the sacrifice. I trust the Liberals, who will, we hope, soon return to office, will settle the education question on true Liberal lines, and thus remove one great source of contention and strife from our midst."

The Rev. F. E. Powell and the Education Difficulty.

Writing to the "Manchester Guardian," the Itev. F. E. Powell, of Sewerby, formerly vicar of St.James's, Warrington, says :—" The letters which have been re- cently appearing in the press afford the profoundest satisfaction to those who long for a just, peaceful, and permanent solution of the eduction problem. Many of us have long seen that this can only be obtained by keeping the provision of the definitely religious entirely separate from the provision of the definitely secular. The various religious bodies should be held responsible for the one, while the State should take over absolute responsibility for the other. All that these religious bodies should request from the State is perfect liberty and equality for each to impart its respective tenets to the children of those parents who desire it for them. And because it is generally recognized by citizens that moral principles should have a distinctly religious basis, the State should make provision for the giving of religious instruction during school hours, otherwise the temptation to both parent and child for the latter to stay away will prove too strong. But here the duty of the State should cease. "It would be better," said Mr. Gladstone, "for the State to limit itself to giving secular instruction— which, of course, is no complete education." RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL. The Light Railway to Stockton Heath.

The monthly meeting of this authority was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday under the presidency of Mr. S. Woodward. There were also present :—Messrs G. Pilling (vice chairman), J. Mclntyre, T. Baxter, Admiral Jones-Parry, T. Wright, J. B. Chadwick, C. E. Linaker, J. Moseley Williams, A. G. Guest, C. Roylance J. Darlington, J. C. Collier, J. Spruce, J. Mounfleld, E. Clarke, J. Bolshaw, T. THE SENiERING "OF WESTON.

At a meeting of the Weston Parochial Committee it was decided that Mr. James, of Heywood, be engaged to make preliminary plans for the sewering of the parish and that the clerk confer with him with a view to the scheme being so arranged as to fit in with the works which have to be carried on by the Manchester Ship Canal Company under an agreement. The minutes were approved.

HIGHWAYS COMMITTEE. TJFIE WARRINGTON CORPORATION LIGHT RAILWAY.

Ata;meeting of the Highways and Public Health Com- mittee the Clerk read a letter from the Cheshire County Council stating that that authority could not entertain the District Council's request that all telegraph and telephone poles on the main roads be painted white to a height of eight feet. The Clerk produced plans de- posited by the Warrington Corporation showing the manner in which it was proposed to construct light railway No. 1. It was reported that the Stockton Heath Parish Council were asking the County Council to press the Corpor:tion to provide for inter- lacing lines opposite the church and to alter the posi- tion of certain poles. It was decided to support the Parish Council in their application. The minutes were adopted. The Clerk read the minutes of a special Ilarish Coun- cil meeting on this matter and said that in accordance with the instructions of the Highway Committee he had forwarded the Parish Council's resolution regarding the width of road &c.,—which has already appeared in the "Examiner,"—to the County Council. "A FLAGRANT BREACH OF THE BYLAWS."

Mr. Lieaker moved the adoption of the minutes of the Building Committee. With regard to the plans for the alteration of the premises of Mr. Lloyd, butcher, Church-street, Frodsham, the committee had sanctioned them, but he considered that the committee had per- mitted the most flagrantl breach of the byelaws which had ever come under his notice.

Mr. Mclntyre said he was a member of the Building Committee, but he withdrew rather than support the passing of the plans. Mr. Williams proposed that the matter be referred back to the Frodsham Parochial Committee. He was a member of that body for a number of years and could bearltestimony to the many flagrant breaches of the byelaws. Mr. Diggle, the surveyor for the district, said the back premises were not in accordance with the byelaws, but under the circumstances ho considered that it would be an injustice to the owner not to pass the plans. Mr. Williams said Frodsham had been guilty of trans- gressing the byelaws. It would be very little use having byelaws if they were to set them aside at the will of interested people. . .

It was decided tO reter the matter back to the Frod- sham Parochial Committee, there being only one dis- sentient from the proposition. A member remarked that it seemed peculiar that the surveyor should recommend the passing of the plans if they were not in accordance with the byelaws. The Chairman.said he'presumed Mr. Digglehadpointed out to the committee that the plans were not strictly in accordance with the byelaws, but he recommended under the circumstances that they be passed. Mr. Linaker: We have strained at gnats and now we have swallowed a camel.

The Chairman: We have not swallowed it. (Laughter). The matter then dropped. FRODSHAM AND DELAMERE STATION HIGHWAY.

The Clerk stated that at the last meeting of the High- ways Committee there was a discussion with respect to the proposed taking over of the Frod.ham and Delamere station highway as a main road reparable by the County Couhoil and he was instructed to write to the North- wich Rural Council asking if they would support the Council an in application to that effect. He had received a reply stating that the Northwich Council were willing to appoint a committee to meet a com- mittee of the Runcorn Council on the road in question. The Council appointed a committee as suggested. RE-APPOINTMENT OF THE INSPECTOR OF NUISANCES. On the motion of Mr. Pilling, seconded by Mr. Far- rington, the Council approved the re-appointment of Mr. Flrrington as inspector of nuisances and inspector of canpl boats. STOCKTON HEATH'S REPRESENTATION.

This matter was again introduced by Mr. Pilling who said that some time ago a committee was formed but so little evidence was brought forward that the question was allowed to pass over. The Parochial Committee had again introduced the subject and he moved the re- appointment of the old committee—Messrs. Woodward, Knowles, Stokes,Darlington and Pilling—together with Mr. C. W. Davenport, Mr. J. Mounfleld, and the over- seers of Stockton Heath, to further consider it. This was agreed to. HONORARIUM FOR THE INSPECTOR.

On the motion of Mr. Moseley Williams it was de- cided to grant Mr. Farrington an honorarium of £5 for his services during the small-pox epidemic. HELSBY WATERWORKS.

It was decided to accept the tender of Messrs. Timmins and Sons for the sinking of a well in connec- tion with these works at a cost of £496. THE RATES.

Mr. Wright produced estimates for the overseers' contributions. He reported that the capital for the current half year was £5,079, the probable expenditure would be £3,376, leaving a balance of £1,703. During the next half year, ending Lady Day 1904, the probable expenditure would be ;5,300, and deducting the balance of £1,703, the sum or £3,597 would be required from the overseers. A rate of 5.1 d. in the £on the assessable value of the district would produce £3,630, and it was recommended that a call for £3,s97—being equal to the oall made for the last half year, but an increase of id. In the on the call for the corresponding period last year—be'made upon the overseers. The estimates were approved. THE PAINTING OF TELEGRAPH POLES.

Admiral Jones-Parry said he understood that two years ago the Council decided not to allow telegraph poles to be put up in the district unless they were painted white to a certain height. Some poles had been erected at Appleton but they were yellow.

The Clerk poinfe-d out that it ivas simply decided.to ask the County Council to support them in asking the Telephone Company and Post Office authorities to whiten the poles to a height of 8 feet but the County Council would not agree to it. The Council did not decide that they would not allow poles to be erected unless they were painted. After a short discussion the matter dropped. HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT.

Dr. Adams reported that two cases of scarlet fever at Daresbury and Whitley respectively had just been notified. Dr. Carruthers had informed him of an out- break of throat trouble at Preston Brook and the water was suspected as the cause. Dr. Carruthers made application for the water to be analysed and he (Dr. Adams) recommended that that should be done. The Workhouse was now free from chicken pox and the children had returned to school.

The report was adopted. This was all the business.

PEN KETH.

THE WEATHER.—The continued wretched weather is playing havoc with the corn and farmers produce in the district. Some of the cornhae been cut for someweeks and in some places it has commenced spritting. Trade is also very slack, just now, and the prospects for winter, are not particularly bright. SUDDEN DEATH.—The village was shocked on Wed- nesday morning, by the news of the painfully sudden death of Al& Joseph Dunabin, who resided at the Lock house, Fiddlers Ferry. Mr. Dunabin has been the Lock- keeper for many years, and rose at 4 a.m.on Wednesday morning for the early tide. On returning to the house he was seized with an epileptic fit, and expired shortly afterwards. He leaves a widow and family with whom great sympathy is felt. Mr. Dunabin was well-known and respected in the village.

Do you know how

to make Porridge ?

Perhaps you do. It is a very simple 'thing. Still, simple as it is, there is a right waY and a wrong way of doing it. The most approved waY

—the way they have in

Scotland, the home of porridge —is, first of all, to put 3 quantity of the very finest oats that can be obtained, which are, of course,

' Provost ' Oats

into a Porringer of boiling water, add a little salt, and boil

for ten minutes, if you can spare / fifteen minutes all the better----it

takes time to bring out the

flavour—the more time you give it, the better you will like your

morning dish.

R. ROBINSON & SONS, ANNAN, N. B.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The " Handy-Man " Appointment.

A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held on Wednesday morning. Mr. A. Brookes pre- sided and there were also present :—Mrs. Clay, Dr. Patterson, Dr. Murray, Messrs. G. Pilling, A. J. Jolley, T. H. Ashcroft, A. Jolley, J. Clarke, William Houghton, Turton, J. Billinge, E.L. Bent, Robinson, G. Mather, T. Ritchie, with J. C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (assist- ant clerk), J. F. Awbery (workhouse master) and A. Wright (superintendent Padgate schools). "THE APPOINTMENT OF A HANDY MAN." __

The minutes of the interim meeting of the Guardians were read bylthe Clerk. These had reference to the scheme reported in the " Examiner " of a fortnight ago, to ease the present congested state of the House, and to make provision for a probable increase of ,iindbor poor in the winter. The means suggested are : Internal andexternal renovation of the old hospital—closure of back entrances thereto, fitting up partitions for effec- tive separation of sexes, connection of electric light. application of water suppiy te baths, lavatories, &c., and without material structural disturbance to make the buildings suitable for accommodation of inmates. Efficient indoor administration is to be provided by practicable changes in the duties of nurses and laundress, except that for the male side an additional male servant is required. The committee decided it to be prac tic able to connect with the duties of the new officer, upon the appointment of a suitable man, sundry painting, plumbing and corresponding work so often required to be done, and such a man had been recommended to them. In view of the need for avoiding delay the appointment if Samuel Birkett of the Southwell Union Workhouse, was sanctioned by the Board at the interim meeting, the remuneration fixed being £25 per annum and the usual emoluments.

Mr. ASHCROFT rose to question the appointment of the "handy man." The CHAIRMAN: You cannot re-open the question. Mr. AsHcßoyr : The reason I ask— The CHAIRMAN: Do you contest the correctness of the minutes?

Mr. AsucßoFT : I protest against the appointment being included in the minutes. The CHAIRMAN: The only question is,are the minutes a correct record.

Mr. Asncitorr : It says the appointment was made of Samuel Birkett of the Southwell Union Workhouse, plumber. Is he a plumber? Mr. PILLING: No; he is a screw driver. (Laughter.) Mr. ASHCROFT : Ile is described on the minutes a.s.a plumber. (Laughter.) _ _

- The CLEthc : -He is not described on the minutes as a plumber. Although he may be so described on the agenda it does not infer that he is described in the minutes as a plumber. _ _ _

The minutes were then passed,and the matter dropped. •

PADGATE SCHOOL APPOINTMENTS.

_ The Padgate Schools Committee recommended and it was agreed to that Mr. W. A. G. Coleman, of Leeds, be appointed master's clerk at a salary of £l5 a year with board, lodgings, &c. Miss Poster, formerly of Crumpsall Workhouse, Man- chester was appointed foster mother of No. 3 home, at a salary of £2O with board, lodgings, &c. Three candi- dates appeared before the Board.

A. DEPUTATION To BLACKPOOL. On the proposition of the CHAIRMAN it was resolved that the Chairman, Vice-Chairman, and Mr. Ashcroft, with the Clerk, attend the 29th annual North-Western Poor Law Conference, to be held at Blackpool,upon the 25th and 26th days of September instant, as representa- tives of the Board. THE AMBER OF PAUPERS IN THE UNION. The CLERK, in accordance with a request of the Local Government Board submitted a statement showing the number of paupers chargeable to the Union on the Ist September 1903, both indoor and outdoor. From this return it appeared that there were 370 males and 555 females in receipt of relief, total 925.

THE COUNTY RATE PRECEPT. The Clerk laid before the Board the County rate pre- cept requiting the Guardians to pay on or before September 21st, out of the moneys paid into the bands of their Treasurer' the sum of £5,255 18s. 3d., the parti- culars of the rate being—(l) General County Purposes, 1 15-16 d. in thee; (2) General Elementsry Education at 3d. in the £ ; (3) General Police Purposes at 5-32 d. inthe £ ; (4) Warrington Police Division Purposes at lid. in the £ ; (5) Wigan Police Division Purposes at 2 1-24 d. in the £ ; (6) West Derby Hundred Main Road Purposes at 19-24 in the £. The total sum payable by each parish forpayment whereof precepts had been served upen the respective overseers and duplicates with service endorsed thereon was as follows :—Burtonwood, £555 18s. 6d. ; Cnerdley, £l7B Bs. 5d.; Haydock, £795 12s. 5d.; Hough- ton, Middleton and Arbury, £55 7s. 9d.; Newton-m- Makerfield, £2,028 3s. 2d. ; Penketh, £251 9s. 7d.; Poulton-with-Fearnhead, £209 16s. • Rixten-with-Glaze- brook, £229 19s. 10c1.; Sankey, dreat, £245 16s. 5d.; Southworth-with-Croft, £ll3 10s. 10d. ; Winwick-with- Hulme, £469 18s. id. ; Woolston-with-Martinscroft, £l2l 17s. 3d.; total, £5,255 18s. 3d. The Clerk reported that he had in preparation the precepts made each sum payable on or before September 16th, to the Union Treasurer.

ACCEPTANCE OF TENDERS. The Board accepted tenders from the following:—Mr. William Jones, clogs; Messrs. E. Pickavance and Son, leather ;M r. J. W.Houghton, milk for Padgate Schools; Mr. Darbyshire, milk for the Workhouse; Mr. C. Roy- lance, Moore Farm, potatoes ; Mr. H. Jackson, brushes; Mr. Lowden Wells coffms; Mr. lames Bowcock butchers meat; Mr. James Hallwood, bread flour and meal. The tenders for Loots and shoes, drapery, grocery, coal and slack; and drugs were referred to the House Committee. The tenders for glazing were not considered as the Master said there vvo uld be no necestity to go outside tbe House for this work in future.

This was all the business of public interest.

WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT

COUNCIL. The Conduct of Gipsies.

The monthly meeting of this Council was hew tit Bewsey Chambers on Wednesday afternoon, Mr. T. Halton presiding. There were also present :—Messrs Brooks, Mather, Houghton, Ball, Knowles, Dr. Murray' Forshaw, J. C.. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (assistant' clerk), Dr. Sephton (medical officer), F. Kerfoot (inspector of nuisances), B. S. Newcombe (surveyor) and H. H. Burnage (assistant surveyor.)

"MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT.

Dr. SEPHTON reported that during the month there had been 36 births and 31 deaths. The death-rate was equal to 327 per 1,000, against 35 the average of the last three months, Ind 21 the rate for the corresponding month last year. Eighteen deaths occurred in the asylum at Winwick, and the death-rate proper for the district was IP9.

THE RATES,

The CLERK informed thP Council that a rate of 6d. in the £ would be required for general expenses during the next half year.

This rate was sanctioned. _ GIPSIES AT WINWICK.

The INSPECTOR reported the receipt of a letter from the Superintendent of Winwick Asylum- calling atten- tion to the fact that gipsies were in the habit of encamping on the piece of ground in Hollins-lanewhich was given to the Council by the Asylum's Board, and asking the Council to take the necessary steps to have the gipsies removed. The CHAIRMAN said the gipsies ought to be removed. Mr. KNOWLES : Quite right. The land was given to the Council for the purpose of widening the road. The gipsies not only eficamp there but stop the cindered footpath near the Asylum by putting ropez across to pen the horses in.

/ Mr. HALTON remarked that gipsies at one time encamped on a piece of waste land at Sankey, but the Council informed the police and had them removed at once.

Mr. KNOWLES : Look what an officious body your Council is. They are the talk of the whole county. (Laughter.)

, The INSPECTOR said he telephoned to the polite inspector at Earlestown and instructions were given to the local constable to remove the gipsies.

LONGFORD DEPOT

With reference to the Longford Depot nuisance, the INSPECTOR reported that the culvert which conveyed the gases to the furnace was now complete, but one of the dryers had yet to be connected up. He was given to understand that further alterations were to be made. The nuisance still existed and further -complaint had been made by Mr. Knowles. The matter was not discussed.

FATAL BURNINGIAcCIDENT:AT WIDNES. A child named William Rawlinson, four years of age, living at 100, Kent-street, was admitted to'the Widnes Accident Hospital on Monday suffering from very severe burns to the abdomen, face and arms. It appears that on Monday the child was playing with matches, and his clothes became ignited. Despite the attentions of Dr. Josephs the boy died on Wednesday.

The Examiner, Saturday September 12, 1903. I PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

" MY husband is plain.spoken ; he calls a spade a spade." "So does mine ; but I must decline to repeat what he calls the lawn-mower." SHE: "Everybody says you married me only for my money." He But I didn't dear. I know you look it, dear, but I didn't."

MISS FROCKS : "Mr. Spokes, do you like Songs Without Words '7" Mr. Spokes: "Well, I very much prefer them to songs without sense."

CLARA : "When I was out on my wheel thi, morning I cracked my enamel quite badly." Mend: "You must learn not to smile." "au, the charms of the vanished childhood !" sighed the sentimentetist. "I've forgotten all of them," said the prosaic person, "except a charm for warts."

"THIS is the bill from your oculist," said the collector to Mr. Grimley. "Just take it back to him and tell him that I can't read it with those glasses he sold me." LADY "Now that you have had a good dinner, are you equal to the task of sawing some wood ? " Tramp: "Madam, equal is not the proper word ; I'm superior to it." HE: " Will you •be my wife?" She: "The idea Don't be ridiculous." " Yes, I know it sounds ridiculous ; but, then, I'm not so particular as some men are."

" LooK here, my lud," said a burglar, "I ain't so bad as you think I am. Only give me time and I'll reform." And his lordship gave him seven years to do it in. INTIMATE FRIEND: "Has your husband's love grown cool ? " Sarcastic wife: " Oh, no! He loves himself just as much now as he did when we were married 20 years ago. _ _

NEW MILK.—Mrs. Pe-ikins : "Did yer see any of them horseless kerridges up in the City? " Mr. P.: "No—o, but I had some of their cowless milk."

PLENTY OF Room: Poet: " I have a little con- tribution for—for the waste-paper basket." Editor —" We have no waste-paper basket." Poet: "I am delighted to hear that." Editor: "We use a barrel.'

"Puffins answered an advertisement in which somebody offered to sell him the secret for pre- venting trousers from getting fringes. round the bottom." What did they tell him 1" "To wear knickerbockers."

Undertaker: "And what kind of trimmings will you have on the casket?" Widow: "None whatever; a plain casket. It was trimmins that killed him." Undertaker: "What?" Widow: " Yes ; delirium trimming." Confidence is not always the growth of time. There are minds that meet each other with a species of affinity that resembles the cohesive pro- perty of matter, and with a promptitude and faith that only belongs to the purer essence of which they are composed. ATE THE MESSES6ERS.—A story is told in an Antwerp newspaper of some carrier pigeons. The editor of a journal published in that city sent a reporter to Brussels for the King's speech, and with him a couple of carrier pigeons. At Brussels he gave the pigeons in charge of a waiter and called for breakfast. He was kept waiting for some time, but a delicate dish atoned for the delay. MR. CHAMBERLAIN AND PROTECTION.

NOTHING NEW IN PREFERENTIAL TARIFFS.

WHO PAYS THE TAX ON FOOD?

An Old Plan.

THESE is a great danger that in di,enqsing Mr. Chamberlain's proposals to tax f0,)4 t people will imagine that his sae ,•43 isthing entirely entirely new, something which will work wonders in promoting the prosperity of the people. Those who are able to do so should read up the history of the last great tight for Free Food. They will learn many things which will help them to understand the great question of the day. They will find the argu- ments that are now being used, in support of taxing food were used sixty years ago. Sixty years ago the Protectionists promised the workers higher wages in return for dear food. They may not have promised Old-Age Pensions, but the workers received very large contribu- tions out of the rates, practically every work- ing man, woman, and child in the rural districts being in receipt of parish relief. But, in spite of dear food, wages never increased. When wheat was 80s.• a quarter, and labourers who were getting 7s. or Bs. a week asked fur more money, they were put off with the excuse that it could not be given until corn was over 100 s. a quarter. Even if wages were slightly increased, the high price of food made the last condition of the worker worse than the first. While Mr. Chamberlain can only argue from theories in support of dear food, our arguments are supported by the stern facts of history.

The Rule 0 'Three. Mn. CHAMBERLAIN claims that circum- stances have changed since 1843. We grant it. We have had sixty years of wonderful pros- perity under Free Trade. But there are some things which never change, and which are as true to-day as they were a century ago. The rules of arithmetic are the same. A taX upon food in 1843 meant dear food. A tax upon corn to-Llay will also mean dear food. People who try to argue that it does not should be asked to explain how it is that the price of corn in France, Germany, and Great Britain has differed according to the amount of duty imposed upon corn. Here are the figures: Price Country. Price. Duty. less duty. s. d. s. d. s. d. France Germany Great Britain 27 6 .. 1 0 .. 26 6 These figures were issued by the Government for June of the present year. Those who will take the trouble to look back into the history of our land will find that the laws of arith- metic were the, same in 1842 as they are in 1903. A duty on corn means dear corn when- ever and wherever it is imposed. Every tax upon food is paid for by the people. Even a shilling duty on corn, the Chancellor of the Exchequer and Mr. Balfour have admitted, is paid by the consumer. A striking instance of this truth was given by Mr. Alderman Bedford, a large miller of Boston, who, speaking in that neighbourhood a month or two ago, said: "The other day I paid £5OO for this shilling duty on corn. Do you think I am going to lose that £500? Of course lam not. lam going to get it out of some of you people by charging you more for your flour.'

The Worker's Vote. OWING to the fact that the workman has a vote, Mr. Chamberlain has had to modify his scheme. He told us, in the first place, that we must give the Colonies a preference or lose them. "No preference, no Empire," was his cry. To give the Colonies a preference, he said, we must tax food. Various offers of pensions, protection for agriculture, pro:Terity for the villages,- and a tax on foreign manufac- tures were thrown in at various intervals, according as the scheme seemed to be failing to "catch on." The working man may not understand political economy, but he cices, or his wife does, understand domestic eco; o ny. He knows that if you tax his bread you are hitting him in his weakest spot. You may tax his tobacco, and he will not grumble; tax uii beer, and he does not know it; tax his tea, end the grocer manages to give him the same weight for the same money. But when yen tax his bread and his meat you place a bunion on his shoulders he cannot possibly bear. You rob him, his wife, and his children of the bare necessities of life. If you tax food the worker will pot be able to pay more for it: he, his wife, and children will have to eat less. The Duke of Devonshire has told us that to-day, with cheap food, thousands ol our people are on the border line of hunger. What would happen if you were to tax their food ? Mr. Chamberlain realises that the workman Understands a food tax, and so now he is dropping his patriotic swagger of the sacrifices we must make for the Empire, and descending from his high Imperial platform to discuss housekeeping accounts with the work- man and his wife.

===== 0002601_19030912_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
Bread or Tobacco.

"LET me tax your food," says Mr. Chamber- lain to them, "and I will take off some of the taxes on your tea, sugar, and tobacco." But there are several objections to his scheme. In the first place, bread is an absolute necessity of life, while neither tea, sugar, nor tobacco is in the same category. If it comes to a pinch, at least tea and tobacco can be managed without. But the workman's wife likes to buy a joint for the Sunday dinner, if not oftener. If they cannot afford fresh meat, they like a bit of bacon as a relish with the potatoes. But for many a workman's family bread is, unfortunately, the chief article of food, and in some cases practically their only food; therefore, it cannot be done without. Even if a little tea and tobacco are bought every week, the amount consumed, compared with bread, is very little. So that to redi;ce the tax on tea and tobacco would not balance the tax on bread and meat. Mr. Chamber- lain's difficulties are not only with the poor, but also with the Treasury. The Treasury depends upon the taxes on tea, sugar, and tobacco to provide money to carry on the Government of the country. If Mr. Chamber- lain were to take these taxes off, the Treasury would come to him and say, "Where is our money to come from " So he says he will tax 'foreign-made goods, and make up the money in that way. A tax on foreign-made goods will be paid, in the first place, by the British merchants who order them. The merchants who order the goods will charge the tax, with interest, on the people who buy them. The people who buy them are the very same people whose food Mr. Chamberlain is taxing. And so we see that instead of making things easier by this latest scheme the Colonial Secretary is adding to the taxes we shall have to pay. He will not only tax our bread and meat, but our household goods as well. The fact of it is, he cinnot tax our food without taking money out of our pockets.

STREET BETTING AT WIDNES.

At the meeting of the Widnes Town Council on Tuesday, the minutes of the General Purposes Com- mittee contained a recommendation that a sub-committee be appointed to considerand report as to the advisability of making by-laws under the Municipal Corpoiations Act. WARRINGTON TRADES COUNCIL. Board of Guardians Criticised.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council was held in Bold street Wesleyan school on Tuesday evening under the presidency of Mr. J. Podmore. There was a fair attendance of delegates.

===== 0002601_19030912_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
"THE HANDY MAN AT THE WORKHOUSE?"

The CHAIRMAN referred to the recent appointment by the Board of Guardians of what they called a" handy man." As far as he could gather this person was to be a plumber, cabinet maker, and joiner combined. He did , not know whether or not he went into the baking line. (laughter)—but he had many duties to perform. The SECRETARY (Mr. F. Stott) said those who read the local press last Saturday week would recognize that the Board of Guardians at their last meeting decided to appoint a man to be called a " handy man.' He did not know whether he was a friend of the Workhouse Master or not, but from reading the discussion it seemed that he was. He had been employed at Southend-on-Sea. Great credit was due to two mem- bers of the Board who opposed the appointment—Mr. Halton and Mr. Ashcroft—on the ground that it would do away with wat he might call tradesmen's work. The man was supposed to take charge of the painting, plumbing and glazing in connection with the Work- house and hospital. Mr. Stott read the following letter from Mr. H. Taylor' secretary of the Warrington branch of the Plumbers' Association :—" On behalf of the Society of Co-operative Plumbers, I am requested to protest against the decisiop of the Warrington Board of Guardians in appointing a " handy man" to execute plumbing work at the Workhouse, more particularly because the rate of wages advertised is considerably lower than the standard rate of wages in Warrington, thereby coming into conflict with one of the funda- mental principles,to our society. It is also objection- able that a "handy man "was not advertised for before the person was selected, thus not offering any oppor- tunity for a local man to apply, or giving our society an opportunity to protest before the appointment was made. Hoping you will take immediate steps to pro- test on our behalf." The painters were not affiliated with this Council, or very likely they would have had a letter on the matter from them. If this Council did not protest they might have the Town Council appoint- ing "handy men." (Laughter.) Mr. BUCKLEY moved that the Secretary be instructed to write to the Clerk of the Board of Guardians pro- testing against the appointment of a "handy man." He was glad the plumbers had written, but it had been his (Mr. Buckley a) intention to bring the matter forward. This was a glaring instance of what they might term "knobsticking." If a public body employed a man who was going to interfere with two or three trades, and who probably knew nothing about them, where was it going to end? If they could not get trade union wage under a public body they could not expect them front private firms. , Mr. EATON seconded the motion. The SECRETARY said one of the arguments in favour of the appointment was that it would reduce expenses because plumbers charged so much for vvalking time. Even if the man was a qualified plumber there was no reason why he should do the painting and receive such small wages. sir bmiTH said it was ridiculous that public money should be spen in such a form. In some cases money was thrown away and when an opportunity of doing good occurred it was withheld. They could not protest too strongly against it, and he would go to extremes to put it down at once. It was stated that the standard rate of wages for plumbers was Bid per hour. The CHAIRMAN agreed with what had been said, and thought Boards of Guardians ought to set a good example inemploying labour and paying good wages. The resolution was unanimously passed. -

LABOUR REPRESENTA.TION COMMITTEE. PROPOSED COMMITTEE.

Mr. BUCKLEY produced the proposed programme of the Warrington Labour Representation Committee as follows :—Municipal I,Trade Union rate of wages and conditions of labour; 2, direct employment of labour wherever possible; 3, construction of artisans' dwellings by corporate bodies; 4, providing of public recreation grounds and branch libraries; 5, freeprimary and secondary education; 6, full and fair assessment of all property; 7, taxation of land values; 8, absolute purity regarding all trading and contracting by public bodies. Parliamentary: 1, to promote immediate and effective legislation defining our Trade Union rights and securing absolute protection for the funds of the various trade organizations; 2,t0 provide fair remunera- tive work for the unemployed; 3, payment of members and returning officers' fees; 4, shorter parliaments; 5, universal suffrage; 6, legal eight-hours day; 6, efficient factory inspection by practical working men and women; 8, graduated income tax and abolition of all indirect taxation ; 8, nationalization of land, minerals, and railways; 10, abolition of hereditary i authorities ; 11, amendment of the Employers' Liability and Com- pensation Acts. Mr. Buckley pointed out that it was an oversight that old-age pensions were not mentioned, but the ques- tion would be included in the programme. Mr. Plumb moved that the discussion on the proposed programme be adjourned until the branches affiliated with the Council bad had an opportunity of discussing the matter. so

Mr. Plinston expressed the opinion that they ought to discuss the matter forthwith. They had not the final settlement of it; that rested with the Labour Representation Committee, on which they had two delegates. On a vote being taken it was agreed to postpone the discussion as suggested.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

~,J.,,...,.,'•,,....,......,.'''''''...^.,,,.....,,...-^

Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible,not later than Thursday. Correspondents are strongly advised to write briefly A short letter has a much greater chance of insertion than a long one. -

MR. CHAMBERLAIN'S TARIFF PROPOSALS.

MR. H. WHITHAM AND THE GREAT INQUEST.

Mr. H. WHITHA.M, of San Francisco, writing on August 24th, says : I have just received a letter from a very dear friend in Warrington, in which he says :—" I have for many years been a convinced Protectionist. Anyway, I have not been satisfied with our present system of absurdly one-sided Free Trade." I know my friend to be a keen observer? and a patriot in the best sense of the word,' and I therefore venture an open letter to him through the "Examiner." One of the cheerful prophecies put forward by the two great leaders of the Free-Trade movement, Cobden and Bright, was the certainty that other nations would very soon carry out the same policy. This optimistic opinion has not been realized, not, even in the smallest degree. Instead of this, England has seen the other nations fighting keenly for their share of the world's commerce. To these have been added her Colonies, and the com- petitive struggle has been waged all around on the lines of Protection. So far the prediction has not been fulfilled. The plea put forward- by America was that she must build up her "infant industries." The same must be said for the Colonies The " infant " of the Stated is now of a robust build, and with the Colonial bantlings is causing the poor old parent, John Bull, much anxiety. That there is cause for anxiety is to me somewhat doubtful. England could not reasonably expect to remain the world's workshop. • Her position in the industrial world is peculiar if not unique. She has to defend herself from protective Germany, and yet that country, and the whole of the protected European cou- anent has an industrial population that cannot begin to compare in the matter of wage-earning with Great Britain. Mark Warren in the "Contemporary " says :—" From 1880 to 1890 Germany was England's most dangerous rival, but thelyear 1891 , saw a prodigous decrease of no less than £123,115,000 in the total general trade of that country. It willnot fail to be noticed that in 1892 the value of the foreign trade of the United States exceeded that of Germany by ten million pounds. In the follow- ing year America dropped to the fourth place among the trading nations, but in 1900 it displaced France.' it may be no consolation to the Englishman to know that he is not alone in his troubles, still if he is made aware of the fact that his heels are in danger of being trodden on Mlle does not wake up and keep awake some good oi a sort,"may result. Now between England and the States the situation is very different and it is here where Mr. Chamberlain will make his greatest and best showing. When the States- man has rid his imaginative mind of the happy family idea, and has done what I certainly think should be done by the whole of his race, that is,allow the Colonies to take their own course, neither attempt by law,nor yet by treaty to do the impossible feat of persuading a people to pay a shilling for what in the open market can be bought for sixpence he will turn to the States for hie examples. This may sound like little England- ism. lam sure if Great Britain were at once to declare a future policy of independence to each one of her colonies for the mere asking she would at once stand inameasurably higher than she at present dreams of. The English speaking race would become a unit, and would clear the world of all the word competition means, and what that means wouldfill a very big book. America, .what does the word mean? and what does it comprehend? It means more than the imagination can conceive of. It means the Western Hemisphere, the fourth quarter of the world, now politically, divided into two nations, yet destined to become the one great home of the English speaking race, and the civilizing force of the world. England's supposed troubles came in part from North America. Well, my advice is don't' worry over what the United States is doing. The iudustrial problem is as difficult a problem to solve there as it is in any other part of the industrial world. If lam any judge I should say that before we are very much older we shall see some startling events in the United States. Looking first at the trusts I must say that I see nothing very startling in them. They have done away with an I immense number of middle-men So far that is satisfactory, it is what we have been aiming at for a long number of years. If they do not sell as cheaply as they might, dont get mad about it, they will by and by. You cannot legislate against the trust until you find the trust that isa monopoly. Some are promoting industrial progress in the highest degree and others are holding it back. In some there is a fair capitalization, and in others there is an over-capitaliza- tion that is simply frandulent. The Standard Oil Company is the nearest approach to a monopoly, still it has competitors. When you ap- proach the trust with restrictive legislation in mind, it is very like approaching the tariff question. Where will you make the beginning and where will you end? Several trusts have• recently gone to the wall, and a number have had to be reorganized. In time the trust will .olve the whole trouble about labour and capital and coupled with the scientific results of the day, will bring about the age of socialism. The mere socialist will never do it. A book has just been published, the title of which is,"The history of the Carnegie Steel Co." This passage occurs in the introduction. No great business has yet been built up upon the beatitudes and it is not all cynicism that condenses a negative decalogue into a positive exhortation to be successful somehow." This book sells for twenty and twenty-five pounds a copy. In this history of the Carnegie Steel Company one may read Mr. Schwab's statement that steel rails could be produced at 12do1s. a ton at a time they were costing the consumer 28do1s. Here, indeed, it would appear has been forged a weapon which may have deadly effect when placed in the hands of the opponents 'of the "tariff protected trusts." One may read of a division of profits running as high as eighty-eight per cent. to the members of the Carnegie Association. Mr. Bridge, the author of the book, was once private secretary to Mr. Carnegie. This steel com- pany is now one of the merged and forms a part of the American Steel and Iron Trust. It had until just recently fa million-dollars-a-year-man as its President, Mr.Schwab.We arehaving ouileyes opened in the States, and from the inside the information comes. What is the result? Never was a country so troubled with strikes. It would be utterly impossible to name a line of busi- ness in which the men and women were unorganizedVe are not only fighting the capitalists, but we are as keen as ever the Kilkenny cats were, in fighting each other. Yes, prosperity strikes are in order all over the United States. The workers are saying that Protection makes the individual a millionaire, but they remain as poor as ever. I must say that it seems to me to be quite true that with all the high wages a very large propor- tion of the wage earners are living from hand to mouth. I was talking with a fellow workman whose wages average about two pounds fifteen a week. He has only his wife and self to keep-and no rent to pay, and yet he assured me he could not save one, cent. There is now sitting a very interesting arbitration court The car men a this city asked for 12s. a day of eight hours. This was referred to a Court. Getting 10s. a day these men could not only not save, but could net keep out of debt. I associate with some of the most prominent leaders of Labour associations and to a man they protest against Protection as it is carried out in the States. I also study the labour papers and rarely find a favourable opinion expressed of it. All agree that Protection is but a millionaire-making machine, and they are also agreed that Protection is the greatest obstacle in the way of International Federation of the labouring classes.We have seen how Socialism is going forward by leaps and bounds in Germany. Surely this is not a result of the high wages received by the German workman and yet the growth of wealth in Germany in the past decade has been vast. Prussia, in the returns for the past year shows sixty incomes exceeding £50,000 sterling annually. In Berlin in 1899 there were only seven men worth one and a half millions. To-day there are eighteen. Four Berliners own from two to seven millions; twenty-one from one to one and one-half millions. Krupp left thirteen and a half million sterling. The growth of German wealth is due to the astonishing expansion of commerce and industry. Yes! Expansion of commerce and industry, but the millionaire-making machine gets in its heartless work all the same, and leaves the work- man poor as ever. I your readers could only have, what I cannot take up space with giving, a sketch of smuggling and its I kinship to Protection as it is daybyday given in the I American press, they would be surprised. The greatest THE WARRINGTON SOCIETYI culprits are the rich. This is scarcely the worst feature of it. Quite a large army of Custom House officers are maintained 0? carry out its ridiculous provisions, and Proposed Memorial Tablet to Dr. the appointment of these officials, and the way they get their political work in exemplify the very worst Priestley. features of corruption. In a word, Protection is in my opinion the most demoralizing, the most degrading, and A circular has just been issued by the above Society the saddest corruption-breeding institution a nation can from which we learn that the executive propose to possibly have within its borders. commemorate the centenary of the death of Dr. I When I say this I still urge the greatest and widest of priestley, the discoverer of oxygen, which takes place , friendly discussion in the great inquest the nation is on the 6th of January next, by erecting on his residence I now holding. " Simple and Healthful Food."

I shall be glad to send a copy of my booklet bearing the above title, post free to any of your readers sending a penny stamp: This pamphlet ,has been compiled with a view of assisting that section of our people whose income is very limited. Economists tell us that almost 30 per cent, of our population is underfed, and underfed to the extent, it is estimated, of 25 per cent. This booklet of simple and healthful food contains 65 recipes for soups, savouries, and sweets, and shows how the daily fare may be enlarged by a more varied and appetising diet, and With lessened expenditure. During the present time of distress it is hoped this little com- pilation may be of service.—Yours faithfully, ALBERT BROADBENT. The Vegetarian Society; 19, Oxford-street, Manchester.

MR. CROSFIELD AND THE ENGADINE GOLF CLUB.

Distribution of Prizes: The Speeches.

At the conclusion of the annual tournament on the St. ' -Moritz links, Mr. A. H. Crosfield entertained all his golfing and other friends to a sumptuous tea on the big rink. All the arrangements, says the "Alpine Post and Engadine Express," were carried out to perfection by Herr A. Janssen, and the result was that everyone hada jolly good time. The tables were artistically and prettily decorated with flowers, and the glorious sun shine lent a lustre to thescene. After tea the Captain made a short speech, in which he dwelt on the prosperity of the Club and the great increase in the popularity of the game in the Engadine; and then introduced Lady Sassoon, who had graciously consented to distribute the prizes. Atthe conclusion of this interesting ceremony, Mr. A. H. Lloyd proposed a vote of thanks to Lady Sassoon for being present and acting as prize giver on this auspicious occasion. Though she is no golfer at present, he said, she had promised to become one next year. The vote of thanks was passed with loud acclamations. Sir Edward Sassoon in reply said: "Now while I beg to express to Mr. Lloyd my most grateful appreciation of his great kindness to my wife I think I shall invokeyour warmest sympathy on being called upon to make a speech. (Laughter) It is rather hard lines on a husband while his wife is to have the credit. (Renewed laughter) I'do not laiew how to-ex- • press to our most generous and distinguished host, Mr. Crosfield, our thanks for this most delightful banquet. Ilthink it is worthy of St. Moritz, and worthy of any garden party in London orlin New York. (Hear hear and cheers). Now, Ladies and Gentlemen golf is not all beer and skittles. (No). We who are duffers are at most beginners at the game and have found that out to our cost in the last few days. I think our hearts should go out in gratitude to thecommitteewhohave organised theseand the Samaden competitions,andwe cordially thank them for"the very great attention they have given to the game we love year after year. (Cheers.) Golf as we know requires a great deal of concentration. I think , my friend Mr. Crosfield will bear me out in that, for he told me his misfortunes in the mixed foursomes to-day were due to his temporary lack of concentration. (Much laughter.) It is a game that leads to emphatic and sometimes even vehement language. (Laughter.) That reminds me of a little caddie boy who was brought before a judge for using strong language. The judge said to the boy, "Why, you are very young, where could you have learned such language?" The boy answered, "I was caddie to your Lordship for two years." (Loud laughter.) Sir Edward then congratulated the winners. Mr. A. H. Crosfield then spoke as follows :—"Before we separate I think there is one vote of thanks which ought to be passed, and I have also an announcement to make which you will be pleased to hear. Our Captain, Mr. Ted Lloyd, has undertaken the duties for another year. (Loud cheers.) I do not know that I can adequately express what is due to Mr. Lloyd for the indefatigable and tireless energy with which he has worked for the Club this year in his position as Captain. (Cheers.) And yet that seems a little strange because, as Sir Edward Sassoon has re- minded us in his apt and charming anecdote, golf in one respect is bound to help all of us. Whatever it may or may not do for us I am sure all who are present, not excepting even those Members of the House of Commons who are limited to Parliamentary language, will agree that it is calculated to extend one's vocabul- ary. (Laughter.) Well, however limited my own vocabulary unfortunately may be, I have at any rate had some experience of the work that is done by a Captain of this Club, because since 1894, when golf was in its infancy at St. Moritz and Samaden—excepting two years when I was trav'elling in America—l have been here every season. (Hear,hear.) That does enable .one to judge of a Captain's duties in this club and makes one appreciate the more the tact, decision, courtesy and immense amount of real hard work which Mr. Ted Lloydhas put in this year,and I will add,put in without an atom of fuss or a word of complaint.l am very glad my friend Sir Edward Sassoon has so kindly referred to the work done bylthe committee,captain and the secre lary, and, ladies and gentlemen' perhaps some of you scarcely realise how much work has to be done in order that season by season we may enjoy our game out here on these links and at Samaden. Mr. Lloyd has been through the mill as secretary and he knows the ropes. (Hear, hear.) We all appreciate therefore very greatly the advantage it has been this year to have him as Captain, and gratitute being a sense of further favours to come we look forward to having him at the head of .our Club next year. (Cheers.) There is no doubt this , season's golf has been a great success, and just as in the I case of the great and flourishing club at Cannes, so here it is pleasant to see many nationalities represented and meeting together in friendly rivalry. Among many other members we are always pleased to see upon our I links may I just say how delighted we are to have amongst us the popular and genial President of the Club at La Boulie. (Loud cheers.) It was a most happy idea of his that we should have that friendly little match at Samaden the other day between the two clubs, and, Monsieur Deschamps, we think it an equally happy suggestion of yours that we should send a team representing this Club later on to try conclusions with you at Paris. (Cheers.) We shall be very pleased indeed to break a Janne with you on your native heath. I have been to La Boulie and I know that the President has worked to develop that Club for the benefit of Paris. Do youknow the definition of a President's duty? He is expected to speak honey, spend money, and look bonny. (Much laughter.) Who else could so perfectly fill that position (Cheers.) Well, whoever wins, there is nothing pleasanter than these inter-club matches, and our rencontre with La Bonne has been by no means the least pleasant event in a very pleasant season. I can only repeat that the success of our season is in a very large degree, in fact mainly owing to the tireless energy and good judgment of our Ceptain, backed up so ably as he had been by our Committee and our valued seem- tary, and I therefore ask you to give our friend Ted Lloyd a most emphatic and cordial vote of thanks. (Loud Cheers.) Mr. Desc.hamps, President of the Societe de Golf de Paris (La Boulie) speaking in French, thanked Mr. Crosfield for his kind remarks, and declared that even if he and some of his countryraenhad been able to start La Boulie and make it a successful club, they owed it first of all to their English and American friends who were their greatest supporters when the scheme was set on foot. Ile thought they owed a great deal to the encouragement and advice of Mr. Crosfield and other well-wishers who hoped to see La Boulie develope into a successful club.

A model circular saw had been balanced on the hub of the front wheel, and surrounding it were pretty fretwork designs. The back wheel con- tained a large octagonal combination of fretwork photo frames and a delicately-constructed but large size lamp was attached to the front of the machine. Miss Davies, of Heatley, looked well as the Harvest Queen, her dress and machine being fringed with corn and flowers. Then there were a brigand's wife, an Indian huntress, and a girl with dress covered with small dolls to represent "La Poupde." Mr. John Hampson, of Warrington, had a very ingenious turnout to illustrate the work of the Post Office. His bicycle was profusely adorned with letters, picture post cards and rainature mail bags, and a small mail van was also introduced. As "The Stamp Queen " Miss Whitney, of Warrington, wore a costume covered with 20,000 postage stamps. Mr. T. Jackson, of Tyldesley, carried off a prize as the " Handy man." He was clad in sailor costume and his beautifully deco- rated machine was surmounted with models of the ancient and up-to-date ships of war. Mr. J. D. Payne. of Bolton, a wall-known parader, was effectively costumed as the Venetian Gondolier and his machine was prettily decorated with real flowers. His brother—Mr. William Payne—sent a finely-constructed model of the" Victory." Considerahle amusement was created by the representation of a schoolmaster, wear- ing cap and gown, who frequently appeared to adminis- ter chastisement to a refractory pupil. Mr. P. Stirock, of Newton Heath, had a, beautiful costume as "King of Spain" and Miss Lowe, of Liverpool, WM a prize- winner with a dress completely covered with red spangles. As "Ivy" Miss Warburton was charmingly costumedlin a dress decked with the familiar ivy leaves. Mr.S.Eastwood,of Newton Heath,was the cause of many a ripple of merriment. His machine had been fitted up as a handcart and between the shafts was a" dummy' woman who was wheeling her drunken husband home. The bicycle was skilfully manipulated and it was a source of wonder to some of the spectators that the venturesome rider did not come to grief. Prom what has already been said it will be concluded that the cyclists rose to the occasion and their display quite realized the anticipations of the townspeople. Then there was a host of excellent characters on foot. One gigantic figure—" Sunny Jim" to wit—stalked about on stilts. He seemed thorotighly at home at such an elevation and shook hands familiarly with policemen

They had had great difficulty in judging as there were so many excellent turnouts. The MAYOn said that very great credit was due to the judges for the pains they had taken and for the excel- lent judgment which those present believed they had given. If there had been double the number of prizes, candidates would have been found for them without any difficulty. In conclusion, the Mayor thanked Mr. Davies, and all who had helped to make the demonstra- tion a credit to Warrington. (Applause). The artistes were heartily thanked for their services, and the Mayor and Mayoress for their presence.
