Mr. H. P. Hughes and the Cobden Club

MR. H. P. HUGHES writes : I see that Mr. Harold Cox,—the able and irrepressible Seoretary of the nationally-moribund Cobden Club—is taking up my letter of a fortnight ago. True, his reply' is a bit belated, but then that is perhaps symbolical of an Institution which is, to-day, said to be at least twenty years behind progressive Britishpublic opinion Of course, I should have felt far more flattered-by Mr: Cox's attentions, if his letter had been drafted for my- self aloae; but find that,—with the same spirit of exem- plary thrift, which animates the housewife who concocts to-day's dinner out Of the leavings from yesterday—Mr. Cox has only served me with a "hash." In other words his Warrington letter is only a fragment from another letter of his, dated 22nd August, and published in the "Manchester Guardian." Withyour permission,l will reproduce part of this letter to the Manchester Guardian"; "Mr. J. H. Bottomley, who describes himself as a Conservative Agent,has stated in the press, that the Cobden Club is largely composed of foreigners. The statement is absolutely false. The long list of names he gives, is apparently taken from an old list of honorary members of the Club. These gentlemen were elected honorary members, for their services [praetical results, nil] in promoting the cause of Free-Trade in their own country. If their efforts were successful,foreign countries would be thrown open to British trade--[tweedle-durn; tweedle-dee]. They were therefore assisting the very cause in which English Protectionists profess to believe, and in which English Free-Traders really do believe,naraely thecause [please call it "dream "] of universal Free-Trade. So far as the pxrely English work of the club is concerned, these foreign honorary members have absolutely nothing to do with it. They pay no subscription, and have no voice [if they had ,d a voice," they would sing " Some- day,someday, someday you will awaken, Bull 1 in deter- mining the policy of the Club. Mr.l Bottomley's sug- gestion that these foreign gentlemen are making tools • of Radical Trade Unionists and Co-operators, and no I doubt, paying a lot of them, is therefore,as false as it is I malicious." I The wailing, minor key of the above letter is very pathetic. Alas poor Cobden Club! After a long period I of strutting and crowing, it has survived itself, and has I lived to be laughed at in London and [pitied in the pro- vinces. All history tells us that nation, or party, or cause, that falls back on the defensive is—lost. The Cobden Club, at the beginning of the present fiscal campaign was proudly aggressive; to-day, it is I figlating even for its British locus stancli. The prestige of the Club is gone for ever. A little more worldly wisdom at the Cobden Mub, would have at least "saved the face" of an institution which—in the past —had won a large measure of popular respect. The cult of Free-Trade has now split itself upon the rock of Imperialism; and therobden Club is much like a cer- tain historical old lady, who tried to keep back the advancing tide with her broom. To the future historian, the term Free-Trade will be a mere phrase, or label, or idiom' to mark an experimental epoch in British trad- ing. An epoch of sixty or seventy years is, after all, only a very brief chapter in the history of an Empire. What is the use of the Cobden Club thinking it can stem the rising tide of British business-opinion, as in- dicated, for instance by the following extract from the "Daily Telegraph" of 25th August :—" At a largely attended meeting, yesterday, under the auspices of the Liverpool Chamber of Commerce, Sir Alfred Jones pre- siding, a resolution was adopted expressing the opinion that it was necessary an inquiry should be held into the fiscal system of the country. Mr. J. IL Welsford, Unionist candidate for the Crewe Division, delivered an address on Imperial commerce, from a practical point of view, and maintained that, commercially and Imperially, it would be to England's advantage to make some alteration in the fiscal system." Time tests all methods; and time has—during the last few years— abundantly demonstrated the fact that, in its fully. matured harvest, the Free-trade seed has brought forth, to the British capitalist and workman, thorns—in place of the figs that were, at the planting time, sixty or seventy years ago, so profusely prophesied. The British Nation has—in the present fiscal cam- paign—nothing to thank the Cobden Club for, in the matter of candour, or of disinterested help in arriving at a practical solution of our current bad-trade problems. The Cobden Club gives the impression that it would far rather that the British nation should be ruined,, than that onejot or oneltittle of obsolete Free-Trade theories should suffer. It is a selfish policy, and will bring its own reward. Originally created to foster freedom of fiscal opinion, the CM is now one of the main instru- ments in trying to stifle it. What changes of relative attitude time brings forth, and how history keeps repeating itself. Protection is, to-day, gaining ground —in Britain—as rapidly as Free-Trade did, say, sixty years ago, and the Conservatives of to-day are taking the place, as attacking forces' of the Cobdenites of over half a century ago. Every day that passes by, the i Cobden Club s losing adherents—particularly among the business communities—and Protection is gaining volume, and please remember that volume of public opinion means momentum, and momentum means a practical victory at the polls. Since drafting the above, I have seen the published letter of Mr. Harold Cox, to Mr. Chamberlain. under date 31st August, upon the same old topic of foreign membership of the Cobden Club. Poor Mr. Cox is having abusy time of it, in explaining away this alleged cosmopolitan feature of the Club. It will be interest- ing to see how Mr. Chamberlain deals with the Club. The incident is about as grotesque as if my little terrier-dog, "Toby," challeneedan elephant to "explain himself." Indeed the disparity is even greater,between Mr. Chambeslain's position, as the leading Statesman of i the British Empire and a club which has apparently lost not only the confidence but the esteem of a large Iportion of the business public. I P.2.—As an illustration of the way in which the club ; is being snubbed, at iteown door, I append a copy of a letter which appeared in the "Pall Mall Gazette "and "Daily Telegraph "Itif 25th August last : COBDEN CLUB AND SIR H. VINCENT. Sir,—The Cabden Club is wise in declining the challenge of Sir Howard Vincent,and in endeavour- to confine the issue to the question of a food tax, a question in whioh the unthinking masses are easily gulled. The Cobden Club dares not face a practical test on main issues, and it is not the first time it has declined' a challenge. It doubtless has a whole- some recollection of an incident which occurred about sixteen years ago, when Mr. Williamson challenged it to send a short circular to the princi- pal representative exporters in our chief manufac- turing centres, asking them what had been their experience as to the incidence of duties. The challenge was declined by the Cobden Club, so Mr. Williamson sent out the circular on his own account, with the result that out of 531 replies to the circular, all except one admitted that the bur- den of the duty fell upon them, and not on the con- sumer. I have already in previous letters laid before the public a large number of instances in which the imposition of a duty has not been followed by an incense of price in the article taxed; space will not admit of a repetition of these, but I may, per- haps, be allowed to cite the following cases in which it is evident that the burden of the duty has not fallen on the consumer. The Manager of the Barrow Steel Works stated, in evidence before the Royal Commission on the Depression of Trade, that in 'one year, 1884, his Company had paid £160,000 in duties to the United States, yet, under the heavy duty, the price of steel rails had fallen from £ll2 per ton in 1873 to £37 in 1885. • Again, the duty on tin-plates in the United States was raised in 1891 from PO to 2.2 cents. per pound; this, according to the Cobden Club reasoning. should 'have raised the price from 35 to 4-7 cents. per pound; but, on the contrary, the price fell (under the stimulus of home competition, as the industry developed in the United States) to 2-6 cents. in 1894, the imports of tin-plates continuing, though in diminished quantities, during the development of the industry, the British manufacturer paying the duty.—Yotirs Scc., (Signed) GUILFORD L. MOLESWORTH. The Manor House, Bexley, August 31st.

SUCCESSFUL DEMONSTRA.TION AT WARRINGTON.

Picturesque Procession,

Nothing appeals more powerfully to the sympathies of the average Britisher than heroism. He loves to read of men who face unflinchingly the greatest risks and are ready, if need be, to sacrifice their own lives in an effort to save others. We are all proud of the gallant fellows who man the lifeboats round the coasts of our "tight little island." Who is there who has not at some time been thrilled by the narration of deeds of true heroism and endurance enacted by the lifeboat crews? Warrington people, at any rate, have shown that they have a warm admiration for these men who "occupy their business in great waters," and once more it has been proved that an appeal on behalf of a really deserving cause has only to be made to the townspeople to call forth a ready and hearty response. Some months ago the idea of holding a demonstration to aid the ;Royal National Lifeboat Institution was mooted, and all along willing assistance has been forthcoming to ensure that the event should be thoroughly worthy of the town. Doubtless this was to be accounted for by the know- ledge of the fact that the whole of the money raised would be devoted entirely to the payment of lifeboat- men,grants to their widows and children, and like purposes. The Mayor and Mayoress willingly lent their powerful influence in support of the movement. Strong committees were soon gat to work—and be it at once said that they have discharged their varied duties with an assiduity which is deserving of cordial recognition. Saturday witnessed the consummation of their labours and the dulness of our streets was, for the nonce, relieved by a pictnresque pageant, which in a town of the size of Warrington, would have been difficult to excel. The demonstration was as success- ful as the most sanguine individual would have ven- tured to predict. As the procession wended its way through the crowded thoroughfares expressions of pleasure and appreciation were heard on all hands. It seemed to be the general opinion that Warrington has never seen anything better in the way of fancy dress pageantry, and the promoters must take it for granted that they have the thanks of the spectators for pro- viding such a display: Our fickle cl.'

THE OFFICIALS.

A more energetic and efficient baud of cacials than those who were responsible for the arrangements it would be impossible to find. The Mayor, of course, was elected to the presidency, and the Mayoress fulfilled the duties of president of the lathes' auxiliary. Miss Fairclough, Gatesgazth, Latchford,'was a capable hon. secretary of the ladies' auxiliary; Mr. Edwin Bradshaw rendered valuable,assistanceas hon.treasurer of the local fund, and in Mr. Stanley Wilson, 'a painstaking hon. secretary was found. A special mead of praise must be accorded to Mr. Wilson upon whom arduous duties de- volved and were performed most admirably. Mr. D. Davies worked hard as chairman of the General Com- mittee and other useful officials were :—Chairman of the Prize and Novelty Committee, Councillor Wilkin- son hon. secretary, Mr. H. B. Houghton ; Chairman of the Finance Committee, Mr.J. H. Smethurst,hon. secre- tary, Mr. J. Pickles; Chairman of the Collectors' Com- mittee, Councillor John Roberts", hon. secretaries. Messrs. W. THE PROCEEDS.

The total proceeds cannot yet be ascertained,but it is roughly estimated that they will amount to a little over £3OO. It is intended to hand over about £250 to the Royal National Lifeboat Institution and the balance will be retained as a local fund to be available for future demonstrations. SPECIAL SERVICE AT ST. PAUL'S CHURCH. •

A special service was held in St. Paul's Church on Sunday evening, when a collection was made in aid of the funds of the Lifeboat Institution. There was a large congregation, and hymns appropriate to the occa- sion weresung. The choir gave an excellent interpre- tation of the hymn "Sun of my soul," which was sung as an anthem. The sermon was preached by the Vicar, the Rev. W. Bracecamp, from the words They used helps."—Acts xxvii., 17. In appealing for funds for the Lifeboat Institution, the preacher said they could spend their hundreds on a football match ; indeed, in one town very often, on a Saturday afternoon, the sum of from £4OO to £5OO was taken at the gate. He was not saying a word against football, bat svrely they had got their heads screwed on right, and if they were willing to give £4OO or £5OO for pleasure, they ought to be prepared to give something to those who were willing to sacrifice their all for 'the sake of their fellow-men. The first lifeboat was, he believed, built in 1785, and ever since that time God had stirred up the hearts of various men to build other lifeboats, and now we had a considerable number. Still more were required, and they could not get them because they could not get the money. If, however, some of their friends who were so fond of football would give up the receipts in aid of infirmaries and other similar institutions what a grand example they wouldset ! He believed the grand old town would show to other towns even larger than herself that she did well on Saturday. The collection amounted to .£8 Bs. 9d. THE STOCKTON HEATH PROTEST.

At a meeting of the Prize and Novelty Committee last evening under the presidency of Councillor Wilkin- son, a protest lodged by Stockton Heath Club against the award of the prize for the largest turnout in cost- ume to the Royal Club was considered. After hearing the statements of Mr. Mao Alpine (Secretary of the Stockton !Heath Club), and Mr. J. Newton (Assistant Secretary of the Royal Club) it was decided that the Royal Club should retain the prize. The Chairman, however, offered to give a consolation prize to the Stockton Heath organization.

MUNICIPAL UNDERTAKINGS AND

DEPRECIATION.

The report on the Glasgow Corporation telephones is, says "Engineering," the first which gives a financial statement for a complete year. Some of its statements are, no doubt, gratifying to the advocates of municipal telephones in Glasgow, and we find that at the end of May of the present year the number of completed in- struments had reached the good total of 9,122, which by a curious coincidence is identical with the number of instruments in use on the London Post Office system. But there is only left, after all charges are met out of gross income, the sum of £1,694 is. 81d., and the committee, adding this to a "surplus " of £399 7s. 10A. ~:brought forward from last year. proposed that the total of £2092 9s. 7d. should be carried •into a general reserve fund. No allowance whatever has been made for depreciation, and the auditors, a firm of accountants of very high standing in Glasgow, make a report, attached to their certificate of the correctness of the accounts, which contains the following statement:—" We have to point out that no depreciation has yet been provided for, nor has any portion of the sum standing at the debit of preliminary and general expenses, in capital account been written off out of revenue. . . . In our opinion the time has now come when the canvassing expenses should form a charge against revenue." One would have thought continues "Engineering," that, coming before the Council with proposals on which such strictures had been passed by the professional accountants, the chairman of committee would have shown cause why his proposals should be accepted in spite of these objections; but this he did not attempt to do. Having once stated the proposal recommended in the report, he simply added that "whether this course should be I adopted, or whether it should be applied to depreciation was a point which he would leave in the hands of the Council." But bad finance and condemnation by account tants is as nothin,76 to the average town councillor, compared with the dread of showing a deficit in any of the trading concerns of his municipality; and so all criticism was brushed aside and the report adopted by a large majority. It will be remembered that the Post Office license to the Corporation only extends to Decem- ber 31, 1913, at which date, or as soon as may be thefe- after, " the Postmaster-General shall buy, and the local authority shall sell," the undertaking at the " fair market value" at the time of transfer ; which will be very much lessthanithe capital sum which has been expen- ded on the plant. Only two items need be mentioned. ltappeared in the discussion that the telephone engineer had been asked to state what he considered the fair period for the life of a switchboard, and had put it at ten years; so that one item alone, of £lO,OOO the cost of the present switchboard, should, on that basis, be wholly written off by 1913. Another point worthy of special notice is that the plant was largely bought at the time when there was a commercial boom, and prices were high, so that copper and iron alone cost 20 per cent. more than they do at present. This was shown by one councillor (Bailie Burrell) to represent a fall in value already of £ll,OOO. But the Corporation—or, at least, the majority—profess to believe that they are putting aside enough for all these items in their pay- ments to the sinking fund.

ST. HELENS VOLUNTEER'S DEATH, Accident at Camp.

Mr. Brighouse, county coroner, held the inquest a,t St. Helens Town Hall, on Monday on James Miller, aged thirty-three, of 18, Gladstone-street, who died on Sunday morning from injuries alleged to have been caused while working in the camp of the 2nd Lancashire Royal Engineers (Volunteers.) Captain Sayce acting adjutant, attended. Mrs. Miller said her husband went with the regiment to camp on August 2nd, and returned on the following Sunday. He told her he had hurt himself on the Wed- nesday, and when he went to the doctor he was told it was only slight.—The Coroner: Who was the doctor? —lnspector Goodall: I believe it was Dr. Monks. Lieutenant Haslam Fax, who was in charge of deceased's company, produced a model of the work the men were engaged on at the time of the accident. Sergeant Sefton explained to the jury by means of the model that a trestle bridge had been erected, and projected for soma distance on to the shore. The com- pany were detailed for barrel piering, which consisted of lashing barrels together to form a raft on which to construct a bridge. The men marched on to the bridge, and, in order to get quickly down on to the sands, they went down a rope from the bridge. Witness went first, and when he got down he held the rope while the men came down. Before they had all descended he left the rope in charge of Corporal Livesey, but it didnot seem to be known who was holding it when deceased-came down. Just as he got his weight on to the rope itiwent slack, and Miller banged with his side up against the end of one of the planks. Lieutenant Fox said he watched the men come down the rope. About fifty descended. In two cases a couple- of men were on the rope together. Sapper Miller was an enthusiastic, hard-working Volunteer, and he got on the rope before the preceding man had got clear, and when the man jumped off the rope it went slack, and Miller's ribs caught on the end of the planks. He did not complain until later in the week. The Coroner said it was a simple matter. Dr. Knowles had certified that it was only a slight fracture. It was well known that people could knock about with a frac- tured rib without knowing that they had seriously in- jured themselves. Captain Sayce expressed the regret of Colonel Murray and the officers and men of the corps at the sad accident and said the circumstances had been reported to the officer commanding the district, and the corps would do all they could for the widow.' The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental deathi" and expressed their appreciation of the kind promige made by Captain Sayce.

Music in the Park.

Weather permitting, the stand will be occupied by- Crosfields' prize band to-morrow (Sunday). The fol- lowing programme of music will be rendered : March, "Honest Toil," W. Rimmer ; grand fantasia, "Joan of Arc," H. Round; selection, Mercadante," H. Round; chorus, "Worthy is the Lamb," and "Amen," Handel:; selection, 11 Seraglio," Mozart ; hymn, " Sabbath," W. Halliwell ; "God Save the King." Conductor, Mr. W. Halliwell. bandmaster, Ist V.B.M.R. Wigan Rifles.

===== 0002601_19030912_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
Ambulance Competition.

The annual competition for the -Argenta Ambulance Challenge Cup took place on Saturday at the parade. ground at the Blackpool Police station. There were 15 entries, the competing teams being from Preston Accrington, Lancaster, Warrington, Brierfield,Nelson, Kendal, Radcliffe, Rochdale, Crompton,Bacup,Rawten- stall, Buxton, Dukinfield, and Birclafield (Derbyshire). The judges were Surgeon-Major Massinghain and Dr. Wheelton Hind, both of Stoke-on Trent. The compe- tition which was conducted in private, occupied close upon eight hours, each team taking nearly half an hour for their test. which comprised examinatione both theoretical and practical in first aid work. At the close, of the competition the judges announced that the winners of the Argenta Cup were Brierfield, and the Mayor of Blackpool formally presented the cup. and a gold medal as well to each member of the win- ing squad.

' Bradford.—Ward, back; Sinton, Mosby, F. Coofier, and Dechan, three-quarter back; Marsden and Surman, half backs; Laidlaw, Turner, Barker, Rees, Sharratt, Grayson, Smailes and Upton; forwards. Widnes.—Hudson, back; Peacock, Brown, Tilley, and Barber, three-quarter backs; A. Taylor and Laney, half backs; Aspey, Cockram, Leyshon, Lynch, Webster, Power, McDonald, and Rigby, forwards. Referee, Mr. P. F. Farrar (Halifax). Touch judges Messrs. T. Barnes (Warrington) and J. Graham (St. Helens). Widnes won the toss, and the Chemicals assumed the aggressive, Ward being sorely troubled to keep his lines secure. After the game had been in progress for about five minutes, Dechan intercepted a pass in his own "25," and running strongly evaded all opposition and scored a fine try behind the posts. Mosby had no difficulty in adding two more points. On resuming hostilities Widnes returned to the attaok, and a fine round of ',slang by the whole of the hacks ended in Peacock scoring at the corner flag. Tilley kicked a goal. Just before the interval Sinton received a pass from Mosby on the half- way line, and beating all his opponents scored underthe posts. Mosby added another goal. Half-time score : Bradford 2 goals, 2 tries (10 points), Widnes 1 goal, 1 try (5 points). On re-starting Bradford attacked, and a bad mull by the home custodian nearly resulted in Marsden getting over. Play settled down at the centre,and then Dechan gathered the ball in fine style aud scored from nearly the half-way line. Mosby placed a goal. A few minutes later, Mosby made a very tricky run and added another try. This time he failed to kick a goal. Just before the whistle blew Marsden scoreda fifth try, and Mosbyadded another goal. Result :—Bradford 4 goals, 5 tries (23 points), Widnes 1 goal, 1 try (5 points). SWINTON v. HUDDERSFIELD. Beautiful weather favoured the\opening of the season at Swinton, and 9,000 spectators witnessed a game full of interest from start to finish. In the early stages the visitors were penalised and Preston landed a goal. Succeeding this the Huddersfield team pressed strongly, their backs combined splendidly, and Winpenny was only tackled dn the line; a narrow escape for Swinton. Winpenny and Deere had extremely hard lines, and the climax of ill-luck came when the first-named dashed over only to be ruled in touch-in-goal. This experience again befel the dashing Yorkshire flyer, and Swinton were very lucky to lead at the interval one goal to nil. On resuming, Swinton had the benefit of the wind, and they quickly attacked. Three minutes from the re-start Albert Valentine scored between the posts after good work by Ryan and Taylor. Preston landed a goal, and shortly afterwards Bennett put on a second try for the home club, the result of a bull-dog rush by Evans. Preston added another couple of points. So far in this half Huddersfield had had the worst of matters,but they made no determined efforts to score, and Marsden at length dashed over, but Holroyd failed at goal. Result: —Swinton three goals, two tries (12 points) ; to Huddersfield one try (3 points). . HUNSIXT v. WIGAN. Hunslet opened the season in fine style against Wigan at Parkside. A neat bit of passing led up to Hunslet's first try. A. Goldthorpe transferred the ball to H. Whiteley. and from him it went to W. NORTHEgN RUGBY UNION. Meeting of the General Committee. The monthly meeting of the General Committee of the Northern Union was held at the Grosvenor Hotel, ; Manchester, on Tuesday evening, Mr. Robert Collinge I (Rochdale) presiding. Radcliffe Rangers asked per- mission to withdraw from the qualifying round of the Northern Union cup ties. This the committee decided to allow' subject to the club drawn against Radcliffe Rangers being agreeable. The Wigan and District Junior League was elected a member of the Northern Union. I It was resolved that clubs mutually agreeing to play 12-a side in friendly matches were quite in order. LANCASHIRE COUNTY MEETING. COUNTY TRIAL "MATCH TO BE PLAYED AT WARRDIGTON. Following the meeting of the General Committee, the Lancashire County Union Committee met under the chairmanship of Mr. R. Collinge (Rochdale). The Dalton White Star F.C. and the Wigan and District Juniors' League were admitted to membership of the Union. Mr. A. Edwards, referee in the match Radcliffe Rangers v. Mara Soap Works, reported J. Foden of the former club, and W.tCrippin of the latter, for using bad language to him. Each of the players named was sus- pended up to and including September 19th. It was resolved to play the County trial match at Warrington on October 12th,. It was decided to play the Lancashire v. Cheshire match on October 28th at Broughton, and the Lancashire v. Cumberland fixture on January 13th, at Wigan. NORTHERN UNION LEAGUE. A meeting of the committee of the Northern Rugby League was held at the GrosvenorTiotel, Manchester on Tuesday, Mr. Joseph Platt (Oldham) occupying the chair. Keighley, F.C., in the match against Runcorn at Keighley on Saturdayglast, were fined thesum of Bs. for late start. It was resolved that referees, in reporting players, be requested to give particulars of charge against players, and that a copy of the referee's statement be sent to the players concerned. Furthermore, the latter will be permitted to either defend themselves i a writing or attend personally if they so desire. The committee , reserves the rightto call upon the referee and the player I or players concerned to attend if advisable. The following players were put on the registers of the clubs first named. Warrington.—J. McHugh (Altrincham), H. Hardman (Pendlebury),and R. Wood (local). Widnes.—R. Price, J. Gill, H. Hughes,and T. Jackson (local). St. Helens.—J. Johnson (Stockport), L. Davies (Leigh), and J. Halsall (local). Leigh.—C. Butler (Howe Bridge), S. Gregory (West Leigh), W. Mulroony (Tyldesley), and T. Mawdsley (Plank-lane). The following players were struck off the registers of the clubs named:— Warrington.—F'. Barber, J. Burton, W. Clare, A. Hudson, W. Cresswell,J. Finnigan, J. Oakes, J. Bate, and F. Jewkes. Runcdrn.—F. Darlington, G. Moore, F. Gayter, W. Gayter, J. Riley. G. Urmson, H. Myers, T. Parr, .T. Weston, Wilson, J. T. Groves, 0. Hughes and J. J. Matthews. Widnes.—F. Annels, J. Grayson and G. Waide.

REVISING THE LISTS. The Revising Barrister at Warrington. -- Mr. J. T. Foard commenced his annual revision of the voters'list for the Borough at the Town Hall on Tuesday morning. The business was very light and was disposed of speedily. The claimants numbered 214 and there were 93 objections. The number of voters on the new list is 11,598 an addition of 271 on last year's list. Mr. John Clayton represented the Liberal Association and Mr. William Holbrook, assisted by Mr. H. Hayes, the Conservative Association. Mr. Gough represented the Non-Trading Association. The officials present were: Mr. Stanley Wilson (deputy town clerk), Mr. W. H. Woodcock (assistant overseer). Mr. T. J. Garnett and Mr. F. H. Aukland (relieving officers.) THREE VOTES FOR THREE BROTHERS. SUCCESSFUL CLAIM BY COUNCILLOR PEACOCK AND HIS BROTHERS. Councillor Peacock claimed a vote in respect of a coal wharf and office situated near Dallam-lane, in Bewsey Ward. Questioned by Mr. Holbrook. he saidhe was the tenant of 62, West-street, and he also lived at New Brighton. Mr. Holbrook: This is a question of distance. His residence is beyond the distance. The Revising Barrister: Is he on the list for New Brighton? Councillor Peacock: No, sir. Mr. Holbrook stated that applicant owned 62, West- street, but there was a person already on the list for that place. Councillor Peacock said that was his sister. The ratable value of the coal wharf and office was given at £65, and the vote was allowed. Mr. Peter Peacock applied for a vote as tenant of 66, West-street. In answer to Mr. Holbrook, he said he , had resided at week-ends for the past three months at New Brighton. He formerly resided at Bewsey-road. Mr. Clayton: As a matter of fact you are here the week through ?—Applicant: Yes. You are also the owner of 66. West-street ?—Yos. Mr. Holbrook said applicant's brother, Mr. Frederick John Peacock, distinctly told him that he was the tenant of 66, West-street, and not Peter. Applicant replied that his brother was tenant up to the end of last year. The vote was allowed. George'Peacock, living at 64, West street, claimed a vote for one of two warehouses in West street. The ' placelwas owned by Messrs Peacock and Company, in which firm there were four partners. The rateable value was £4l. Mr. Holbrook said this should be a joint claim ; it looked ra therTcurious. Mr. Clayton pointed out that there were two distinct warehouses and stables and the applicant was applying for a vote in respect to one of them. The Revising Barrister allowed the vote. A CLOSE THING." George Bailey, Wilderspool-road, claimed a vote as partner in the firm of John Bailey and Sons. The ratable value of the premises was £3O, and in the firm were applicant's father, brother, and himself. The brother had a vote in respect of a house of which he was the tenant. :11r. Foard, in granting the vote, remarked that it was "rather a close thing." AN EVENING SITTING. Mr. Foard attended in the evening, at six o'clock, the sitting occupying about half an hour. THE DISQUALIFICATIONS OF RELIEF. THE QUESTION OF MEDICAL ASSISTANCE. Thomas Potter, whose address was given as near ' Moss-street, was objected to by the Liberals on the ground that he had had relief during the past twelve months. Mr. Garnett said the relief was in kind and was worth 4s. 11d. The vote was disallowed. George Chidlaw, a Conservative, claimed a vote in , respect of 261, Knutsford-road. Mr. Holbrook informed his Honour that medical re- lief had been given in this case. The applicant was ordered into the workhouse by the medical officer of the union. The Barrister: He got his board and lodging. Mr. Holbrook: Yes, but under the doctor's orders ; otherwise he would have been treated at home. The Barrister said he would have to inquire into this matter. It might be that the man was ordered into hospital merely on medical grounds. Mr. Holbrook: On medical grounds, because the applicant had not the convenience at home. Applicant: Dr. Braide told me he could not do any- thing with me at house; and said I would have to go to the hospital. I objected at first, but I went with him. The Barrister: \Vas it merely for the treatment or because the space was too small? Applicant: It was for the treatment. I was in the hospital eight or ten weeks. I was not suffering from an infectious disease, but from nervous debility. The Barrister said he would consider the matter, and he would give the man a vote if he could. A case of nervous disease stood on a different footing to other cases. Sometimes the necessary things could not be obtained in a house, such as milk, wine, &c., and there might not be sufficient air. Therefore, this case stood on a different footing to other cases. The further consideration of the case was adjourned until Wednesday morning when the Revising Barrister, after consulting authorities allowed the claim. A LODGER'S CLAIM. William Norman. Bewsey-road, claimed a lodger vote in respect of a sitting-room and bedroom which he said he occupied exclusively. He stated that he lived with his mother, who owned the house. He had his meals by himself in his own room, and part of the furniture was his. His Honour said he was not satisfied, and it seemed to him to be difficult to recognize the fact that other people did not use the sitting-room. The claim would be disallowed. VOTE OF THANKS. Mr. Clayton, Liberal agent., moved a vote of thanks to the Revising Barrister for the courtesy he had shown during the revision of the lists. The duties had been I comparatively light, and this was due to a great extent to the excellent list which had been prepared by Mr. Woodcock and his assistant. The percentage of objec- tions and claims had lessened each year. In conclusion Mr. Clayton referred to the fact that Mr. Dunbobbin, who had assisted him, had attended the revision court for 34 years, which was in itself a record. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Holbrook seconded and also alluded to the excel- lent manner in which the list had been compiled. It was free from clerical errors and printer's mistakes. The Revising Barrister acknowledged the compliment and said he was struck with the exceeding care with which the list had been prepared. It was a bulky volume con- taining over 11,500 names. It certainly reflected credit upon all the persons vrho had prepared it. He con- gratulated the Borough upon the care with which the whole of the work had been done. The work of the officials had reduced his duties to a minimum. This concluded the proceedings. .
